## Some Get-togethers with St Josemaría {#inedito.some-get-togethers-with-st-josemaria}

11 March 1956

Today, Don Alvaro’s birthday, our Father had two get-togethers with students of the Roman cn 1989, 1 College. Among other things, he referred to the radical newness of the pathway of Opus Dei in 371-378

the Church, to the need to be contemplative souls in the middle of the world, and to the way of

living temperance.

12 November 1956 (women) cn 1997, 2 251-259 A get-together our Father had in the Roman College of Holy Mary.

19 March 1957

cn 1987, On this feast of St Joseph, our Father recalled the freedom of the members of the Work in all 3 710-718 matters of opinion. The hope of heaven and prayer for the Church were other topics of the get-

together. The example of the philosopher’s stone and the fable of the proud toad.

4 October 1957 (women)

cn 1991,174- During an annual course in Villa Sachetti. Among other things, our Father encouraged his 4 180 daughters to be faithful to their vocation, not to be discouraged by weaknesses and he had them

consider the enormous proselytistic effectiveness of hidden work.

21 November 1958

Feast of the Presentation of Our Lady. Our Father referred to the sanctification of professional

work, which is thus converted into an instrument of apostolate. Commenting on the progress of

cn 1988, the construction works of Villa Tevere, he recalled the lack of means when they had started, and 5 143-151 he highlighted some points on poverty according to the spirit of the Work He asked for prayers

for the Conclave which was taking place those days. He related the filial tussle with the Holy See

so that they would approve the figure of the custodes. He recalled Tia Carmen’s final hours on

earth.

25 December 1958

In the Aula Magna of Villa Tevere, our Father encouraged his sons to persevere day after day in cn 1989, 6 interior life, overcoming possible difficulties. Example of the stakes painted red and the story 977-982

books. When necessary we have to play out a human comedy for the divine spectator. Legend of

the acrobat monk.

1 January 1959

cn 1987, After breakfast on the first day of that year, our Father was with his sons living in Villa Tevere. He 7 357-364 spoke of the need for priests of the Work. Towards the end he spoke about some ways of living

poverty.

14 February 1959 (women)

Meeting of our Father with his daughters in the Aula Magna of Villa Tevere. On this anniversary, cn 1992, 8 our Father described in detail fundamental points of the spirit of the Work, especially the 535-546

supernatural character of our vocation. He also encouraged his daughters to have true desires for

holiness.

1 & 14 February 1960 (women)

cn 1990, Meetings our Father had with his daughters of the Roman College of Holy Mary during the first 9 168-176 months of that year. Among other things, he encouraged them to support the apostolic work

which was to begin in new countries with their prayer and sacrifice.

14 February 1960 (men)

cn 1978, Need to do proselytism: auguri e figli maschi! It is a right and a duty, which never ceases. 10 270-277 Freedom of spirit, to give up from time to time a practice of piety which does not form part of the

plan of life.

Santiago de Compostela, 25 July 1961

Feast of James the Apostle. Those attending a summer semester were in three get-togethers with cn 1987, 11 our Father. The need to be very faithful to our vocation, proselytism and affection for others in the 140-153

Work were central topics of these get-togethers, in which our Founder encouraged prayer for the

special intention. Commentary on ut iumentum.

3 December 1961 cn 1986, 12 First Sunday of Advent. Our Father’s words were about fidelity to vocation, referring to the gospel 1187-1192 passage on the resurrection of Lazarus.

1 April 1962 cn 1988, 13 Our Father asked for prayers for the priests being ordained that year. Apostolic zeal and the help 465-468 which we should offer one another so as to be faithful, were other topics in this get-together.

2 July 1962

cn 1986, Today, our Father spoke to his sons in the Roman College (in Villa Tevere) about the 14 440-445 responsibility with which one receives formation in the Work, so as to transmit it to others.

Anecdote of the cook, of the village fool and the orchestra, and of the mill stones.

Paris, 8 September 1962 (women) cn 1996, 15 A get-together our Father had in Paris. 766-774

Saragossa, 15 September 1962 cn 1989, 27- 16 Passing through Saragossa, our Father had a get-together with his sons in Miraflores. He spoke to 32 them about prayer, dedication and proselytism.

Pamplona, 23 August 1963 (1)

cn 1999, In 1963, having been with his sons in Zurich and Paris, our Father arrived in Reparacea, a 17 376-383 Navarre country house in the Batzan Valley. On 23 August he had a get-together in the sitting-

room of Belagua.

Pamplona, 23 August 1963 (2) cn 1999, 18 After we sang several songs of the Work, our Father continued the get-together in Colegio Mayor 482-491 Belagua, where he spoke about several topics that we should always consider in our prayer.

19 March 1964

Feast of St Joseph. Our Father spoke in Villa Tevere about renewing one’s dedication and love for cn 1986, 19 freedom. He emphasised our voluntary subjection to God’s wishes and to the spirit of the Work, 758-768

and full freedom in temporal matters. The precept of charity. Examples of the donkey, the boat in

danger and the pearl.

24 October 1966

First get-together of the academic year with students of the Roman College following our Father’s cn 1990, 66- 20 trip to Spain. He makes reference to a new site which has been acquired for the new Roman 75 College. Several memories of the work of St Raphael and our Father’s discovery that the Holy

Mass is also Opus Dei.

9 July 1967

On the eve of the departure of some Roman College students returning to their regions, our Father cn 1989, 21 was in a get-together with them in the Arco dei Venti of Villa Tevere. He spoke of the need to get 1173-1183

to know and to practise the spirit of Opus Dei, of the juridical path which was then being

followed, of 14 February 1943, and of love and union with the Father, whoever he might be.

7 April 1968 (1)

Palm Sunday. Our Father spurred his sons on to be truly saints, and he asked them to help him be cn 1991, 22 good and faithful. Referring to an olive branch, he spoke about the Custom of sending one each 290-296 year to the Counsellor, as a sign of unity. He also expressed his wish that many of his sons would

dedicate themselves to theology.

7 April 1968 (2)

This year for the first time, students from all around the world attended the course in Rome in cn 1991, 23 Holy Week. In the Palm Sunday get-together he spoke a lot about proselytism, about apostolate 373-378 among artists and pluralism in matters of opinion. Our Father laid stress on the care of recent

vocations.

4 April 1969

In the soggiorno of the fifth floor of Uffici, our Father spoke about Holy Week, and, in particular,

cn 1990, about the institution of the Blessed Eucharist. It was the second year when the Easter course was 24 527-534 taking place in Rome, and there were many anecdotes of the apostolate. This allowed the Father

to highlight some ideas about the work of St Raphael: love for freedom, the need for formation,

the world of cinema.

10 April 1969 cn 1978, 25 In Easter week, after other groups had already returned home, this group had a get-together with 382-391 our Father. He spoke about Gaztelueta, about parents and teachers, and young people.

28 March 1971

cn Today, the 46th anniversary of the priestly ordination of our Father, he encouraged his sons to

26 1987,1130show gratitude towards the Lord. Love for the Holy Mass was the principal theme of the get-

1143 together. The Father also spoke about the freedom we have in matters of opinion, of

contradictions some years previously and the great love for the Church which we have to foster.

27 July 1971

cn 2004, This year our Founder spent some weeks in Caglio, close to Castello di Urio. On several 27 1080-1093 occasions he went to the conference centre to be with those of the Work who were doing an

annual course there.

18 October 1972 cn 1997, 35- 28 During his Catechesis throughout the Iberian peninsula, our Father was with some of the older 44 members of the Work in Diego de Leon.

3 December 1972 cn 1998, 65- 29 On his return from his catechesis throughout Spain and Portugal, our Father was in a get-together 77 in the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo with his sons living in Villa Tevere.

Sao Paulo, 29 May 1974

cn 1988, A get-together in the Centre of the Regional Commission of Brazil, where our Father recalled 30 255-261 stories of his childhood. Referring to the story of Maria the cook, he spoke about how he lived in

the presence of God throughout the week, specifying a particular devotion for each day.

1 11 March 1956

I remember that day especially because it was don Alvaro’s birthday. The date is also fixed in my mind because of one of those unimportant episodes that remain in one’s memory: snow was falling in Rome. Also in the get-together with our Father there were special references that make it easier to remember when certain events happened. Around that time our Father announced that the Vicolo degli Archi would be finished quite soon – at last! – and that the beginning of the course in journalism in the Estudio General de Navarra (which was how the University began) would also start very soon. He asked us to pray for the site of the new Roman College, and he also talked about his wish to give a great push to the apostolate of public opinion everywhere. You must also pray that those dreams of mine for so many years – to make a great network of Christian publications – stop being just dreams. If you are faithful and humble, we will cover the whole world. How many things which were then only dreams have now come true! Remembering this today, I cannot but recall those frequent exclamations of our Founder: prima, piu, meglio, or dream and your dreams will fall short.

Opening the way It was snowing then, that 11th of March. Just as we hoped, the Father came down with don Alvaro, whom we had the chance to greet. Our Founder commented that in the Work we celebrate don Alvaro’s saint’s day and also his birthday. It is he – he added – who was the first Procurator General, the first Secretary General, the first priest to come to Italy, the first Counsellor of the Region of Italy, the first Rector of the Roman College of the Holy Cross … That same day, as the Father told us in the get-together, don Alvaro was going to visit a cardinal who did not know the work Opus Dei was doing in the service of the Church. Our Founder asked us to keep that interview in mind. He went on to speak about the radical novelty of Opus Dei in the Church. Among other things he said: We are opening the way, little by little, with effort and with a lot of grace from God and it is noticeable. If only I were to tell you about the difficulties of the first years! It was like a cloudburst: misunderstanding, calumnies, persecutions… While at the same time some even wanted to imitate us! Norms have to come from life itself. When there is no life or when there is a lack of juridical sense, there happens what happened to the poor ignorant man who, when he went to steal the princess’s jewels, seeing the shiny crystals he left them, thinking they were just glass, and took the coloured pieces of glass he saw instead. When there is supernatural and apostolic life, the juridical norms are like a suit made to measure. However, some don’t take this fact into account: it is as if they take clothes made to someone else’s measure, and insist on putting a man inside who has very different physical characteristics…. Then when they see the suit doesn’t fit, they throw it away. He recalled the difficulties and misunderstandings that the legal path of Opus Dei had encountered, and concluded by saying that we should love all those who work for Jesus Christ, making allowances and forgiving everyone. Only after some time, when knowing that chapter of the history of the Work better, have I realised the suffering behind those words of our Father, because he always behaved with the sense of a good family man who does not allow his little children to notice that he is worried. With God’s grace – he continued – we have always cheerfully left behind in tatters our youth, our health and life itself, always joyfully. But that is not enough: you need to have a big heart. Love for souls helps us understand everyone, making allowances for them and forgiving them. You need to have a marvellous charity: veritatem facientes in caritate, as I have asked to be etched on a window on the stairs: we must be intransigent with error, and each one of us with himself, and understanding with others, helping them on their path towards God.

Out in the street Our Father then went on to remind us about a fundamental aspect of our spirit: the need to be contemplative souls. Our prayer is not simply a question of recollecting for a few minutes, or a few hours… It’s for every moment of the day! Our entire life has to be turned into prayer! Our way is to be contemplatives. My very dear children, con-tem-pla-tives! If you are truly such, you will be happy. He said that emphasising every syllable, so that it would really sink in. Then he continued: I’ve often told you that the highest prayer God ever granted me was in the street, in a tram, in the metro… and at Mass. My dearest children: contemplatives! If you are, even your sleep will be prayer... Now, as I recall this scene, I remember the lovable figure of our Founder, who always urged us to find the greatest union with our Lord in our everyday tasks, and I can still hear even his very voice, when he taught us – in a very clear way and with examples – to make that encounter with God and with the Mother of God a reality throughout the day. Everything that bothers you, the thoughts that sometimes come to your head – because I am, because I have earned it… – all those unreasonable reasons are due to our not having given ourselves truly, and because we are not really and truly contemplative souls. In our life, my children, everything has to be prayer, contemplation, union with God. Anyone who strives to live like this will be all right, and will be holy. I measure the effectiveness of the corporate apostolates by the degree of holiness attained by those working in them; and I don’t want any apostolic activity if the people working in them don’t improve. It’s of no interest to me! All those things pass, and what we are seeking is permanent. Prayer, my children, constant prayer; this enables divine conversations to take place in our life, and, by divinising us, they also make us more human.

The Father raises his voice The following moments of the get-together cannot be easily forgotten either. Our Father was referring to the construction works of Villa Tevere, which all of us, in one way or another had helped to supervise. I recall that sometimes, because of the busy timetables and the many jobs we had to do, it seemed difficult

– or so we thought – to look after the material tasks perfectly. Our Father did not allow anything to be neglected, because taking great care of the little things is very much in accord with the spirit God entrusted to him. That is why on one of those days he had corrected us forcefully. He reminded us of it in the get-together. Don’t be afraid when I raise my voice. You are all very clever; but at times you are ‘a bit thick’ and do not understand that the tradesmen cannot be allowed to trample on the carpet. He brought to our attention very vividly the time when he walked from one place to another through the streets of Madrid, because he did not have a cent to take the tram, wearing shoes whose soles were worn through, discarded by the residents of Ferraz. And he concluded: And now I see that these sons of mine behave like little “lords”! I like to see my sons behave like gentlemen, and carry out all the things of the earth – the highest and the most humble – as when your brothers used to come with me to the hospitals of Madrid to wash the wounds of those who were ill… with good manners, yes! but not like “little lords”. You will be able to do everything in a divine way – even the most material things. He added: Your quick understanding, because you are all very clever, should be shown in the orderliness with which you do things, which is a refined way of living poverty. That very afternoon, in another get-together, our Father made the same correction a little more gently, telling us not to take it badly because at times he was demanding, or because of the way he told us things. In having to correct he himself suffered as much, if not more. If I shout at you, it is because I have to do so. Although Manolo loves his horses – he said, referring to a son of his who is in the army – sometimes he beats them with a whip. Then jokingly he added that the comparison was not fair because we were not horses, but… donkeys.

The value of temperance To go back to the morning get-together. Our Father continued to talk about mortification and penance, precisely because we are present, present as apostles, in the midst of society: Our vocation is in the middle of the world, and we have the duty to make our Lord present, cheerfully and very naturally, wherever we are. So when one of my sons has a more intense social life, he tries not to lose the habit of mortification. If necessary, he pointed out very graphically, one puts on the cilice much tighter. To ensure that his words were fixed in our minds, he used very concrete details: When someone of the Work goes to a meeting, he must not forget that he must take there the good aroma of Christ. A glass of whisky, a cigar? No, thank you. If the others smoke three cigarettes, you smoke one. And addressing each one in particular, he added: You are salt, light, leaven, and you cannot but take this spirit to the farthest corner of society. They have seen you: what you do, what you don’t do; that you struggle, and that you mortify yourself. They don’t notice this the first day, but in the end it gets through. And this, which seems so trivial, carries with it the bonus odor Christi; it draws others along. With the power of this virtue, which is a cardinal virtue – from cardo, hinge, pivot – all your friends are influenced without realising it, They think: that sober person who knows how to smile and is one of us like a fish in water, he carries with him some sort of – I don’t know what – light, fire… Then, when the time is right and they have some problem, they will come to you in confidence as a friend to ask your advice. Without your looking for it, you end up carrying out real spiritual direction with people who do not know what spiritual direction is and perhaps might not want it. This presence of ours even in the remotest corner of society, just like the apostolate of public opinion, is a visible reality today in many parts of the world, and yet it once seemed just an apostolic dream: a dream which, nevertheless, sounded in the words of our Founder like a sure announcement of solid and tangible realities. What followed at the end of the get-together is, again, not easy to forget, this time because we heard now from the lips of our Father what we had often heard in one form or another. He said: Believe me, that if we do not fight to please God, even with our weaknesses, our life has no meaning. Holiness does not consist in not having weaknesses, nor in not falling… It consists in fidelity to our vocation. It often happens that our effectiveness is lost through the tongue. That is why it is better to act than to speak. With the ease with which our Father went from the most divine to the most human, he finished the get-together asking us not to put on weight, and to do some sport or exercise. I do not remember if it was still snowing when we ended, or when we met our Father again. He came downstairs once again that afternoon, with that special look he had when he had fallen again into the “proximate temptation” which meant being with his sons for a while. I find it difficult not to come down, because I love you with all my heart. But I will try to come less often. He said this because he did not want to make changes to the timetable of the Roman College. From this second get-together I have in my notes his advice to do our internal studies always according to the Tradition of the Church and following the thought of St Thomas. He then read us some words he had written in 1949 in a copy of The Way, dedicated to don Alvaro: For my son, Alvaro, who to serve God has often had to fight many bulls. These words were verified a little later that same day when don Alvaro returned from the visit our Father had referred to that morning. Our Founder told us that it was noticeable that we had prayed, because that cardinal was enthusiastic about the Work and had asked for the Work to begin in his diocese as soon as possible. It was with these remarks that those moments with our Father came to an end on that memorable 11th of March 1956, don Alvaro’s birthday.

12 November 1956

I wanted to bring you to Rome so you can dedicate some time to improving your formation. When you went to begin Opus Dei in other regions, the Work was still very small, and it was necessary to start swimming. That is how our Father explained to some sisters of ours the reason for their joining the Roman College of Holy Mary, having been called to Rome after working for years in many Regions. The Father then pointed out other circumstances that had led him to act in this way and that showed his very human, fatherly and motherly heart: You will be able to rest a little; and also… I knew you would be delighted to see one another again. This happened in the weeks before the 1956-57 academic year. The Numeraries who were going to the Roman College for their course began to arrive before summer. Some had helped in the very beginning of the apostolates of the Work in countries such as Venezuela, Argentina…; others were the first vocations of their Region: Germany, Ecuador, Chile… When we arrived in Rome we joined those working in Villa Tevere, ironing or in the servery; and of course in the cleaning, for in a house where construction work is going on this requires a lot of attention and energy. At that time, the area of the oratories of Villa Tevere was under construction – the oratory of the Most Holy Trinity, that of Pentecost, the Main Sacristy, and the room for the Chalices…, the oratory that the Directors of the Central Advisory would use, and the area of Uffici, which would form part of the Residence.

Special Days We were very aware of the financial difficulties, because the Father would often send us messages to keep in mind with a lot of peace of mind don Alvaro’s outings to ask for money. We were convinced that Our Lord would not “let us down”. The diary of La Montagnola, dated 3rd of September, reads: The works have not stopped! The noise the builders are making can still be heard. We have given many thanks to God. These days the Father is telling us to keep in mind don Alvaro’s visits. A few days later it reads: Today he said: This would be the first time for prayer not to produce results. I assure you that we – all of us together – have not asked for anything without the Lord giving us everything. It wasn’t only a financial problem; some neighbours complained that the views from the future building of Uffici would block their view of the existing garden. This setback led to a pause in the work until, with everyone’s prayer and don Alvaro’s ability, the obstacles were overcome. During those days a number of us helped with the upholstery, and I’m very grateful to God for this because it meant that I could attend some get-togethers with our Father which otherwise would not have been “officially” possible. There were a lot of changes going on at that time. A number of people passed through Rome and stayed for a longer or shorter time, before going to a country different from their own: Chile, Venezuela, Portugal, Ireland… Towards the middle of October the second Ordinary Congress of the Women’s Section began. To have the electors with us was a wonderful injection of proselytistic fervour. There were many gettogethers where we heard apostolic news, and the names of those they hoped would soon discover their vocation. During the days they were in Rome we shared with them the joy of praying at St Peter’s, the centre of Christianity; of listening to our Father, perhaps after a long time; of meeting up with familiar faces, with whom we had shared the joys and difficulties of the apostolic development in one place or another, and of getting to know Villa Sacchetti. We all felt very grateful to have the opportunity to spend some time, or perhaps only a few days, in those buildings which we love so much, physically close to our Father, and with the people of the Work from different Regions. And how forcefully the Father spoke, on so many occasions, saying that we should make good use of our stay in Rome, whether for a short or a longer time, so as to leave, when the moment came, with our hearts more aflame with love for the Church and the Work, better prepared to serve all souls in any part of the world.

The Best Prologue As we left the summer months behind, more students of the Roman College arrived. With great enthusiasm we took up the distribution of jobs in the administration and at the printing press, where we would work until classes began. The poor knowledge of Spanish, of some who had arrived recently, provided amusing moments. Finally the 12th of November arrived. During the midday get-together the Director announced the news we had been waiting for, which also brought with it a marvellous gift: –Classes begin tomorrow…; the Father will come to see us this afternoon. We were very happy but remembered that we were not all there yet. The Director said the Father knew that, and that he was really sorry, but we needed to begin. We started the prayer at four o’clock in the afternoon in the Oratory of El Santo Cristo, which is the one we would normally use. Afterwards we went up quickly to the Soggiorno of La Montagnola. Our Father had already arrived with don Alvaro, and while we were sitting down he waited in the salotto. Pax!, my daughters, may God bless you! he said when he was coming in; and immediately afterwards he commented that he would try to speak slowly, so that everyone would understand, including the ones who did not speak Spanish. Then he asked: This year, how many countries are the students in the Roman College of Holy Mary from? On being told that it was fourteen, our Founder added that they would soon come from many other countries. This comment aroused in me heartfelt thanksgiving for the extension of the Work, realising the universal dimension of Opus Dei which our Father had foreseen from the beginning was becoming a reality.

Ut eatis! The Father wanted the Roman College to have its own centre, and he explained how it would be. This plan became a reality later on, for in 1959 our Father decided that the buildings of Villa delle Rose would house the new Roman College of Holy Mary. The works began in April 1960. Villa Tevere had not been finished then and, as already mentioned, the financial problems were very great. The works would go on for two and a half years. Referring to that instrument of formation, he concluded: We shall soon have it, but for that I count on your prayers and your sacrifice. We need it. Among other reasons, there is one very basic one: already you do not fit in Villa Sacchetti. He then started to talk immediately about the reason for our own stay in the Roman College. There is no human reason why you are here. It does not make sense to look for a human explanation. When I want to explain the reason for your being here in this house, I have to turn to our Lord, and hear his voice again: the same words he said to the first twelve, he has said them also to you (…): Ego elegi vos et posui vos, ut eatis et fructum afferatis. I cannot forget the strength, the faith and the hope-filled optimism he inspired when he commented on this text of the Gospel of St John: I have put you here so that you will go far, with a desire to yield fruit, with a hunger for souls: ut eatis… So that you go out capable of doing something, so that you yield apostolic fruit. So that you are formed, so that you are prepared to struggle in this Roman College of Holy Mary. In several countries, the Work was taking its first steps; in others the women had not established themselves yet; and in all of them the apostolic work was going through a stage of expansion, so that the presence in Rome of a good group of Numeraries – nearly thirty – represented a notable effort for these regions. That is why the Father, as if speaking to himself, made the following considerations: That you have come means that on the part of Our Lord there is a love of predilection, a second calling.

That is why He has placed you here, in the heart of the Work, in a firm and stable way. He gave you – some long ago, others not so long – the great, immense, marvellous grace of your vocation, and to this he has now added your being chosen to form part of the Roman College of Holy Mary, when Opus Dei is practically in its beginnings. For a moral entity twenty six years are practically nothing. Why has Jesus Our Lord brought you here to the heart of this supernatural family we form in Opus Dei? It is certainly not to do nothing; you have come for a very clear purpose: to strengthen your vocation, to become strong in your love for the Work, so that afterwards you can go far away, with a desire for the harvest, with hunger for souls. We were a number of people there from different countries and, as our Father had just told us, in a few years’ time people would come from other countries where the seed of the Work had not yet been sown. Listening to him, who would not have been enthused at playing a direct part in such a great adventure? Perhaps because of this our Founder went on to say: But bear in mind that the commandment to go forth – ut eatis – does not mean to run around the world from pole to pole, but to be effective there where our Directors place us. The Father continued, asking us to consider another scene from the Gospel, very rich in its teaching: Do you remember the passage of the Gospel about the barren fig tree? It had leaves, it appeared to fulfil its duty, but it didn’t. You, my daughters, have to go from here with a hunger to work, with a desire to produce abundant supernatural fruit. How will you be effective? Overcoming obstacles, removing any difficulties you come across – sometimes it will be the barrier of habits, of nationality, of languages …– , overcoming all this so as to live the unity of this great family which is our Beautiful Mother.

Without cutting corners On addressing those who had come from Chicago a few days before, our Father was more specific: You will go to a number of countries outside the United States, for example, to Australia. But the expansion will be possible, if you are faithful, if you are pious, if you are saints. One of them, who was making an effort to understand everything our Father said, without doubting for a moment, exclaimed: Yes, Father, we will go in two months’ time… The Father smiled, amused by her impulsive reaction, and made us see that the urgency to do what God is asking for has to count on the passage of time. It takes two months for onions to ripen; souls require longer… In this pleasant atmosphere, where laughter mixed with serious topics, our Father urged us to be demanding in our struggle, but completely at peace, because it is not based on our own strength, but on the grace of God. If you have come it is because the Lord has brought you, and He will give you his grace to be faithful to vocation, to prepare you for the battles we will all have to fight – whether interiorly or from external opposition: whatever Our Lord allows – and also through the formation you will receive in the Roman College of Holy Mary. And he stopped in order to show us the way to achieve those good dispositions: You have to be very sincere. You must open your heart wide in spiritual direction, without hiding anything. Look! When we go to a doctor, we tell him all our symptoms, without hiding any. If anyone were to act differently – hiding the reality of their situation or mentioning symptoms they did not have, we would think that they lacked common sense, that they did not really want to get better, isn’t that so? Yes Father! Of course! If we want to recover our health, we have to tell the doctor all our illnesses, without being ashamed. Daughters of my heart, in the spiritual life nobody is completely healthy, because she would be perfect, and as long as we are on earth we are on the way to holiness, to perfection. When we reach the maximum of our perfection, the Lord calls us to Himself. In an amusing way, he explained that we should not be afraid of our weaknesses or our failings. If he himself, he said, who has spent many years struggling to be a saint, finds so many miseries in his life, why should we be surprised that we have them too? So speak, and be transparent, so that you are helped to struggle and become saints! 9

Our Founder asked us to tell him something, and for a moment there was silence: we all preferred to listen to him… In the end, someone asked a question; and we all felt encouraged to join in. Our Father listened and addressed each one, making comments that showed his knowledge of the circumstances of each country. It was noticeable that he was happy with the work done by his daughters in the different Regions; how many things would become a reality if we really fought to be saints! Our Founder, who had been greeting us as soon as we had arrived in Rome, wanted to know how well some of us could speak Spanish, and whether we found it difficult to adapt to the food or the climate… I can’t remember who asked the Father when he would go to America. I then heard for the first time the answer that over the years I heard many times: the Father does not have personal plans, for that would mean doing what he wants; the Father always seeks only to do God’s Will. He also talked about the classes we would receive. Although, to start with, we would not have much time to study (there was plenty of work to do) he wanted us to have a number of subjects explained in depth, and he asked us to make good use of those hours, trying to retain the knowledge of what was explained to us. This was important, the Father said, but it was even more important that we should identify ourselves fully with the spirit of the Work, that we should fulfil the Norms very well, that we should seriously endeavour to be contemplative souls; and that we should love and take care of the unity of the Work. We wanted the get-together to go on, but it had to finish. Before he left, I was particularly impressed by the gestures with which he accompanied his words while he described how the Divine Sower would hold us firmly in his Wounded Hand, would soak us with his Blood and then send us all over the world just as the corn is sown. Some grains, he added, would fall nearby; others would go far, taking the seed of the Work to this or that part of the world. Our Father got up, and with fatherly concern, reminded us that we had not yet had our merienda. He then wanted to show those who had come recently the oratory of the Central Advisory and the room where the chalices were kept. He explained that we always give Our Lord the best we have, and that was why our way of practising poverty in worship was, for us, to obtain good objects and to have only those needed. He finished by saying that, as with the rest of the things in Villa Tevere, we would find everything done with great love of God and very clean. The moments we spent with our Father were a precious prologue to the year 1956-57 in the Roman College, and the first of a number of moving occasions that we spent close to our Founder, which always seemed very few. The classes started the following day; we gave thanks to God for so many means used by the Work for us to receive the necessary formation, overcoming all types of logistic difficulties. Because there was no other place, the dining room became a classroom. Every day for a number of hours in that part of the house we worked in total silence. Later, life became normal again. But as always happens, the memory of that course in the Roman College is an inestimable treasure, in which we were also able to live wonderful moments with Aunt Carmen, who sometimes ate with us or joined us for a get-together. As time passed, we began to disperse. Most of us went back to our country of origin; others, a few, had the good fortune to remain in Rome to work, and others received with joy the news that they would go to start or to reinforce the apostolic work in different countries: Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, France… All of them specially strengthened by the prayer and sacrifice of Aunt Carmen, who at that time was spending her last days here on earth.

19 March 1957

The last Sunday of St Joseph was 17th March. The countdown to the feast of our Father and Lord was coming to an end. Spring, on the other hand, had come a few days earlier and it was a lovely day. Encouraged by the good weather, a group of students of the Roman College had their get-togethers out of doors, in the recently finished Cortile dei Leoni. Someone read a letter that had come from Paris, with news of the apostolic work in France. As in other years, these days just before the feast of St Joseph, many letters had arrived from all over the world, filled with news about the apostolic work and names of people to keep in mind. In Monday’s lunchtime get-together we read a letter from Venezuela. The people from the United States had a gettogether with our Founder in the garden of Villa Vecchia. The reason was that Manolo had come from New York and was passing through Rome. That Monday the afternoon prayer was brought forward and the ceremony of the big List began immediately after. After singing the Veni Creator, the Rector of the Roman College opened the envelope with the names of the people who had been commended especially to St Joseph the year before, and read them out one by one. This was often interrupted by a loud “he has whistled” or “whistled”, followed by applause reflecting our joy and gratitude to our Lord for each vocation. When the whole list had been read, the various groups dispersed throughout the house to write the new List: the soggiorno of what is now the Casa del Vicolo, the Cavalcavia della Madonna, the vestibule of the terrazzino, the study on the sixth floor, and the secretary’s room. For the first time there were names from Uruguay and some Hungarians who were in touch in Germany. Then came the 19th, feast of St Joseph. “Since waking up this morning”, the diary reads, “I’m sure we have recited many times the Oremus pro Patre. Then, in the Mass, after renewing our dedication, we offered a gift, the most pleasing one of all: the gift of our fidelity.” As we left the oratory, there were greetings all round: there are many who are called Joseph, starting with don José Luis. If there are some days when we may see the Father, today we can be sure we will. And indeed, the Rector told us that he was going to come down, and we all waited for him in the Galleria della Campana. As he entered, he greeted the Josephs first, and then all of us greeted him. As he made his way, he had affectionate words for each of us, one by one. When he reached the soggiorno, we crowded around him and between one song and another he spoke to us about many things. Our Founder touched on many topics. He began by commenting on a newspaper report that mentioned the name of a son of his who had been appointed a government minister in his country. He reminded us that what is really important in the Work is not the kind of profession or job we have (provided it is honest) but the way in which it is carried out: sanctifying it, sanctifying oneself, and sanctifying others. He stressed even more what we already knew, that all licit professions have the same dignity, because they are all an occasion for meeting the Lord. He also underlined the total freedom of his sons in temporal matters, within the wide margins allowed for by the Church’s Magisterium. Referring to the news item that gave exaggerated importance to our brother’s appointment because he was a member of the Work, he explained: Have you ever seen anything in such poor taste? They’ve only just realised that there are people of Opus Dei in public office! I don’t care: it is as if he had been made a member of the municipal council of … and he gave the name of a village on the outskirts of Rome, whose name I don’t now recall. All that I want is for you to be saints. In the get-togethers, our Founder left evidence on many occasions of his veneration for all the apostolic ways approved by the Church. He always referred to people with great charity, even when he was referring to someone who did not understand the Work. This understanding for people did not mean that he would not speak firmly and clearly to make sure that we understood properly the fundamental characteristics of the spirit God had given him. Where our spirit met the greatest opposition to being understood – he said on this occasion – was in the sacristies: not with priests but with some lay people who “use” the Church, with Catholics who make a living from calling themselves Catholic. It is difficult for such people to understand that Opus Dei, by its very nature, cannot be a party: not in politics, nor socially, nor in the scientific field, nor in anything else. Blessed be our freedom! You have to love freedom a great deal. When in doubt, within what the Church has left open to discussion, always place yourself on the side of freedom: you will be much safer that way. At this point, his tone of voice became even stronger: as if to make it very clear that (although thank God it had not happened nor ever would) in the Work there is no room for dictators. I abhor dictators, even though they may say the rosary every day. I cannot tolerate any dictator, unless for a short time, in the same way one has to bear a cast when one has a fractured arm. Although in the Work there can only be freedom in political matters, I can understand other institutions which, in addition to a religious goal, may also have a political one. As long as they respect other institutions, they have a right to do so. But it will never be like that in Opus Dei. It is contrary to its spiritual nature: something that God has made me see with no need of miracles. Long live freedom! Always! Some do not understand our blessed personal freedom, because there are ways of thinking that make them unable to: others do not understand because they cannot conceive that people can be truly free; and there are also people who do not want to understand, because in that way they can criticise us. But many, the vast majority of those who know us, understand and love it that way. If ever someone in the Work wished to impose political views, you would rebel, and you would be right: you would have my spirit. Not only would you not offend God, but on the contrary, you would please Him instead. Freedom of opinion? All you want. When it comes to work, there is absolute freedom of opinion. He recalled how this blessed freedom of ours does not diminish our freedom. What’s more, precisely because we are free we take our dedication more seriously. And so we all have absolute freedom of opinion: regarding our professional lives, we Numeraries are limited, because we have freely wanted it that way. That is why, if we are needed in some place, we leave behind a most brilliant professional situation to fulfil our duty in the place where our Directors ask us to go. To underline this point, our Father gave some examples of this very free dedication, which has given such fruit. If a son of mine, a chemist, has a test-tube in his hand and knows that by adding only a couple of drops he will discover the philosopher’s stone, and at that precise moment he is needed to look after the reception area in the furthest house of the Work, he goes there immediately, and spends the rest of his life there if needs be. And he does not discover the philosopher’s stone, because he has discovered the philosopher’s stone of the love of God, because he has discovered holiness. Faithfulness! That is our philosopher’s stone. The Father went on to point out: The supernumeraries, although they have the same vocation of total self-giving as the other members of the Work, are limited by the demands of their state with regard to their availability to look after the apostolate: they enjoy full freedom of thought and action, like the others. And of course, the Cooperators too. At this point in the get-together our Father changed the topic. And as on so many other occasions, he once more he opened his soul to us, in confidence as a father. Ask our Lord often that I may be good and faithful. Last night I did not want to go to bed without praying to St Joseph some words that I recited as a child, a child’s prayer … In God’s presence, always children! In this way we will be tough men and strong. I am going to tell you something. Last night, on the news, a little old man with a good natured face appeared, and he said, quite simply, that he was satisfied with the thirty years he had spent on his scientific work… I looked at myself and said: Thirty years! I was pained, saddened, to consider that, after thirty years, I am still beginning and I have done nothing. That man was so happy because he had done this and that… I, on the other hand, thirty years of work and I have scarcely known how to begin! Pray for me, that I may be able to put the last stone in place, as I find myself still on the first. I always feel serene, but last night I felt very ashamed. Thirty years! It is almost a man’s whole life, because while we are still children, we don’t appreciate things – children are a little crazy – and when old age arrives, it is as if we go back to our childhood. For an institution like the Work thirty years are few, but in this time the Lord has wished our Father to see so many fruits, although in his great humility he saw himself as a useless instrument in God’s hands. Nevertheless, this humility did not prevent him from recognising, as he has always taught us, the marvels that our Father God has worked through Opus Dei, which will extend throughout the world while there are human beings on earth. This is why our Founder immediately placed before our eyes the immense panorama which awaits us if we are faithful. Faithful, yes! It is worthwhile! Perhaps sometimes you will experience laziness; but, if you are sincere, you will overcome everything: the cravings of the flesh, which naturally the flesh demands: the ravings of pride – which I fear more than those of the flesh – the deceits of vanity etc. If we are sincere, what Love awaits us in Heaven! Without any treachery or deceits … All the love, all the beauty, all the greatness, all knowledge… without making us ill: it will satisfy us without satiating us. I cannot imagine for myself what heaven will be like. I have such a poor imagination! But it will be a marvel. Father, you will say to me, Isn’t it a little selfish to think of heaven? No, my sons: it is the virtue of hope in action. And hope requires a strong faith. And faith and hope together demand a pure and noble love for the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit. Here we are again with the three theological virtues. This is the spiritual process you go through each day without realising it, just as you hardly realise you are breathing, if you have captured the spirit of the Work well. In this way you will you will enjoy the goods of the earth like the temperate, well-mannered people you are. When he spoke of temperance and mortification, there was nothing our Father said that suggested excessive asceticism. On the contrary, he knew how to combine the human and divine so well, that he went quite naturally from the ascetical to a fatherly piece of advice, as when he added: But if, today, because it is a feast, they serve you a better meal, enjoy it with acts of thanksgiving, for it will make our Lord very happy. He gave us an example of the affection his friends in Bethany felt for Jesus: Jesus sometimes went to the house of Lazarus and of those two holy women, Martha and Mary, who loved him with the same Love, but shown in different ways. Lazarus would spend some time in conversation with our Lord – he made the Confidence – and then the three of them would offer Jesus a meal, the best they had, and he would accept it gratefully, because their hospitality was another proof of their love. Now, as on other occasions, he spoke of the development of the apostolate in different parts of the world. The feast of St Joseph brought apostolic zeal to the fore in a particularly lively way. There is good news from everywhere. There will always be small setbacks, but they are overcome with charity and good humour. They are overcome by passing over the difficulties, and sometimes passing under them. Proud people always want to go above the obstacles. I advise you that, when necessary, you go under them, or avoid them if that is possible or easier… in any case, the obstacles which some have placed in our way, during these thirty years, have disappeared. And so that this idea would be fixed in our minds, he gave an example: a story which had been related to him, many years ago, by a friend who was a bishop. A very important man had died, and the bishop who told me this story had to preside over the funeral. It happened in Spain around 1910, during the time of the “Padlock Law” (Ley del candado) during the reign of Alphonse XIII, when the President of the Council of Ministers was [José] Canalejas. The “Padlock Law” was a law presented to Parliament by the Government which forbade the establishment of new religious Congregations. During the funeral, Canalejas had sat down to the right of the bishop, without even looking at him. And the Bishop started talking with the man sitting on the other side, but so loud as to be heard by the President of the Government. “Have you heard the fable of the proud toad? You haven’t? Well, there was once a toad who lived by the side of the royal highway, and he saw a splendid golden carriage coming. He thought: If I put myself in front of that carriage, it will not be able to pass. He put himself in the middle of the road, and puffed and puffed to make himself bigger. As the carriage came closer he puffed even more, as much as he could. The carriage got to where the toad was, and he huffed and puffed even more, and splat! He burst… Our Founder drew the conclusion. The carriage of the Church went ahead. And now I repeat it for you, although it is on a different level. If, when the years go by, you find misunderstandings along your way, don’t offend those who bring them up: very often they are good people, perhaps better than you, but the Lord has not given them the grace to understand our way. Treat them with charity, without hurting anyone, without crushing anyone: they are God’s creatures. Ubi caritas et amor, Deus ibi est. Don’t you see what we practise in our Centres? While one loves his own country with all his heart, he makes allowances for others and understands them and knows that everyone makes mistakes, because errare humanum est. How beautiful that you love one another and that you spread Christ’s charity throughout the world! How beautiful! May God bless you! On this earth there should be no barriers, races, or tongues, just children of God. Our Father finished speaking. We sang some songs and Giorgio did a humorous sketch of a car accident. In the middle of it don Alvaro came back. He had been making a phone call, and he left the room with our Founder. The get-together was over. Once more we had had the joy and the responsibility of collecting gold coins which, just as in the wedding of a great king, our Father had been giving out with both hands. Our heads and our hearts were excited by the lessons of love for freedom, and of charity and affection for everyone. And when renewing our dedication on such a special day as the feast of St Joseph, we also renewed the determination to safeguard this marvellous spirit which the Lord has wanted to give us, by making use of our Founder as a most faithful instrument.

4 October 1957

Our Father used to say to us that Opus Dei had been born Roman, and wanted some of his daughters (though not possible for all) to spend at least some time in the Eternal City, so that they would hold in high esteem the place where the Prince of the Apostles lived. Towards the end of 1957 I had the good fortune to attend an annual course in Villa Sacchetti. The buildings of the Montagnola had been finished and that is how a large group of Numeraries came together from different Regions: Colombia, England, Ireland, Portugal, United States, Spain, Germany. We had all previously met Aunt Carmen, who had left us for Heaven only two months before. As well as hearing directly many details of her last moments on earth, we had the chance to go down to the sottocripta of the oratory of Our Lady of Peace, now the prelatic Church, where her remains now rest. Activities were to begin on 16th September; but by the 14th, feast of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross, we were all in Rome, and we went to pray at St Peter’s. Our Father had been away for some weeks, on a journey through a number of European countries – Switzerland, Germany, Belgium, Holland, Luxembourg… – and he had sent his daughters in Rome some postcards which were received with loud cries of joy. One of them, which had been sent from the French frontier, was especially moving for our first French sister. She was living in Rome, for the definitive establishment of women of the Work in France would begin soon. We waited impatiently for the Father’s return; his absence was noticeable in the general atmosphere and, what is more, we could see the days of the course passing by and we imagined what we were missing… In fact after he had come back, we had the chance to listen to him daily, in talks or get-togethers. I will now refer to the get-together of the 4th of October, towards the end of our stay in Rome. We had the vigil to the Blessed Sacrament the night before, as on all the first Fridays of the month. I dare say that the intense, insistent prayer of all of us who were attending that annual course was reduced to one point: Lord, I ask you for whatever the Father is asking you for. On the morning of the 4th, we gathered in the soggiorno of the Montagnola at half past eleven. We heard the voice of the Father, as he was coming up the stairs between the first floor – where, in the Sala de Calices, there is a communication door with Villa Vecchia – and the second, where the sitting room is located. He came in with his characteristic step, determined and yet controlled, accompanied by don Alvaro. He immediately went to one of the armchairs, one with a rose-coloured silk tapestry, which was placed next to the small table in the corner. Don Alvaro sat next to him, in another armchair. During the annual course, we had been taught many things. Undoubtedly, though it was a short stay in the heart of the Work, it had left an impression on the soul, a deep and lasting mark. The Father covered different aspects of the spirit of the Work in talks, in meditations, classes and, of course, in our personal conversation with the Lord. That autumn morning, the Father began the get-together referring to that fact: We have talked about personal holiness, about unity, about charity; fundamentally, about self-giving and perseverance. I have told you, my daughters, that whatever might happen in the lives of each one of you

– whether a difficulty in the interior life or external setbacks – it will have no importance if you relate it naturally and supernaturally to your vocation, which is like a shield, like steel armour that protects you from top to bottom, and prevents even the smallest harmful germ from entering your life as souls dedicated to God. We were listening attentively. Gathered there together were people of various nationalities, ages and circumstances, but what united us was much stronger than any differences: the spirit of the Work, and an irrepressible desire to spread Opus Dei across all the continents. Our Father not only saw that group of daughters in front of him: he was thinking of the thousands of hearts that with the passage of centuries would come to Opus Dei, to make all the paths of the earth divine. And this urged him on to form us, and to sharpen our sense of responsibility. 15

His way of expressing himself was particularly clear, even strong, full of affection, and not at all hurtful. It produced in the soul a healthy reaction to reject any idea of lukewarm self-giving. The awareness of your weakness cannot lead you to react as follows: I am so much a donkey that it must mean I don’t have a vocation. No! My daughters! Your reaction has to be precisely the opposite: I have behaved very badly, but because I have a vocation, I am going to be humble and sincere; I am going to own up, and by the grace of God, who never loses battles, I will win in the end. This teaching is as clear as can be. The day before, he had spent some time talking especially about the conviction we should have that the divine call is the foundation of our perseverance. And he now went back to the same theme. Yesterday I said that I wasn’t telling you about all those things of our spirit just for you (by now you all know that very well), but so that they remain clearly etched in your minds and your behaviour all life long. And also, as well as giving you strength to struggle while you are on this earth, I may help you to pass on the right spirit, encouragement, strength and joy to all those sisters of yours who will come after you, with a marvellous continuity, like a river of holy souls.

From Pole to Pole The expansion of the Work to different nations was a reality. During the first years of the 1950s, the women of Opus Dei had established themselves in the United States, Mexico, Portugal, Argentina, Chile, and elsewhere. Preparations were being made as well to go to other places, such as France, Canada, Austria, Japan and Kenya… A little earlier, in mid-September, some sisters of ours had made a long journey by boat, to settle in Brazil and Uruguay. The Lord was blessing his Work with many vocations, but it was obvious that more hands were needed. Furthermore, as our Father always taught us, for someone who lives their self-giving completely, proselytism is a need, not something driven by merely an external sense of urgency. Our Father, always immersed in an irrepressible desire to bring souls to God, was referring to this. Addressing us in second person singular, so that we should feel personally involved, he continued: My daughter, consider the fact that for new vocations to come, together with the main cause – the grace of God – it depends in great measure on you, on your thirst for proselytism. You cannot rest easy if you are not concerned to try to bring other souls to the great family of Opus Dei, to serve God and the whole world, from pole to pole. And this has to be now and when you are older – in forty or fifty years’ time…– wearing a little ribbon around your neck, and needing a walking stick, as we always say. In those years many of us were dedicated to professional work in the administration of our Centres. Countless times our Father made us dream that as the years went by – and it was much sooner than we ever imagined! – there would be daughters of his who would be practising all types of noble professions, and that we would also be involved in a number of educational and social initiatives, or those related to fashion… However, as our Father had always taught us from the beginning, for a dedicated soul all her life is apostolate. That is why he made it clear that: From the last corner of any Administration you carry out an effective apostolate, marvellously and joyfully fulfilling the little tasks of each day: those details that have to be carried out always, at the same time, day after day. In this way we will win the battle that is only seen by God, and that’s what you will talk about in the Confidence and in Confession. The work of proselytism is constant. It would be too comfortable – and a grave error – to think: Since I do not deal with people, since I am not involved in apostolic work… With his way of talking clearly and simply, our Father continued: The good gold is always underground, not in the palm of the hand. And it is in that apparent darkness where the great goldmine of holy vocations can be prepared. The work of proselytism depends on your fervour, on your joy, on that work of yours, hidden, common, ordinary. Then, foreseeing what in years to come might become a temptation for some daughters of his, he continued:

If some day you were to tell me; Father I am now older, I am not young enough to do proselytism, to look for souls…, I would answer: You have even more reason to feel an insatiable hunger eating your insides to keep your apostolate alive, to be able to leave many young souls at the feet of Christ, with your same spirit, with the same vibration, and, if possible, better than you…! On other occasions, our Founder had reminded us that families without children are destined to become extinct, and from there comes the holy desire for vocations, that we should foster in ourselves and in our sisters. Neither the circumstances of our apostolic work, nor age, nor illness: nothing can dispense us from our doing apostolate, he insisted, once again. At that moment, the Father made an aside to say something he had very much in his heart: care of those who were ill, about whom he had written so many years ago, that for a soul in love (…) they are Him. I have already told you on other occasions that those who are ill are a treasure of Opus Dei. It has never happened, and with God’s grace it will never happen, in the Work, that any daughter of mine who became ill should lack the smallest detail of care. A person who is ill doesn’t have to be remembering the smallest details that her mother would have with her, because her sisters in the Work will look after her a thousand times better. And I have added that, if necessary, we would steal a little bit of heaven, knowing for sure that Our Lord would be happy. And all this with real charity, without sentimentality, without doing silly things. All clear, and shiny, clean. The Father again went back to proselytism. Proselytism at all times. Those who have an external profession should not miss any opportunity, not even half a one: they must be like a fire that consumes. Have you seen what happens when a fire does not consume what is around it? It goes out. My daughter, if you want to keep in your heart the fire of Jesus, if you want to be faithful to your divine vocation, you have no choice but to set on fire the places you go to, each person you meet; in that way you will fall more in love with God Our Lord. You will carry out this passionate task without doing peculiar things, prudently, in a natural way, with a holy astuteness; preaching first of all with your example: in the way you dress, with your elegance, your work and your good professional preparation – whether intellectual or of any other type – which will enable you to be very effective. Everything, everything can be proselytism! Our Father used these words with the conviction and the persuasion of someone who had a lot of experience in what he was saying. Foreseeing again, as a good shepherd, the possible temptations that might arise at a critical moment, he warned us, as if whispering to each one in her ear: It could perhaps occur to you: this proselytism always, always: won’t it make me mad? My daughter! Proselytism shows the quality of your spiritual life; that constant flapping of wings, making you supernaturalise everything. That desire for souls will do you no harm, just as the heart does not get in the way with its continual beating. What is more, the day it stops beating is the sure sign of your death. Apostolic desire brings with it a supernatural movement, like a permanent beat, without the noise of words: Lord, I love you, I want other souls throughout the whole world to serve you with the fervour of total selfgiving. Not only with the lips, the tongue, and with words: the most important thing is that you proselytise with your deeds, with your very life. The Father proposed that we should have a universal interest in souls, including those who are very far away: to attract souls to the faith of Jesus Christ, if they don’t have it. We are interested in all souls; we are not a closed shop. I often say that you have one important characteristic, a plus sign. He also talked to us about helping those who are already Catholics to maintain a life of piety and ensure that their life is always coherent with their faith. Afterwards, with everyone, when they already have interior life and are well disposed, one must try to get them to know more and more about the Work, and to ask our Lord to give them the grace of a vocation. 17

The get-together was coming to an end, and the Father made use of the final minutes: in a few days time we would be scattered again throughout the world and, through us, he wanted every person of the Work to do a more effective apostolate, a more daring proselytism, and a more extensive work: Proselytism should not be neglected, especially the one directed at fostering vocations of Numeraries and Associates. And wanting to make clear that all vocations are necessary to extend the Work, he added immediately: We are also very interested in the Supernumeraries. If you are faithful, my daughters, if you really make yourselves available, and form them well, they will be a colossal instrument. The proselytism that is done by all of us shows that there is not one soul in the world we are not interested in. The Father got up. After giving us the blessing, he left the soggiorno, while some of us got close to him to speak for a moment or two. In the two following days we were able to receive new golden coins from the Father. On the 7th, Monday, most of the participants in the course went back to our regions, with our hearts full of firm resolutions, and with the desire to give a good push to the apostolate wherever we might be.

21 November 1958

The 21st November, the feast of the Presentation of Our Lady, was a Friday. Until lunchtime, the day had passed in Villa Tevere like any other weekday. But for the get-together at midday our Father came down, together with don Alvaro and the other members of the General Council. When we had all sat down, our Father asked don Julian to read the text that he had used as the outline for that morning’s meditation in the oratory of Pentecost. It is the one our Father later had published on the first pages of internal publications, for January 1959. When they are busy in their work, the sons of God in Opus Dei try not only to fulfil, but to love, which is to gladly exceed their duty and their spirit of sacrifice. Our Father commented on those words: This is the key to sanctifying ourselves in our professional work, going beyond the boundary, sacrificing ourselves for the love of Jesus Christ, and, therefore, loving all mankind. We are interested in all the souls of the world, without exception!, he affirmed strongly: those who love Christ are our brothers, and we love them a lot: for those who do not love him, and even hate him, we feel a tremendous pity: we pray for them, and, as far as possible, we try to remove the bandage from their eyes that prevents them from seeing clearly. There is no human being who is not loved by a son of God in Opus Dei. My sons, we must always act with a right intention, in the presence of God: in this way, in spite of our personal weaknesses, we will not feel ashamed either before God or before men, and we will try joyfully to correct ourselves as often as necessary. That is why I want you to know that we do not have any enemies. I have never felt an enemy of anyone. And if anyone were to consider himself our enemy, we would want to embrace him as a brother, with all our strength, and bring him into the Heart of God. With a great love for all souls! With love: for there are many good people, and many people with good faith! We have to pray! We have to be understanding! We have to make allowances! No one who comes close to Opus Dei can leave with empty hands: he must feel the attraction of being loved, and that he will always be treated honourably. Klaus sat very close to our Father. Our Founder turned towards him, and told him that if he studied, was cheerful and offered God the uncomfortable things of ordinary life, he could help the apostolic work in Germany a lot. He then referred immediately to the small annoyances caused by the construction works in Villa Tevere: My sons, these houses do not have proper conditions for you to be living here. And, thanks be to God, they never will. I am grateful to Our Lord that you do not even have something to sit on, he told us when he saw many sitting on the floor. You can’t get too comfortable! I am glad that you can’t, because this is the only way to build the definitive Roman College. He reminded us of the tremendous material shortages at the beginning of the Work, which lasted for a long time. During the first years of the Work, and also when we came to Rome, we lived in the most absolute poverty, and other people did not know. We lacked everything, and didn’t show it, because our life is a hidden sacrifice, and that’s how it ought to be. He explained the characteristics of the Christian virtue of poverty according to the spirit of Opus Dei. We are poor. And for that reason we spend as little as possible. But externally we have the obligation to be well dressed, or rather, correctly dressed. He pointed to his cassock. Because I go around the building sites a lot, I usually use the older of the two cassocks I have. We spend very little, but the building works cost a lot of money. Although at the beginning of the Work I didn’t have anything, later, on many occasions, and with so much to be done, we counted on the help of 19 generous people, who wanted to give from what they had in the service of the Lord. Some gave us money; others gave us properties for the apostolic work. I insist: there are people who help us generously, and, thinking about what God expects of Opus Dei, we have the right and the duty to count on people are very competent, and well prepared professionally, to administer that money and make it yield a proper return. Why should the money of Catholics be managed by incompetent people? It is only common sense, he continued, that people who are professionally well prepared and capable of benefitting from the means provided by so many Catholics, and many who are not Catholics but who see we are honest people, should really benefit. On the other hand – and difficult to understand! – there are, also among those who call themselves good, people who are pained by this. Because, until now, dealing with money was considered shameful. And, continuing with these ideas, he said: We, with the spirit that the Lord has given us, do not despise any upright and noble human activity: they can all be sanctified! Even those that seem irrelevant: we join in them and, with our spirit, we set about changing them into a way that leads to God. The Church has officially confirmed this, and encourages us to involve ourselves in all the public activity we can. Notice that, reflecting on what we presented them with, it has been confirmed that it would be imprudent to act otherwise. We can and should sanctify all types of noble human activities. All of them! Why not? The Father was speaking forcefully. He wanted to leave very clearly engraved upon our minds even the ultimate consequences of our spirit. Moreover, I have seen (and some have even told me themselves) that people from many different walks of life are happy to know that their money is being administered by honest people, by Catholics. And, yet, I repeat, some feel bothered about it: in our case, they see that a person of Opus Dei is busy with financial matters, and they exclaim hypocritically: ‘But, he works in a bank! He is a director of a financial institution!’ Well, of course! We are not going to tell whoever had this career before he came to the Work to change his profession, because that would be to interfere with his professional calling, and would not be true to the spirit that the Lord has entrusted to us. That is the way we deal with everyone. We don’t tell a worker to go to university, if he is not able to do so; nor would we tell someone with a university vocation to go and work on a farm. In the Work no one is moved from his place; rather, everyone is encouraged to try to improve his professional formation. At the beginning of October, on the 9th, Pope Pius XII died. From then on our Father asked us to pray a lot for the new Pope, even before he had been elected. One of these days, while he was checking the work being done in the Oratory of the Holy Apostles, where some of the students of the Roman College were helping out, he told us: I would like to talk to you once more about the coming election of the Holy Father. You know the love we have for the Pope, my sons. After Jesus and Mary, we love the Pope, whoever he may be, with all the strength of our soul. That is why we already love the Roman Pontiff who is to come. We are determined to serve him with all our soul. Offer each day’s work for that intention. It can and it should help you to be in the presence of God. A work well done, well finished, perfect, with love for the little things. During those weeks our Father did not miss any opportunity to ask for prayers for the new Roman Pontiff, even before he was chosen. I remember in a family gathering in the Aula Magna he insisted again: Pray, offer your joy to Our Lord. Even this (referring to the very happy time we were having together), we offer it to our Lord for the next Pope, as we have offered the Mass all these days, as we have offered… even our breathing. With the beginning of the Conclave, our prayers increased. At last, in the afternoon of Tuesday, the 28th of October, John XXIII was elected. Many of us were at St Peter’s Square at the time of the fumata bianca; and there we received the first blessing urbi et orbi of the new Roman Pontiff. Nearly a month had gone by since that get-together, but our Founder wanted to remind us about the duty to pray for the Holy Father. 20

Pray a lot for the new Pope, who is so good and makes himself loved so much. And even if he wasn’t like that, we would also pray, because the Pope is the Father of all Christians. These are our loves on earth: first Jesus Christ, afterwards the Virgin and then the Pope. At that point, in a more intimate tone of voice, he called upon our sense of responsibility, living so close to the Founder of the Work. My sons, I am a poor man, a sinner who loves Jesus Christ, a poor creature who needs your understanding, your affection, your prayers… But I am the Founder of Opus Dei, because the Lord has wished it so. Popes, I have already known five; cardinals, many; bishops, a pile of them… Founders of Opus Dei, on the other hand, there is only one. And when I die there will not be another. The spirit of Opus Dei has been given to me by Our Lord, and I have enshrined it in our particular Law, and in many other documents that I have written for you. God will ask you to give an account for having lived close to me, even though I can’t dedicate as much time to you as I would like to, close to that poor priest who loved you so much, so much, more than your mothers! Here he made an aside: If your parents were to hear me, first they would be cross, but afterwards they would feel very happy… I will pass away and your brothers who will follow you will look at you as one looks at a relic, because you have known the Founder. When I die, someone will take my place. You must love him more than you love me, with more human affection as well. If not, something is wrong. I ask you to do so for the love of Jesus Christ. But God will ask you to account, I repeat, for having been formed close to the Founder of the Work. When some time has gone by, and they ask you: what did the Father tell you? You will answer: the Father told us that he was a poor sinner. It is true. I am only a poor man: I have deserved to go to hell so many times. Pray for me to be good and faithful! You will also tell them: sometimes the Father gave me a kiss on my forehead. And he gave one to the person who was nearest. He reminded us about how marvellous our vocation was, for it had opened up a new way on the old path of a Christian life. In God’s Church, Opus Dei has raised and resolved a great number of juridical problems, which once solved seem simple, just like the egg of Columbus.1 Among such questions is the one of having only one class of members: for the first time in the history of the Church, clerics and lay people have the same vocation. There are no castes in Opus Dei. In other institutions, the clerics form the aristocracy; here we are all the same. We have broken centuries’-old moulds. And he concluded: Apart from my own miseries and yours, everything else in Opus Dei is marvellous. He then went on to talk to us about fraternal correction as one of the means of assuring the purity of our spirit. One of the most beautiful Customs that we have in Opus Dei is the practice of fraternal correction, which is a marvellous means of holiness. If in some Centre fraternal correction were not to be practised, there would be a lack of good spirit. When I presented our Statutes to the Holy See, around the years 1943, 1946, 1949 and I spoke about making fraternal correction to the Father, they always came up with a difficulty: how can the person in charge be corrected? He cannot be told anything! But I wasn’t satisfied, and I would tell them: How are they going to leave me, being a poor man, and those who will succeed me, who will be better than me, but also poor men, without benefitting from that means of holiness? When practising that deeply Christian custom, those who give a fraternal correction, even though it may cost them an effort and they will have to overcome their reluctance, and those who receive a fraternal correction, even though it hurts and they have to be humble, are

1 This refers to the problem people had in the time of Christopher Columbus, with his novel theory that the world was not flat but round.

both in possession of a marvellous means of holiness taken straight from the Gospel. This reason convinced them. They told me: well then, add some words as an explanation. Remembering the Guardian Angels (Angeli Custodes), I then gave the name custodes to the two persons in charge of making opportune remarks to the Father.
