One is in charge of material matters, don Javier; and the other the spiritual ones, don Alvaro, who is also my ordinary confessor. I make use of the fact that he is not here, to tell you that if somebody in Opus Dei can be called co-founder, it is Alvaro del Portillo. As we did in many other get-togethers with our Father, we sang some songs. Our Father liked us to show our cheerfulness in that way. When we had finished, he commented: Don’t be afraid of singing popular songs, which nearly always speak of love; human love, which is noble and holy, when it is clean. We carry that love with us to the divine level: it is a wonderful light. We think about the love we have for Our Lord, and about the love of our parents, and the love we might have had for another human being, if He had led us that way; and we offer it all to the Most Holy Trinity, in total dedication, always new, always young, always fervent, even though we have to go against the grain. Referring to the words of the song, though I can’t remember which one it was, our Founder talked about our self-giving, which is also for love. Our Lord has said to us: “You, you are all for me!” And we have given him our whole heart! Be grateful to God, because he has asked us for all our love, all our life, all our heart, with total self-giving. But be very clear about this: How are we going to be scandalised about human love, about that holy love of our parents, to whom we owe a great part of our vocation? I always say that I bless that love with both my priestly hands. Matrimony is a sacrament, a supernatural reality instituted by Jesus Christ. Opus Dei has made matrimony a divine way, a vocation for Supernumeraries, and there are now, all over the world, thousands of normal Christian, bright and cheerful homes! There are now thousands of married people who struggle to be saints, and they can be so, as much as, or more so than the Numeraries: that depends on how each one corresponds to grace. Never be frightened of what is noble, even though it is not for us: it is for those whom the Lord has called to another way. We had already been in the get-together a good while. When I remember moments like that, I always think: How we felt at ease with our Father! We could be hours and hours, sitting on the floor or standing without getting tired. It was the feast of the Presentation of Our Lady, the anniversary of Aunt Carmen’s First Holy Communion. This was the reason why he felt like talking about her last hours on earth, the previous year. She bore her illness like a holy person of Opus Dei. I feel consoled when I think about it. Before she died, I told her that we would bury her here, in the sottocripta. And it occurred to her to comment: But listen, if Santiago goes there, he should be careful, because it is very cold. Next to her, with me, were don Alvaro, don Javier and Dr Pastor, who was taking her pulse. There were also some women present, Numeraries and Assistant Numeraries. She well deserved such company. I cried like a child, alone, quietly before the Tabernacle, until she died, because I saw that another chapter was ending, because I loved my sister very much, and I was thinking about how much she had helped us, though she didn’t have a vocation, as she said. Later when she left this earth, I prayed a response, and, as soon as I could, went down to say Mass. I experienced great peace and was very happy: happy with God’s Will, because He knows very well what He does. But it cost me an effort, because with her – I insist – thirty years of history of the Work had gone. And it cost me also because I have a heart. It is not bad to have a heart. It is bad when it is out of control, not properly checked. But not to have one is much worse. Don’t tell these things until I have died. Then you can tell them. Now they have escaped me, and that is that. I had not, even remotely, thought of speaking about them. We were all moved, and the Father explained to us that ours is a holy sanctity. We have a great recipe for being effective: personal sanctity…, and good humour. Let those be sad who do not want to be children of God! 22

We are not bothered about human glory, not even spiritual glory, as something exclusively ours, but we have to be saints, with a very great sanctity, in order to praise and glorify God. I am telling you that we are not concerned about glory, because we must be hidden and cheerful saints, hard working and cheerful, effective and cheerful, humble and cheerful. To say it in a few words, we must hide and disappear, so that it is only the Lord who shines. The get-together finished. Each one went back to his place of work or study. We knew that we had had the immense good fortune of having been a few minutes with our Founder; and having listened to him, we were aware how responsible we had to be. As on so many occasions, our Father had given us many things to speak to our Lord about.

25 December 1958

When remembering Christmas 1958, there comes to mind a jumble of images mostly related with the task of furnishing some areas of Villa Tevere. I remember for example the joy when on the 23rd of December we at last put in place the armchairs and the tables which formed the main furniture of the soggiorno of Uffici. I also clearly recall some scenes of the construction of the Christmas crib in the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo, which is now in Uffici: the dexterity of those who dealt with plaster, and the satisfied faces at seeing their work finished. In any case, the most vivid memories refer to the Christmas present our Father gave us on the 25th of December. The day before he had given us a clue, when he unexpectedly came to the midday gettogether. Among other things, he told us that he had been thinking for some time about don José Ramón, who would be spending those family days thousands of kilometres away in Japan, without anyone else of the Work. These words of our Father, with which he showed all his affection for that son of his, made me see in a new light the fraternity which, thanks be to God, we live in the Work. On the 25th, after attending the last of the three Christmas Masses, we venerated the Child in the oratory of the Holy Apostles. Later, most went to the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo to finish things off. There was a marvellous atmosphere with a background of Christmas carols, conversations and laughter. After lunch, during the get-together, Christmas carols again formed an important part. Before it ended, the Rector gave us the good news that our Founder would join us all in the Aula Magna. When he came in, our Father asked us to tell him things, for he had not been able to be with us earlier. He went up onto the stage and addressed his sons: Since this morning – he said smilingly – you have been greeting me with your auguri. I also have been greeting you from the beginning of the day, and I now repeat: auguri! May you have a very happy Christmas, and may the New Year bring you many blessings from God, and be very fruitful. Our faces showed clearly that we could not have wished for a better gift. And also, we could see that the Father wanted his greeting to be a bit special. What do you want me to tell you? he continued. It is not going to be anything new, just the same as always: that we must be effective and happy in the world. Because I do not want you to be happy only in Heaven. I want you to be happy on earth: a happiness that is always relative and incomplete, here on earth; a happiness which is compatible with tears and suffering. Pain is often a pathway and an expression of love; and in a Christian soul it always produces happiness and joy. I want you to be very happy and very faithful in this year that is just beginning. Listening to those words of our Father, it was easy to resolve to improve personally and be more faithful. Even more so when he made us consider the splendid apostolic panorama which depended on our response to our vocation. We have many things to do in the world, many great and good things. Many years ago now – because this is the thirty-first year of my vocation in Opus Dei – I would tell your older brothers, and I tell it to you now, that we are living a great, marvellous, splendid, novel: of love and adventure. When you were fifteen or twenty years old, you picked up a big book, with great interest, and practically devoured it! Well, you yourselves are the protagonists of a novel of love and adventures, both human and divine. But the Lord asks us for fidelity. And we will only be faithful, if we don’t lose our way, if we continue to fix our eyes on the goal. The image of the way brought to our Father’s mind and lips a scene common on the mountain roads, and which he had seen often during his apostolic travels through Europe. That is why, addressing don Alvaro, who was still convalescing after an illness, he asked him: Do you remember when we were travelling along those roads of Central Europe? Don Alvaro nodded in assent, and our Father went on: There were some tall poles there, painted red, so that when it snowed there would be a point of reference and you could know where the road was. Like the good teacher that he was, he drew a supernatural meaning from this fact. We also have marvellous signs which our Lord has given us so we don’t lose our way: our Norms of piety. You must care for them affectionately! Fight every day! This is our interior life: the struggle to fulfil the norms, to fulfil them lovingly, for ours is a way of love. And stressing this idea, he commented: On earth, when people do not form a home, they usually do not do so for two reasons: a selfish one, or for the Love of God, (with a capital L) which includes love for all mankind. This marvellous novel, this epic journey, is travelled by means of the Norms. He referred later to the difficulties that can crop up at times in the interior life, and how to solve them. Talking this morning with a group of your Italian brothers, I was telling them: Sometimes one of my sons asks me: Father, what if I find myself tired and cold; what if, when I do the norms, I think I am playacting… I tell that brother of yours and I tell all of you: play-acting? That’s a great thing, my son! The Lord is playing with us – ludens in orbe terrarum (Prov 8:31) – as a father plays with his children. God is eternal, and beside Him, we are very small children. We are playing before God our Father, and God plays with us, as fathers play with their little children. During these days, especially: the days of Christmas, the three wise men, in Italy La Befana, or Santa Claus…: God is always with us, his children, because we are very dear to him: deliciae meae esse cum filiis hominum (Prov 8:31). The Lord is playing with us! Well, when we feel like we are play-acting, because we feel cold and listless; when we find the interior struggle is hard, or it goes against the grain… Our Father interrupted the list, to tell us his personal experience. Poor me! I have always had to go against the grain! Sometimes the Lord has led me along another way; but normally, I go against the grain. I do things not because I like doing them, but because I should be doing them, because it is my duty… He then continued the imaginary dialogue with that son of his: Father, if it seems that I am play-acting … Very well: A wonderful moment has come to perform a human play for a divine spectator. God is the spectator. Act your play. The Lord is your spectator! The Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit: the Blessed Trinity will be watching us while we act out the play. When we act out a play for God, for love of him, trying to please him; when we “go against the grain”; when we feel we are playing the role of a jester: let’s think that we are jesters for God. It’s beautiful – don’t doubt it – to perform a play for Love, sacrificing ourselves, without any personal reward, simply to please God who is playing with us. Ludens coram eo omni tempore, ludens in orbe terrarum (Prov 8:31). Live by love. Filled with love and speaking very clearly, his words took on the tone of an urgent call on our loyalty. Be faithful for me! This is what the Father asks of you for this year about to begin, and for the whole of your life. I think I will die a little old man: within a few years they will have to carry me in a carrycot into the sunshine. I am no longer young and I am not ashamed to say that, when you are older, your brothers will envy you for having lived with the Father. They will ask you: What did the Father say? Well, he said that when the moment of interior dryness comes, when one doesn’t feel for the things of God, when piety does not come easily, we have to serve the Lord with the same supernatural warmth as when we felt we were on fire, being really pious – if necessary – play-acting. Play-acting? What does it matter? It is not play-acting. It is love for God the Father, God the Son and God the Holy Spirit. I have special veneration for, a great devotion to, the Blessed Trinity, and I would like to inject it into you as a part of the spirit of Opus Dei. Then, so as to engrave these words deeply, he related an ancient legend of a monk acrobat. There was a poor man, a lay monk in a monastery, who was not good at doing anything. He reasoned in 25 this way: Father Prior speaks very well; Father Vicar hears confessions; and that other one is a man who gives good advice… I am nothing, but I wish to honour our Lady in some way. And at night he went in front of a statue of our Lady in the monastery church and did somersaults and played tricks with his hands, and did everything he knew. He did it marvellously, because it had been his job for many years. One day the Superior watched him without being noticed, but did not scold him or say anything to him. He let him carry on with his jumps and tricks before Our Lady until the end of his life, because in the glowing face of the statue he discovered a Mother’s smile. From the lips of our Founder there sprang up the supernatural lesson of the story: My son, when you don’t feel like it, act out that great comedy of love before God, and you will do something very good on this earth! Fulfil the Norms always, and you will pass on to others the divine madness of the Work! You will be salt and light. You will love all creatures. You will be a plus sign in the life of men: you will not be anti-anything. You will be Opus Dei! You will be the glory of this poor man, who is a sinner! You will be the glory of God, which is the greatest thing you can be, and you will do great things on earth, while being no more than clay, which is what I am! When the Father finished speaking, we were somewhat overwhelmed by the strength of his words. Fortunately, those who belonged to the choir quickly snapped out of their lethargy and, directed by don Iñaki, began to sing the Acclamations, with the words that don Alvaro had composed the year before as a manifestation of our affection for the Father. Afterwards, all together, we sang the carol Madre en la puerta hay un Niño. When we finished, our Founder thanked us: we had reminded him of our Grandmother, who would sing him that carol when he was a little boy. Every time I remember or hear again about the marvellous novel, our life in the Work, I cannot help raising my heart to God in acts of thanksgiving. Over the years, our Founder’s words have really been fulfilled, and the horizon becomes wider and wider. Many of those of us who were there that day in the Aula Magna have crossed oceans to sow the seed of Opus Dei all over the world. But the most marvellous adventure has been to discover, in the Norms, in the silence and constant work of every day and in the apostolate, the value of unquestioned fidelity, which shines above all else; also when the Lord, playing with us, allows us to go against the grain.

1 January 1959

“The New Year has begun in the best way possible. After breakfast the Father came to the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo, with don Alvaro and some other members of the Council”. That is how the pages of the diary of the Roman College began. I remember the scene very well. Those of us who were in the soggiorno sat down around our Founder, while the bell of the Galleria and of Uffici summoned the rest. I expect many things during this year that is beginning, if you are faithful, were the Father’s opening words. Our Founder had sat down in one of the two armchairs close to the fireplace, facing the windows that look on to the street. In front of him, between the columns and behind the table, we were sitting on the floor, on the seats or standing further away, attentively following his first words of the new year. Don Alvaro had remained standing. Many of those who came in took the opportunity to wish him a Happy New Year. Our Founder commented that, not to break tradition, he would celebrate the feast on the 9th – my birthday, he added amusingly – with the Roman College. Someone showed him a copy of the Christmas carols that those in charge of book-binding had prepared to replace and improve those we had used until then. Our Father explained that later on a better edition would have to be made, which would include the music. He also said that perhaps he would preach a meditation for us in the afternoon, adding: The purpose of the meditation is to bring us closer to God, to bring God into the things of the earth: deliciae meae esse cum filiis hominum (Prov 8:31). He turned to Peter, who had come from Britain, and continued: I consider it a special blessing of God that this year we can begin the work in Oxford. Let’s see how we can push it this year of 1959. It was great desire of our Father to begin the apostolic work in that English city, with its old and very prestigious university. The summer before he had spent some time working in London; there he had written on the back of an image of Our Lady, the day after he had arrived: Sancta Maria, Sedes Sapientiae, filios tuos adiuva! Oxford, Cambridge, 5-VIII-58; and had encouraged many of his sons to pray for the Work to begin in Oxford. It was one of those intentions that he asked us to pray for especially that 1st of January 1959. Afterwards, he asked those around him for a diary, or a picture…, to write the first words of the year. Joaquin gave him a small copybook. What do you want me to put? our Father asked. Whatever you want, Father. Well, then – he said, while he prepared to write – I will write what I always do. And he wrote: Semper ut iumentum! Mariano, Roma, 1 de enero de 1959. Addressing Klaus, our Father recalled that once he had passed through Germany in the middle of winter. Do you remember, Alvaro? And he talked about a journey from Bonn to Vienna, with everything covered in snow and a very slippery road surface, and hearing the sound of people singing in all the towns they passed through. Peter sang an English Christmas Carol, which they sing a lot in London. When he finished, our Founder asked us to pray that year especially for those who would be ordained. It was precisely about this that he spoke most of the get-together. We need priests. Pray for those brothers of yours who will be ordained this year, so that they may be splendid instruments of unity. Union with God, with the Holy See, with the Father and with the Directors, is the root of our effectiveness. Pray that we will never be lacking cheerful, learned and holy priests. We are receiving requests from everywhere because, although there are already quite a few, without them the work would be incomplete: it could not be done as the spirit of the Work demands. Our Father continued talking and, to illustrate the need for priests in Opus Dei, he started to comment on the two miraculous catches of fish. Do you remember that first miraculous catch of fish? The Apostles are at sea, on that tiny sea that is Lake Genesareth, and they spend the night trying to fill their nets, in vain. Praeceptor, per totam noctem laborantes, nihil cepimus (Lk 5:5). The same thing can happen to us in our apostolic work. If we have been working on our own, as some people do sometimes, without being united to the Church, how effective can that apostolate be? On our own we have not been able to do anything, per totam noctem laborantes, nihil cepimus. I have only managed to do what is in the hands of a poor creature, and have wasted my time. He reminded us that, years ago, when he preached many retreats to priests, he gave spiritual direction as a brother priest, affectionately but firmly, to those who needed to improve their union with their Bishop. When I was young and used to preach many retreats to priests and religious, I met some who were not very obedient to their bishop, and I would ask them: ‘How are you going to be effective as a “freelance” apostle? How can a branch separated from the vine yield fruit? It wouldn’t even produce green leaves, let alone choice, juicy clusters of grapes. The branch united to the vine bears fruit. Otherwise it becomes a dry stick, fit only for a fire, or at best for herding animals, and then to be trampled on by passers-by. My children, be very united to the vine stock, be closely united to Christ, by being very obedient to the Directors, especially if you have a position of government or if you are priests. He went back to the scene of the first miraculous catch of fish. But we must continue listening: in verbo autem tuo laxabo rete (Lk 5:5). In the name of God I will cast the net. What a beautiful thing, rectifying one’s whole life, when for whatever reason one has done apostolate on one’s own! It is to put oneself again in intimate union with the vine stock. It means having leaves and bunches of grapes once again. He explained to us that, for the first time in the history of the Church, the priests and the laymen do not form two categories of members, but are one class only. Here all of us, priests and lay people, as members of the Work, are all the same. There is only one practical difference: the priests have a greater obligation to be a carpet so that their brothers and sisters may tread softly. Priests should be willing, affectionate, cheerful slaves of their brothers and sisters. They are not ordained to command, but to serve. He went on to speak about some characteristics of government in the Work, before finishing the gettogether with a comment about the role of priests in Opus Dei. In our way of the Work, priesthood is, as it were, an accident. The Father calls this one or that one, and all are completely free to agree to be ordained or not. Of course, before that they complete their ecclesiastical studies o the highest standard of the pontifical universities. But, for us, I repeat, priesthood is a circumstance that does not modify our vocation in any way. However, it is very necessary, because when a soul comes up against the sacramental wall, if there were no priests of Opus Dei, others would have to be found who would either have no particular spirit or another distinct spirit. And that would be disastrous. To make sure we understood this point very well, he once again went to the Gospel: this time to the second miraculous catch of fish in St John’s Gospel, after the Resurrection of Our Lord. I remember he invited us to recall the scene: the Apostles who again get tired in vain, and Jesus, who from the shore, indicates where they should throw the nets. They obey, and catch one hundred and fifty-three large fish. Continue with that scene. Look how Peter and the other Apostles bring the fish to Christ, who is on the shore. The fish, to the priest. You have the duty to take as many souls as possible to spiritual direction with your brother priests. Our Father made a brief digression: 28

Nobody in Opus Dei has any free time. When somebody tells me that he has time to spare, I become very sad, because I think he has not assimilated the spirit God has given me, that he is not fulfilling his duty. So, he repeated, this is going to be the fruit of this get-together. Already from this first day of the year, we are going to pray especially for the priests who are going to be ordained this year of 1959: that they may be learned, cheerful, and holy. Do you agree? We are being asked from all over, and we and we cannot deny these requests. We are thousands of people in the Work, who have to be looked after properly; and there are also those large groups of active Cooperators, who need a lot of care. The Supernumeraries look after the Cooperators. The Numeraries look after the Supernumeraries, helped by some chosen from among themselves. Our sea is immense, without shores! And there is so much to be done! The salt of the priesthood must not be lacking. Is the priest more salt than the layman? Well, I tell you: as members of Opus Dei, the same grace, the same salt; but the lay person cannot administer the sacraments … In the Work we need priests, among other reasons, to administer the sacrament of Penance, which is a very important means of spiritual direction. I have already said that a good part of spiritual direction is done by laymen: you have a right to administer real spiritual direction with the souls that come to the Work. Whoever says the contrary knows nothing about Theology. But I repeat, when we come to the sacramental wall, we need priests. Because it is sad to see how, at times, they take the fish, and they put them back in the sea, ill-treated and half-drowned. He stopped for a moment and asked us to sing Soy una mula. When we had finished he asked us to pray for don José Ramón, so that during those feast-days he would feel accompanied in Japan, where he was alone, with no one else of the Work. He is happy working for God in that place which is a new frontier. The Americans (he explained) will have to go there. And soon, everyone else to more places. Let’s see who starts first in Australia and New Zealand; because Hermann is going to Holland. We are dividing the world among us! he said as a joke. He added that in all those nations we would begin to work apostolically, as we have always done in the Work. Our brothers have gone to many countries, he reminded us, with what they were wearing, a wooden cross, an image of our Lady and the Father’s blessing. The rest will be given by God. However, he clarified immediately that, for the apostolic work to develop faster, it is better, if possible, not to start from zero. When bees have to prepare the wax to make the honeycomb, they waste a lot of time and energy; if they find the honeycomb already made, they produce more honey from the start; clearly, before God nothing is lost; but, in the end, we have to prepare the honeycombs well, so that my sons can produce honey as soon as possible. On the other hand, beginning with nothing one could give the impression of a kind of poverty not in accordance with the spirit of the Work. If necessary, we begin as we have always begun: with absolutely nothing. We start with whatever we can. That is very wonderful. But I understand that it is God’s Will that we should no longer act like this now that we are in a position to do something about it. Now we do things with the same spirit and the same optimistic and loving enthusiasm which has characterised our beginnings everywhere: with the same spirit, I say, not with more, because that is not possible, but with more material resources. In this way, from the very beginning, the corporate works will look as they should and in accordance with the instruments of Opus Dei. He spoke about the specific ways that had been thought out, so as to facilitate the beginning of the work in new countries, and added: The financial effort should always come from the Regions and Centres themselves, because eventually they will have to give back what we have managed to raise for them here. It is worth praying for this, during the new year that is just beginning. He reminded us of some anecdotes that reflected the absolute poverty which has been characteristic of the Work: There are quite a few brothers of yours who know what it is to have only one meal a day, and a poor one at that, in order to serve God. Those who might have had everything in their parents’ home have deprived 29 themselves of a thousand comforts for the love of God, and have not missed them. When we opened the Centre in Oporto, we didn’t have any chairs to sit on. They borrowed a table and a few chairs from some neighbours. But we had a good time. And in Valencia we began with a small house, where there was nothing. The Father went on to comment how one day, in 1939, the Vicar General of the diocese of Valencia wanted him to celebrate Mass using for the first time some very good vestments he had been given as a present. While celebrating Mass at the altar of the Most Blessed Trinity, in the cathedral, the Father became unwell and nearly fainted. They took me home in a car, but there was no bed, only a camp bed: some planks on iron legs, with curtains for bed sheets. Total poverty! Now, even though we have more money, we must be as poor as before. If not, my sons, we would not be doing the right thing. Our Father stood up: the get-together had finished. But we did see him again that same afternoon, in the Aula Magna. There he spoke to a group of our brothers from the Italian Region, and mentioned details of the life of Cardinal Schuster. Towards the end of the afternoon, we had a meditation beside the crib of the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo. And we were all left with our Father’s holy zeal for souls, after he had asked us to make a special effort in our daily struggle to do Opus Dei.

14 February 1959

As on the eve of all the great feasts, during the dinner of the 13th of February of 1959 we were greatly looking forward to the possibility of having a get-together with the Father the following day. Then we saw a sister of ours who looked as if she was bringing good news. “The Father wants to see all of us tomorrow in the Aula Magna,” she said. I don’t need to describe our immediate outburst of joy. And it wasn’t surprising, because, apart from the fact that we enjoyed those occasions very much, we were very conscious of the graces we would receive as we listened to our Founder. But also, although we did not want to think about it, we students of the Roman College of Holy Mary, who at that time were living in Villa Sacchetti, knew that as time passed, certain unrepeatable dates would go by and would not come back, and the moment of our return to our respective Regions was approaching. All of us wanted to make the most of the good fortune of living close to our Father. We were particularly glad to hear from those who had just come to Rome, and who brought news of Montse. One of them had been able to see her before leaving and told us that she was offering up all the pains of her illness, which was progressing aggressively, (she would die at the end of the following month) but she was very happy. The sun rose on the 14th. The diary of the Roman College on that date records: In the meditation our thanksgiving has continued and increased on hearing (…) such great and wonderful things about our Beautiful Mother. I have read the words the Father said in previous years, this same day. We felt very small, seeing the magnitude and divine nature of our way. And all we can say is: Gratias tibi, Deus, gratias tibi! with the firm desire to keep making acts of thanksgiving all day. Once outside the oratory, there were greetings and hugs all round… The time between breakfast and the get-together, which took place at half past ten, was busy: we all hurried to finish our work quickly, so that we would be ready some time before the get-together started.

A divine path Apart from those of us who lived in the Montagnola and Villa Sacchetti, a good group of sisters of ours from the Italian Region were also present; many of them had not met the Father personally, and eagerly awaited his arrival. A big table had been placed on the platform, with a red velvet cover with golden stripes, also embroidered with gold. This was the first time it was used. At the back of the armchair prepared for the Father there was also an embroidered seal of the Work. On the table, to the left, was the crucifix that normally presides over the meeting room of the General Council. While we were waiting in the Aula Magna, the telephone rang. It was the Father, to tell us that he would be coming a few minutes late, because don Alvaro had a visitor. During those days, don Alvaro had been convalescing: he had just come home after an operation. I remember very well that we had been praying for his health, because our Father had told us that he was not feeling well. But on the 2nd of February, our Founder came to Villa Sacchetti, lit the candle of the Galleria de la Madonna and, after speaking to us about what don Alvaro meant for the Work, urged us to pray intensely for his health. He added that when the candle was spent, we should light another one, and continue to do so until don Alvaro returned to Villa Tevere. I also remember that during that time we received a pile of letters from the Regions, telling us that that they were very united to the Father, and the whole Work, and they were also keeping don Alvaro’s health in mind. It was a real clamour of union with our Founder and with this brother of ours, whom we needed to be at our Father’s side. Ten minutes later, our Father came down. As soon as he came in, he looked at where the crucifix was, and turned it a bit, explaining that in this way we could also see it. He then started to speak; clearly and incisively, he explained basic details of the spirit of Opus Dei, beginning with the exclusively supernatural character of our calling: I wanted to be with you today, my daughters, because today we celebrate the anniversary of the day on which our Lord thought fit to open this divine way on earth for women. Today also is the anniversary of the foundation of the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross. The first date was in 1930; the second, in 1943. Nearly thirty years had gone by since 1930 and the Work had expanded greatly: apart from the growth in the countries where we were already working, we had started recently in a number of different nations: Germany, Switzerland, Venezuela, Colombia, Peru, Guatemala, Brazil, and Canada…; soon we would go to new places, and the vocations were coming. My daughters, a divine way on earth, our Father continued. When I wanted to impress upon you that our life is supernatural, that our way of acting in the world, with this dedication to God among people who are just like us, who live an ordinary life on earth, when I have tried to etch this in the minds of my children and my own, I have had written everywhere – engraved on stone or painted on walls – words from Sacred Scripture, to wake us up. The Father was referring precisely to the phrase: elegit nos in ipso ante mundi constitutionem ut essemus sancti in conspectu eius, which was engraved on the lintel of one of the doors of what was the Secretary General’s office, and today is the Map Room of Villa Vecchia. The wall where that door opens has a geographical representation of a part of the globe, expressing the universality of the call to holiness, and causing whoever looks at it to be filled with a greater desire of fidelity and proselytism. Our Lord has chosen us before creating the world, you and me, each one of my daughters and sons, so that we become saints in his presence. How beautiful this is, isn’t it? It is divine, our Father continued, as if relishing those endearing reflections from the inspired Book. And here those words of Isaiah fit like a glove: redemi te, et vocavi te nomine tuo: meus es tu. These are things that I invite you to meditate on, as if we were listening to them directly from the lips of Jesus Christ: I have redeemed you, I have called you by your name; you – our Lord adds – belong to me: you are mine. You are his because you have dedicated your life to God, among the people, with the same desires as other people have, passing unnoticed as happens with salt, which disappears into what is cooked, and makes it taste better. And making an aside, he gave a homely, graphic example. You know perfectly well how tasteless a dish is with no salt. On the other hand, if you add a little, as you do for me sometimes, it is something else, it becomes tasty. Our way is one of holiness. Without any merit on our part, you and I have been called to be saints in conspectu eius, in his presence. And so, the commitment of each daughter and each son of God in his Work is to make every effort to achieve personal holiness. Our Father spoke forcefully, showing a firm conviction. With the benefit of hindsight, I now think of the many times he must have had to explain this basic idea of our spirit all those years, in order to make them understood by people who could not conceive that holiness in the middle of the world was possible. As he continued, our Founder’s words became more personal, more intimate; he started to talk in the singular, as if his fatherly heart wanted to call each one to a greater self-surrender, and lead to a deeper examination: That is the secret of Opus Dei, personal holiness. This is what we have come for, and we have to propose it seriously, all the more since we know – I don’t get tired of saying so – that holiness is fidelity and is “felicity”. You are only unhappy when you are silly enough not to follow your way of holiness; when you cease thinking about extending the Kingdom of God, and concentrate on yourself, when you forget your condition as a person who has given herself to God, with a total, complete dedication.

A means within easy reach We were listening to the Father in silence. Close to me were some who had come from the United States, England and Ireland, to join the Roman College of Holy Mary. At this stage of the course, they no longer needed anyone to translate our Founder’s words for them. Perhaps you are thinking now, my daughter: And what will I do to become a saint? Truth only has one way, and that is why I always say the same thing whenever you ask me this question: Fulfil the norms for me! If you struggle to fulfil the Norms of the plan of life of the Work, you are going along the way of holiness, you are pleasing God and you will reach the goal to which the Lord has called you: before the creation of the world, you were – we all were– in God’s mind, in his heart. How marvellous! How grateful we must be to our Father God! And to fulfil the Norms well? You know it perfectly well: if you are bent on having presence of God, you will see how well you do the prayer, you will see then how perfectly you work, with what enthusiasm, with what eagerness. Father, very often I don’t feel like it…! I don’t feel like it very often either. But, my daughter, you are not silly, and neither am I; we carry out this task and that other one because we know they please God, not because we feel like it. That is what fidelity consists of, and from this we find happiness: acting for the love of God, the same whether it is day or night, if it is raining, or if the weather is fine, if it is hot or cold. In sickness and in health. Let us do things with love! After so many years, I remember perfectly the energy with which our Father stressed during those months how important the Norms were. In particular, at Christmas he spoke to us several times about the Divine Comedy that our Lord wants us to perform when our feelings seem to be off somewhere else. Now he continued urging us on, to seek support and refuge in our Mother Holy Mary: My daughters, in the morning prayer – which I did aloud for your brothers in the General Council – I thought about what I consider every day at the moment of the examination of conscience, at midday, and, above all, in a more thorough one at night. During those brief moments we have to be like shopkeepers, who prepare their accounts at the end of each day, to see how much they have earned or lost. I drew a line at the bottom, and do you know the result? Pauper servus et humilis! Lord, I am a poor man! Forgive me! Help me! I suppose you do the same, and… what do you discover you are? Also a poor creature, full of weaknesses, of little things, that you often feel the attraction of sensuality, of pride and of so many other things. But the Lord has chosen us from all eternity, as we were seeing before, knowing what we are like, and also knowing that, with his grace, we can become saints, and therefore, marvellous instruments. Then, after recognising our humility and our nothingness – pauper servus et humilis! – after that, we must immediately be filled with joy. Have you seen how mothers behave? Each one can remember hers, I remember mine. I now recall the scene of the little boy who escapes from the arms of his mother and who can hardly stand on his tiny little legs, but wants adventure; and, if the mother is not attentive, he will go out to the street, and mingle not only with the passers-by but also with the traffic. But the mother goes out running after him, picks him up, and hugs him, and presses him close to her heart. My dearest daughters, this is the sure refuge we have in our Heavenly Mother! I tell her now, for you and for me: when the imagination goes a little bit mad, when sensuality attempts to drag us down, when pride threatens to darken our intellect…, You, my Mother, our Mother, come, take us up in your arms and press us against your Heart.

One love only Devotion to Our Lady, the Father continued, Love her a lot. She is Mater Pulchrae Dilectionis, Mother of Fair Love, the Love of all loves. In your heart, my daughters, there can be no other love than this. Our Lord will not allow you to share your heart with anyone else. If we give our whole heart to Jesus Christ, he will grant us the grace to treat all souls with charity, with immense affection, and for you to do so especially with your sisters, without particular friendships or silly behaviour that makes you ashamed. Charity without boundaries! What holy and unselfish affection we can and should have for one another! But for this to be so, we must belong to God. Jesus, only You! Tell him so really and truly, from the bottom of your heart. I tell him so, and I’m not embarrassed to repeat it aloud. Jesus, only You! I want to be only Yours, 33 because You are the Love of my loves, because You have singled me out with special love, because You have raised me up from the earth in spite of my being pauper servus et humilis, a poor and humble servant. Our Father was presenting us a whole programme of life during that time which, as he had also told us, he would have liked to spend with us in the oratory. My daughters, many virtues are required of us by Opus Dei: marvellous virtues, always based on many little things. We have to love poverty, with the common sense of a mother of a large and poor family, spending little but keeping everywhere pleasant, and being personally free from the desire for comfort or possessing things as if they were our own. I always tell you that we must love poverty and what poverty brings with it. And we must love God with a whole, clean heart: My daughters, a clean heart! We have already talked about that cry that has to come from the most intimate part of our soul: Mater Pulchrae Dilectionis, ora pro nobis! Have you noticed that a light is lit in the Galleria della Madonna, and that I have had them put an inscription and a date: to ask the Virgin, our Mother, to give my daughters a clean heart, better and richer than gold set with precious stones. A heart that should be totally and exclusively for God. In fact, many of us remembered those circumstances. We knew that one day in July 1958 our Father had been praying in the Basilica of St Mary Major, and on his return he asked that some words should be added above the glass frame of the Galleria de la Madonna, in Villa Sacchetti, with that aspiration and a date: 14-VII-58. The inscription, carved in stone, was put in place that summer, while our Father was in London. On the afternoon our Founder returned to Rome (it was the 30th of September) he gave us a surprise which filled us with joy. We were on the stairs of the Montagnola, when suddenly we heard our Father’s voice in the soggiorno. Through the loudspeaker he was asking who was around. The speakers had been installed in some parts of the house (the ironing room, the soggiorno of Villa Tevere and in the Montagnola…) and our Father used them frequently for several years, always warning us in some way (by ringing a bell, for instance) so that we could know that he wanted to speak to us. We hurried to the sitting room, and greeted our Father. He asked us how we all were, if there was anyone who was ill, and also if we had done the Norms well. May God bless you, my daughters, he said at the end. A little later, the news went around that the Father had gone to see the inscription placed in the Galleria. He said something about the wrought iron stand of the candle that had been put there, and he lit it for the first time. Every time that you pass that way, our Father continued, without anyone noticing it, without stopping what you are doing, without stopping your conversation if you are with someone else, just give her an affectionate glance, saying an aspiration to yourself asking for that marvellous holy purity for all those of us who form part of this great family of Opus Dei.

Only one will We were coming to the end of this time with our Father when he mentioned one last idea, a consequence of the total and unconditional self-giving which he had earlier been urging us to have. When one loves, when one really loves, there is a very great desire to be like the person we love. You must be another Christ. You must tread where He trod, accompanying Him along all those paths usque ad mortem, right until death itself. Obey always, my daughters. However much it costs. Obey until you reach the Cross, even though for the moment Jesus has not asked you to sacrifice your life in this way, nor will he normally ask you to. The death that our Lord expects of you and of me is the death of our own will, of our own judgment, through total obedience, constantly, willingly, cheerfully; overcoming difficulties without being noticed. I sometimes receive calls from the Holy See. They ask me for the curriculum vitae of some brothers of yours. And then, thanks be to God, we can mention many good things about their university degrees, their professional work, their apostolic task… I usually laugh and tell those concerned, if I have them near me: Do you see how proud we men are? The biography of Jesus Christ Our Lord appears in the Holy Gospel in three words: erat subditus illis, he was obedient to them (and in Spanish with one – obedeció – he obeyed!). If only this can be said of you and me when we reach the end of our way, when the moment comes to answer God’s call: da mihi rationem (cf Luke 16:2). God will not be a Judge, but Jesus, because we will have loved him a lot, and he will forgive us a lot. Do you remember that owner who asked his administrator to give him an account of his work? Render me an account of how you have administered my grace, of how you have worked for me on earth. Then how fortunate we will be if our angel can cry out: she obeyed! Love obedience. When a person is proud, she finds it very difficult to obey, because she sees what is bad in others, and where there is nothing bad, without meaning to, she invents a shadow, or a stain, because she considers that only her soul is clean, the one that has light, who can count on having done good works; the others are hopeless cases. You, my daughter, if you have really given yourself, will feel a great joy in your heart when you contemplate so many good things in your sisters, so many good things. You will know how to overlook their defects, without giving them importance, overlooking them without appearing to do so, going unnoticed. If anything, you will help them with fraternal correction – which is your duty – in a kind, affectionate way. But, above all, you will admire so many virtues in the others. On the other hand, you will have to notice in yourself so many things that are not going well. And that will not make you feel sad, but will lead you to make a deep sincere act of contrition and to say to our Lord: nunc coepi! I will begin again!

By way of a summary Our Father had told us many things, and in the end he reminded us of the essential points: My daughters, the Father has wanted to talk to you for a little while. Later each one of you will meditate on her own what we have talked about. You will consider that we have a divine call, and that we are travelling along a heavenly path on earth; you will consider that the call and the way are there for us to become saints. Don’t forget that to be saints is to be assured not only of our future happiness but also of our earthly one and, of course, of our fidelity. Why do you sometimes worry and think: Will I persevere? Nobody knows that! Nobody. That is a mercy that God grants us, because if we knew that we would be saved, that we were going to be faithful until the end, we would be far too proud, and God rejects such people. Let us try to know ourselves well, so that our interior struggle is well founded; and then, let us abandon ourselves in God’s hands. Fulfilling our Norms and fighting hard we will gain Paradise, my daughters, because we will be simple, humble, effective, loving the little things, passing unnoticed, not doing anything peculiar, knowing that our Lord, ordinarily, does not ask us for orchids cultivated with special care by the gardener. He asks that during the whole day we gather the many small, humble, wild flowers that grow in the fields, and we will prepare a bouquet to offer to Him, who is our Love, and our All. Even so, every day – if we are not blinded by pride, we will be obliged to recite: pauper servus et humilis! Lord, today has not been a day of holiness. It is true that I have collected these flowers, which I present to You through your Mother’s hands, thinking about You, but… And you make resolutions for the following day. My daughters, this is what interior life is, this is what it means to be saints. Holiness is nothing else. Be convinced that it is so, daughters of my soul. Opus Dei is a large nursery for holiness. That is why we understand and are able to live with everyone, and we are not persons who form a party, because in our heart there is room for all creatures. Because we also want to help them go to Heaven, at the end of their time in this world. Do not forget these words of the Lord: elegit nos – who has chosen us, who has chosen you, my daughter

– ut essemus sancti in conspectus eius, so that we will be saints in his presence. With a cheerful sanctity, with a smiling mortification, with a sincerity full of light, and clarity, without being afraid of anything or anybody, because the Lord, who has called us for this great work, will never fail us, will not cease to accompany us and sustain us with his grace. More than half an hour had passed, and the get-together came to an end. The Father wanted the newcomers to come closer to the stage, and he talked with them for a few minutes. When leaving the Aula Magna we were quite silent; it seemed the right way to be. We nearly all went 35 to the oratory, before going back to work. There was much to be thankful for, and to tell our Lord. I then went back to the kitchen, to help with the dessert for lunchtime. “In the Administration, a lot of work well done, until lunchtime. At table there were small details of family life.” That is how the general tone in the house was after the get-together with the Father: and that is how it appeared in the diary, very to the point. The day continued with details characteristic of a great feast of the Work: Uncle Santiago sent a huge box of chocolates and sweets for the celebration. During the get-together, the Central Secretary joined the students of the Roman College, telling us things she remembered about Aunt Carmen: the last moments of her life on earth, her generous and continual collaboration with the most difficult and tiring work, her thousand details of affection. In the afternoon, a solemn Benediction of the Blessed Sacrament followed the meditation, when we sang the Te Deum and the Salve, for it was Saturday. It all helped as thanksgiving, which followed without interruption for the rest of that day.

1 & 14 February 1960

During the last years of the 1950s, our Founder encouraged us to prepare the future presence of the Work in new countries – Austria, Kenya and Japan – with intense prayer, sacrifice and cheerful offering of our work well done, and taking care of the little things, thus helping our sisters who were going to plough the ground. The prehistory of Austria had already started with our Father’s journeys to that country, and it was strengthened when, on the 4th of December of 1955, he invoked the Most Holy Virgin for the first time with an aspiration he told us about immediately. Regarding the work in Eastern Europe and beyond, please petition our Mother, and Jesus Christ, through His Mother’s Heart, Sancta Maria, Stella Orientis, filios tuos adiuva? Later, he urged us to pray for Japan, placing on our lips another aspiration of confident piety: Sancta Maria, Stella Maris, filios tuos adiuva! Following his indications, this had been engraved under the stone triptych dedicated to our Lady, at the entrance of the Aula in Villa Tevere, which was then under construction. Our Father also enticed us in practical ways to keep those intentions very much in mind and to inspire us to go to those places if needed. So, for example, when don José Luis Múzquiz came back from a visit to Japan, our Father asked him to tell us about the work awaiting us there, where millions of souls have never heard about the true God. He also gave us an idea of their customs and lifestyle. Those countries were a frequent theme of conversation in the get-togethers. Someone got hold of some travel books describing them, so we could begin to learn about their geography and history. We were happy to think that soon the seed of Opus Dei was to be extended to new continents. We started the New Year, 1960, with midnight Mass in the oratory of Pentecost. In fact, our Founder’s wish was that, while he celebrated the Holy Sacrifice for his sons for the first time in the oratory of Our Lady of Peace (it had recently been finished) don Alvaro would celebrate do so for his daughters. During the meditation before Mass, don Alvaro spoke to us about what the Father had just asked him: that we should consider what the situation was like in the world twenty centuries ago, and compare it with the present day; and on realising that today, as then, so many hearts are cold, we should make the resolution to receive our Lord with great love, offering the fire of our self-giving, of our fidelity or whatever he was asking of each one of us. I remember the background of fireworks, typical of a Roman New Year’s Eve which, as our Father had taught us so well, did not affect our dialogue with God. Don Alvaro encouraged us to make concrete resolutions so that the New Year would begin under the sign of humility. The first day of January, our Founder came to wish us a happy New Year. He knew that on feast days the work in the Administration increases, so he decided to come and see us with don Alvaro in the different areas of the house: kitchen, servery, ironing, telephones… We were so happy to be with the Father that it was difficult not to leave what we were doing and follow him where he was going, but noticing this, he said: Don’t move, any of you: I will see you all, and in that way the work will not be interrupted. Among the comments he made, he presented wonderful prospects for the year: This year is going to be very effective, marvellous, if we are humble, if we are ready to be faithful in everything the Lord asks of us. On the 9th the last stone of the buildings of Villa Tevere was put in place. We had the good fortune to attend solemn Benediction of the Blessed Sacrament, which was celebrated by our Founder in the oratory of Pentecost, and to be united to his thanksgiving for this new mercy of the Lord, by singing the Te Deum. The difficult period of looking for the wherewithal for the construction of the central offices was coming to an end, and already our Father, with his characteristic magnanimity, was going ahead with new 37 projects. The previous June, classes had been suspended in the Roman College of Holy Mary, in anticipation of the completion of Villa delle Rose, its new site in Castelgandolfo, which needed to be remodelled. Our Father felt the need for many of his daughters from all over the world to receive a more intense formation, and it was not possible to continue in Villa Sacchetti, where they had been until then. Just before the end of 1959 our Father had given his daughters a water-colour of the plans for Villa delle Rose. Those days, he talked to us about that project a number of times. Soon, sisters of yours from Africa and Japan will be coming here, he said, full of faith and certainty. And he added that it was the responsibility of each and every one of the regions to contribute effectively to bear the weight of the new projects. The work in the Administration and in the printing press (which our sisters had been running for a number of years) had increased. The expansion of the apostolates of the Work all over the world required more people in the offices of the Central Advisory… A number of us who were in the Roman College at that time had the good fortune to stay and help with those new tasks. It was precisely in October 1959 that our Father appointed the first officials of the Central Advisory.

In the Aula Magna The 1st of February we celebrated the birthday of the first Portuguese Assistant Numerary, who is now in Heaven. Our Founder, as was his custom with all his daughters and sons who he had near him, had remembered her by name, particularly on that day. But what she or nobody could have imagined was the marvellous surprise that awaited us in the get-together. We had heard that it would be half an hour earlier than usual, and that we would see slides of Kenya and Japan. We headed for the Aula Magna quietly; as we approached the Scala Magna we went a little faster, because our Father was going to be with us, and had already arrived, with don Alvaro and don Javier. As we arrived, we sat down in the front rows of seating. Before beginning, our Father went up o the stage. With his habitual good humour, he made us realise affectionately how casually we had entered, and then commented: I’d like to give you a little bit of my fire…! He explained that this get-together had a dual purpose: on the one hand, to learn something about the two nations we were soon going to work in; and, as if thinking aloud, he added: We will go in May, the month of our Lady. Do you like the idea? A loud Si Padre! greeted his words. It was a small detail that showed his love for the Mother of God: he wanted to offer her that present, in the month dedicated specially to honour her. Although eventually it had to be postponed until the first days of June, no doubt Our Lady was equally happy with that plan, which our Founder had prepared so eagerly. He then mentioned the second object of that get-together: My daughters, you have to pray a lot for those two countries, where there are many souls to be saved. It will be hard work, and I want –and the Lord needs! – those daughters of mine who go there to be especially pious, strong, cheerful, and with a strong desire to work. We listened in silence; our Father then came down the steps of the stage and went to the row of seats at the back where don Alvaro and don Javier were sitting. The lights went out and the screen was lit. The Father began to explain the scenes as they appeared; but he very soon stopped, because when we heard his voice we all instinctively turned our heads, so he said to don Alvaro: Let’s go up to the front! We quickly took two armchairs from the Coso and placed them beside the screen, which immediately started to show scenes of the life and customs of Kenya: an African woman wearing a national dress and a big veil on her head; scenes of smiling faces, small towns, isolated huts, poorly inhabited fields, and, in contrast, views of the city of Nairobi, with well-built streets and modern buildings. Your brothers tell us that it is a beautiful city, our Father remarked. They showed us different slides; the Father’s amusing yet supernatural comments made us laugh, at the same time helping us to discover the apostolic panorama opening up on that continent. We were most enthused. At some point he said: Don’t forget, those of you who are going there to start the Work, the many things you will have to give up, little details to offer up to our Lord, and some little less agreeable things; but loving God and souls, you will know how to put up with them smartly and cheerfully. When a group of girls appeared on the screen, our Father explained that they would soon start an interracial school. It will be marvellous, also because of the variety of colour, he concluded, referring to the different ethnic groups there. From Kenya we went to Japan: streets and buildings with an oriental look; a church dedicated to the Most Holy Virgin in Nagasaki; groups of students at the University of Tokyo… Our Father made us laugh when he said that one of the ecclesiastical authorities of Osaka wanted the Work to be established in Japan so much, that he had asked a brother of yours to go to Japan precisely when the cherry trees were in blossom, thinking he would get a good impression of the country and would talk to our Father favourably about Japan… At some point some Japanese families appeared who – our Father said – had already made contact with priests of the Work. A girl appeared in one photograph, and our Father said that she could whistle, and he concluded: Now you have a specific person to pray for. With those photos we also learned about some oriental habits, and our Father explained that as we do not go to those countries as foreigners but to become like the people there, his daughters would be happy to learn how to sit on the floor, as they do in Japan, and eat with chop-sticks... The streets we saw were full of people; our Founder, with his immense interest in souls, a consequence of his love for God, had us consider the millions of persons in that country, and how few knew God. Our mission, he commented was precisely to make Him known. Unfortunately, the slides came to an end. Our Father asked if we had liked them. Our unanimous, positive answer left him in no doubt. My daughters, I envy you…, he commented. You are young, and what a great work for souls you can do throughout the whole world! And immediately, in a lower voice, he added: Also from here you can move souls too. Before he left, we had a fresh example of his great capacity to notice small details in all circumstances: when he passed the door of the Aula Magna he turned around and told us that perhaps it was not necessary to switch on so many lights, referring to those in the ceiling, because the side-lights gave sufficient light. Those moments left us feeling the get-together had been very short. Really, how pleasant our Father made those periods of family life, in which he spoke about the divine and the human with such naturalness! And, above all, how much closer those moments brought us to God. One of those present wrote down everything, and she concluded: We gave thanks to God for having had the opportunity of seeing the Father during that time of family life; to appreciate his good humour and, above all, the great love for souls and the universal heart God has given him.

A Great Day The days passed by very quickly; everything revolved around the coming 14th of February, the date on which it would be thirty years since women became part of the life of the Work, by explicit divine will, in the intimacy of our Father’s soul. The evening before that anniversary, I was helping to clear the servery after finishing my work in the office, when the good news spread throughout the house: the following day the Father would celebrate Mass in the oratory of Pentecost, and his daughters could attend. The homily, given with indescribable force and joy, expressed his happiness on seeing the expansion of the apostolates of the Work. My daughters, I want to put my heart more into today’s greetings, than on other occasions. Because quite soon we are going to Japan, we are going to Kenya to seek souls for Jesus Christ. And I am proud, I am very happy, and I give thanks because I see this apostolic readiness in all my daughters. They want to go. To go far. To take that joy they have in their heart, to India, to Japan, to Africa … to wherever. Turning to the Tabernacle, our Founder went on:

As a demonstration of our gratitude, Lord, a good handful of my daughters are going. They are going gladly, with joy, with enthusiasm. Faithful, faithful, with an unshakeable fidelity. When the Mass had ended, some of us went to the ironing room, perhaps expecting something. A few minutes later we heard don Alvaro’s voice saying that our Father wanted to see us for a few minutes. The news spread quickly, and we all went to the ironing room. Our Father came very soon; he went to the end of the ironing room, and went briskly up the stairs, to the little vestibule we call La Rotonda. He leaned on the banister and, not seeing don Alvaro, asked: Alvaro, my son, where are you? “Father, I have been caught by the tide...” was his good humoured answer, from the door of the ironing room. In fact, in our eagerness to see the Father, as soon as he came in we had completely blocked the path behind him, without realising that don Alvaro was also coming. Let don Alvaro come up, my daughters, the Father said smiling At the same time, he moved the piano stool a little, inviting don Alvaro to sit down; he was convalescing those days. It was an expression of affection and consideration, typical of someone who was always aware of the smallest details of the people around him. Our Father asked for one of our sisters, and said she would go back to work in Italy, her Region, after having spent these years in Villa Sacchetti. But we do not say goodbye, our Father continued, because we are never separated. We are consummati in unum. What is more, my daughter, every time you come to Rome, come to see us, he added with affection. He then began to read the names of those sisters of ours who were going to Japan, Kenya and Austria. Where is...? he said when naming those who were going to Japan. When he saw another, who was going to Austria, he commented, amusingly: You, my daughter, can begin to dance a waltz… Our Father was very happy, and so were we, seeing clear signs of the new stage of expansion of the Work to those countries where his daughters were going to start in a stable way. They are not going on their own, he commented later: they are accompanied by the prayer of the whole Work. And he added that, humanly speaking, the task awaiting them was a very beautiful adventure; and they were going to find souls who were eager to know Christ. He also told us that those who were going to those countries from their Regions would first come to Rome for a special seminar. He asked us to pray a lot so that this preparation would be effective, and to keep in mind the work in those places. It was a few minutes before twelve when our Founder left the ironing room. The deep gratitude for what we had just experienced was accompanied by the supernatural and human joy of seeing again a number of those who had lived in Rome, and would attend the seminar here before going to their new countries. As we had been asked by our Father, our prayer from that moment on was more intense, and the petition for each one of those who would start in those regions could now be replaced by their actual names. When, after the 12th of June that year, letters began to arrive with news about the beginning and the development of the apostolic work, I understood very well what the Founder was constantly and untiringly saying: In Opus Dei everything has always worked out as the result of prayer. That is our only weapon. Thirty years after the events described here, we give thanks to God for the marvellous reality of the desires that were then included in the pages of our internal publications: Soon – the months and the years go by very quickly – Africans, Japanese, Indians and Australians will also be daughters of the Father.

14 February 1960 (Men)

That year the feast fell on a Sunday. Naturally, we expected to have a get-together with our Father, although we did not know what time it would be. For years it used to be after lunch; and those of us who ate at the second sitting tried to be quick so that we could go to the soggiorno as soon as possible. However, it was not uncommon for our Father to come down to the sitting room in the morning and find some students listening to classical music. The records were chosen and given an order, usually by Ramon. The programme was left on a tray placed on top of the console. Then a door was left open which linked the Cortile Vecchio with the Galleria della Campana. Most of us were still having our breakfast, when we were told that the Father was in the soggiorno with don Alvaro. He saw us come in, one by one, in a hurry, recovering from our haste just in time to kiss his hand and greet him for the family celebration of the 30th anniversary of the foundation of the Women’s Section, and the 17th of the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross. Invariably, the Father answered everyone: Auguri e figli maschi! I was surprised. It was my first year in Rome, and I had never heard that expression before: it can be translated literally as: Greetings! and male children! which is the Italian way of greeting newly-weds, that God may bless them with many sons. Are you all here? I felt that our Father was a little eager to start yet wanted to make sure no one was missing, as he wanted to tell us something which was very much in his heart. We have no choice but to be proselytistic. You cannot be satisfied if each of you does not win at least one vocation every year. When our zeal for proselytism stops vibrating, it’s as if the heartbeat of the interior life has stopped. I dispense nobody in Opus Dei from the duty of seeking vocations; neither those over fifty, nor those over forty, who are already many in the Work. I insist that there is no reason why a person over eighty should not be admitted as an Associate, if he meets the necessary conditions; as for becoming a Supernumerary… If we weren’t concerned for proselytism, it would seem as if we weren’t happy. A person who is happy, in possession of something good, tries to pass his happiness on to others… Dealing with secular priests and religious – though our calling is very different – I have come across people who have not done anything to look for vocations for their seminary or their institution, and it has made me very sad: I have seen them unhappy; it seemed to me as if they felt they had failed in their personal life. These clear words led to a personal examination of conscience and, at the same time, they raised our sights and encouraged us to say Yes. The Father continued: We all have to seek vocations. In so many places, your brothers are in the breach; and here too, we are in the breach, helping their work with our prayer and mortification, with our self-giving in our daily duties. Don’t think that their apostolate or yours is easier or more difficult; it depends on each one, on the spirit of sacrifice we place in what the Work asks of us. In any case, not a year must go by without each one bringing at least one vocation to Opus Dei. My sons, if I say this to you it is not because things are going badly; on the contrary, precisely because the Work is growing, we need to be many more. Furthermore, now that we have won the battle of formation, we find ourselves already in a good position to form new vocations very well, without any hurry. When you leave here, you will have to help with that work, but I tell you: Do not forget that you yourselves will continue to need formation, and that you will have to continue fighting personally, as I have to. What do you think? Without personal effort, without struggle, this service of ours would be of no use… And we would even be like sacks of sand. 41

We have to fight always! Sometimes we will be defeated; but if you have a sense of being sons of God, it will be in small things, not something substantial, something of less importance: small faults of love, which are remedied with an act of contrition. If you are going along the proper path of Opus Dei, your struggle will normally be in points which are far from the basic issues of our life. Our Father was silent for a moment, and then went back to what he had spoken of at the start of the get-together: Not to be proselytistic would be a symptom of laziness, of sickness, and perhaps of death. Proselytistic zeal! Set those around you on fire! And also those far from you, with your prayer, your sacrifice, and the smiling fulfilment of your duty. I receive your auguri, your good wishes with pleasure, with the tenderness of a father and a mother, so long as they are accompanied by the desire for many vocations: with deeds, of course. .As they say in Italy: Auguri e figli maschi! Now, each of you, put your hand on your heart; meditate on what I have just said to you, and make the necessary personal resolutions to do your duty better. I ask my sons and daughters who receive Confidences, for the love of God, to ask your brothers or sisters every week in the chat about their proselytism, no matter whether they are sixteen years old or ninety. I want it to be clear that if there are no vocations, what’s lacking is love of God! All our eyes were fixed on our Father. And his eyes also looked at us one by one, quickly, while he talked. Then, with that characteristically optimistic tone of voice of his, he started to comment on some immediate projects of the work in Africa, in Oceania (the first people to go to Australia would be leaving soon), and Asia. He ended by saying: All this is a lesson in proselytism: as a doctrine, as a duty, and as to its accomplishment, to its being effective… with tangible deeds! Then, changing the topic, he mentioned some details of the foundations of the Women’s Section and of the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross: circumstances which the Father has recently explained to us in greater detail, and where the loving Providence of God and the unconditional fidelity of our Founder can very clearly be seen. The week before, Warren had been ill, but he had now completely recovered. Our Father invited him to sit by his side, while he affectionately said to him: Don’t worry, at your age!... you will soon be stronger than before. And, referring to the matter of personal struggle, our Father continued: Don’t convert a mortification, or saying a prayer which you don’t have to say, into a fixed custom; that is to say, something which does not form part of our plan of life that God wanted: such as the small sacrifice of not eating what you like, or always saying the prayers that you learned as a child. From time to time, with complete freedom, it is good to interrupt those customs; if not, we run the risk of creating scruples of conscience, considering them an obligation. For instance, I usually say the prayers I learnt as a child; but at times I substitute them for other acts of love of God; then, after a time, I return to those childhood prayers, which are the ones I nearly always recommend when I am asked for a vocal prayer. In any case, never stop raising your heart to God, making an act of love or atonement or thanksgiving, very often accompanied by a good resolution… Have you nothing to say? Do I have to speak all the time? In fact, on other occasions we would tell our Father stories, news, jokes as well, or we would sing something. But that morning nobody did, although later on, in the afternoon get-together, we did. Before leaving, our Father went back to what he had said at the beginning. Vocations, my sons! What is foremost in your mind? There are so many good souls in the world who are waiting for us! Many vocations, thanks be to God, come to the Work; but we need more and more, more all the time. We have to move, we have to break the crust of indifference, we cannot remain passive. As for me, why should I get involved in the lives of others? Because, as a Christian, I have the obligation to do so! Because Christ has got into your life, and mine! As he got into the lives of Peter and Paul, into the lives of John and Andrew... And the Apostles learned to do the same. Otherwise, after having received the Master’s express command: Go and preach..., they wouldn’t have got moving, and they would have remained just Twelve; there would be no Church.

My sons, I personally, with the grace of God, have allowed myself to be slighted many times; but I do not permit that my rights and my duty to proselytise should be slighted. We cannot be at peace, we cannot sleep in peace, if we are not able to do this: that our right to make Jesus Christ known should be respected! Nearly immediately afterwards, our Father got up and went towards the door, followed by don Alvaro. Peter was there. He had made sure to get there first, to be greeted again by the Father, and he managed it. Our Father stopped for a moment, looked at him smiling and threatening him kindly with his hand, said to him; Oh you English! You have great hearts, but you hide the fact… In England you must not respect that false modesty… Get rid of it!

25 July 1961

In Santiago de Compostela it is said that the feast of the Apostle is the hottest day of the year. I don’t know how true this is, but on the eve of 25 July 1961 a very hot day was forecast with no clouds in the sky, and where the sun reigned supreme. In La Estila we were studying a summer semester, and that afternoon we were told that the classes would be shorter. We rehearsed some ceremonies, for the following day was a feast and the Bishop of Angers was coming to the hall of residence to celebrate Mass. The prelate was leading a pilgrimage from his diocese to the Apostle’s tomb, and early on the 25th itself they would begin their return journey. For that reason, we had to bring the time of the Mass forward, so the feast of St James began by getting up very early. Coming out of the oratory, when the bishop had already left, don Tomás gathered us together in the sitting room: “It would be good to have breakfast and finish tidying up the bedrooms quickly and stay up here… The Father is coming in twenty minutes’ time.” I thought I had not heard properly: the Father? don Tomás said it again, but even so it took us time to react, such was the surprise. Then, there was such an explosion of joy that, as someone commented, the very foundations of the house shook, The Father in La Estila! It seemed incredible. We had never tidied up the bedrooms nor had breakfast so fast. Any noise in the street that could have been a car paralysed everyone as if by a charm. Then, realising it was a false alarm, we took up our tasks again with greater determination. Around ten o’clock, a car stopped at the entrance. We all hurried to the street.
