Smiling, the Father entered la Estila. It was true! With him was don Alvaro. We gathered in the activities room. The chairs formed a large square. The Father arrived soon after. He sat near the centre on one of the sides. Quite a lot of us sat on the floor, very close to him. Look at how things turn out, my sons: I had just arrived in London, when we were told that it would be good to come here. Don Alvaro and I took a plane to Biarritz, where we were met by car… and here I am! Do you know that you have some very nice English brothers? I have come to see you because I have the heart of a father and a mother. The Work has grown a lot, activities have extended all over the world, and vocations are abundant. The Father is no longer young, and it is probable that I will not see quite a lot of you again. You will be scattered throughout the five continents. What a marvellous adventure you are going to have, if you are faithful! The Father was talking with that characteristically affectionate tone of his and, truth be told, we were very moved. That is why he immediately changed the whole tone of the get-together. Well! Don’t stay there so quiet! Ask me something! But I warn you, I will only answer the questions that are impertinent. Father, in the Catechism of the Work you say that we have to live divine filiation… It was don Francisco who asked the question. It was perhaps a couple of years since he had left the Roman College, and the Father showed his joy at seeing him again. Then he said jokingly: I am going to give you a big beating! Here you go again with your impertinent questions… But of course! All Christians are children of God, but in Opus Dei we have a special motive for living that divine filiation. You will know why some day. When you study the Catechism of the Work, do not forget that you have the particular Law of Opus Dei in your hands, explained clearly and precisely, with its question and answer structure, accessible to all who form part of our family. Learn it fondly, taking to your prayer those points where the spirit, sculpted by God, has been given me for his Work. Father, how is the special intention going?

Continue keeping it in mind. One condition for the efficacy of prayer is perseverance, which, where I come from, we also call stubbornness. Things work out after many years of praying. He then told us the story of the statuette of Our Lady of Pilar that had recently been rediscovered, and which was a testimony to his prayer many years before founding the Work. Father, which are the next countries the Work will go to? We’ll soon be going to Australia and Paraguay. And the new ordinations? If we were to add a zero to the number of those going to be ordained this year as priests, they would still be few for the needs of the Work (…). Pray that there will soon be many, or rather, those that are needed; cheerful, pious, learned, and with a heart as big as this, because the priests are ordained to serve their brothers, not to be served. As can be seen, the questions were on different topics. As the Father answered them, he joked with each one… He placed his hand on Fernando’s head and ruffled his hair. Then he kept looking at him in amusement, and, after saying a “no” of disapproval with his head, he took out a comb from his pocket and offered it to him so he could comb his hair again. Father, how many editions of Camino have been printed now? My son, the Father cannot keep track of all those small things… I know that they will soon reach a million copies; but what I can say is that when I wrote it, it didn’t enter my head that it would spread so much. I thought that it would reach a few people, just a few more than those of our family. But the Lord wanted that little book to be an instrument to move souls. Father, Father… Let us see what this son wants so urgently. What do we have to do to keep the spirit of the Work intact? The only solution is to be saints, taking advantage of the thousand and one little things of each day. For a person who has acquired the habit of doing things well, the struggle comes naturally. It is a matter, for instance, of lending a hand to help those who don’t manage, gently, without them noticing. Generally it can be said that you keep the spirit of the Work intact if you live it well: if you are very faithful, very cheerful, very pious, very sincere, working and living your professional vocation well. When I was about to leave Rome, I dictated a reflection to be published in the next issue of the internal magazines. It says this: “One specific characteristic of the spirituality of Opus Dei is that each one has to sanctify his profession. Those sons of mine who fulfil that programme will not lose my spirit, which is what God has entrusted to me. There is no room in the Work for lazy people: we are all hard workers; that is why I cannot understand how there can be someone who has time to spare.” For the get-together Pepe was wearing his lieutenant’s uniform. The Father looked at him for a moment, and said: Well General, what are you going to ask me? Pepe reacted immediately, telling the Father that a number of officers had whistled. Very good, my son. Lots of them must whistle. Military people usually have a good preparation for God’s call, because their profession requires many human virtues. Sometimes the seed takes root later, perhaps after a life full of weaknesses. Their sense of loyalty awakens in them a great desire to make reparation, and they give themselves with colossal energy. I am very glad; but it is more beautiful to give oneself in one’s prime. Father, why do you love donkeys so much? If you open the book of Psalms, you will find the answer: ut iumentum factus sum apud te, et ego semper tecum. Tenuisti manum dexteram meam, et in voluntate tua deduxisti me, et cum gloria suscepisti me. As a donkey I am close to You and you have taken me by the bridle – this is a free translation – to do Your will, and afterwards you will lead me to glory, to Love. Don’t you think this is a good reason? In short, we are madly in love, with a great Love that has moved us to say No to the little loves on earth. There were other questions, but the get-together soon came to an end. When the Father got up, we felt that we had experienced something unrepeatable. Paco was going to Brazil, and as we were leaving the room he came forward to tell the Father something that he had not managed to say during the get-together. Father, do you want me to say something to the people in Brazil on your behalf? Yes, my son, that they be very happy, because the work there will turn out marvellously. He gave him some personal advice, and then, leaning his arms heavily on his shoulders, he added: My son, I lean on you, and Jesus Christ together with me. The Father left us for the time being. We did not know what would happen next, nor did we dare have expectations. We kept in mind the work he had come to do, very united to his person, and it was then time for lunch.

More surprises In the afternoon we saw our Father again. My sons, I am very tired and I don’t know what to tell you. Why don’t you ask questions again? Father can you tell us something about Isidoro? He was an ordinary young fellow in the Work, one more. He didn’t have great talents, but he made up for that by working a lot. What? Has your idea of Isidoro come down a few pegs? Well, lift them up again, because holiness in the Work is like that. Isidoro was very obedient, he fulfilled the Norms. He became a saint because of this. His dedicated life was noticeable, and holiness is self-surrender. Father, why do you talk to us so much about freedom? It’s a very important topic now, because some people are bent on denying our freedom. You have to tell the truth everywhere: that you are free, professionally, socially, and politically…, with the same freedom as other Catholics, who are just like us. Father, what must we do with our defects? Ask about this in Confession or in the fraternal chat. In any case, we have to fight against each one.., although sometimes we don’t want to. But you and I are going to make a greater effort from now on, and in that way there will be two silly fellows fewer in this world. And what do we have to pray for more in the prayer? That’s easy: pray about the things that occupy you: neither the Father nor anyone in the Work should have worries;2 so, whatever is taking place, what the Father has in his head and in his heart. Father, how can we be responsible? We are responsible in the measure that we act freely. Freedom and responsibility are two parallel lines which always have to be of the same length. Our Father asked someone next to him for his ring. He put it on quickly, and immediately he received many others, which he placed on all the fingers of his hands. Afterwards, showing off these precious jewels, he told us: I am strong through you. For me, you are my protection, my heart, my head before God, and my glory and my crown…, sometimes of thorns, he added smiling. Father, how can we be effective? Without personal holiness – that is, without fighting to become saints – there can be no true effectiveness: forget about yourself and grow in affection for the others for God. The one who advances along the way of holiness does not think about himself; that is why he doesn’t even notice how effective he is. The questions kept coming, and the Father answered briefly, with that capacity he had to condense in few words the criterion that best corresponded with the spirit of the Work. Father, what does prima, piu, meglio mean? Don Alvaro, who until then had remained quiet, translated immediately: “Sooner, more, better.” Do your prayer! And this is not pride… When you have a difficult task, you put yourself in the presence of God, and you say to him: Lord, I leave it in your hands. And in this way it will come out, sooner, more and better. Do you see that they are words that grow out of a humility that trusts in God? You can be sure

2 Ed. In Spanish: a play on words: Ocupación refers to one’s work, what one has in hand; preocupación means worry. Often people are doing something but do not do it as well as possible because they are worrying about something else which distracts them.

that this thing… whatever it may be… will work out! Father, but doesn’t that phrase come from some event in the history of the Work? It has to do with your own history…Pray and you will see! Father, and how can we know if we are truly faithful? Ask your Directors if they are happy with you… And, laughing, the Father added: You can be sure, they will answer: Er…Average! The unity of the Work, the importance of internal studies, devotion to our Lady… The questions came up as soon as the Father stopped speaking. A somewhat longer moment of silence was broken as one of those who was close by slowly asked: Father, which is your dearest memory in the history of the Work as Father? The Father smiled and kept quiet. It seemed the smile was going to be his only answer. Then he looked at all of us and exclaimed: You! I have called you before gaudium meum et corona mea… The silence lasted a little longer. We understood that many things must have passed through our Father’s head, and that once again he reminded us of what he had always said: that his greatest joy was the happiness of his children. Father, how should we consider the older people of the Work? You owe them an enormous debt. Nowadays perhaps you may have some personal difficulty – mostly without any real foundation – but in those days they needed a lot of faith in God and in a poor young priest about whom all sorts of atrocities were being said; even that he was mad. Do you remember, Alvaro? Why don’t you tell these lads something? Don Alvaro recalled the time the Father had taken refuge in an asylum in Madrid, during the war, to escape persecution from the communists. While there he had obtained a certificate saying he was insane, which he later showed to the priest who had publicly accused him of being mad, and who later became a great friend. Each one comes to the Work because he is called by God. Vocation is an exclusively divine reality. But those first brothers of yours helped me to open up the way; that is why you owe them a good part of the beauty of your vocation, and why you now find it easy to follow the divine call. The get-together was extending. The afternoon was quite hot, and it was noticeable. The Father saw that the priests were feeling the temperature, and he took off his collar, commenting: Very few people have seen me like this! Immediately, don Alvaro did the same, and then the other priests. In the interval, Paco began to strum the guitar. Father, what do you want me to sing? Whatever you want, my son. It was then Javier’s turn, who thought he should warn us about the type of songs he had in his repertoire. Father, they are nonsense. Well, if you sing some nonsense to amuse the others, I think that’s wonderful. The get-together finished shortly after. It was already time for tea.

A complete day In the dining room, while taking some refreshments we thought the day was over. Whatever else might happen, nothing would add to the importance of what had happened in La Estila, a corner of Spain where one had to make a point of going, for it is not on the way to anywhere… We could only hope for a blessing from the Father, before he started the return journey. But we were wrong, because we had yet another get-together. Incredible. And it was even longer! Again, it was a flow questions and short and direct answers of our Father. When someone does not see the way clearly, how much can we try to force him? Coercion, never. I have never coerced anyone. I have always respected the freedom of everyone and, in the first place, that of my sons. Ah! But if you were to see me going astray, you would do very badly if you 47 didn’t raise your voice to warn me, or if you didn’t give me a couple of slaps on realising that I had lost my sense of reality. Father, do we have to be astute? Yes, all the saints have been astute. What does the Work signify in the world? It is a way that God has opened on earth, a splendid way, with many different forms, a marvellous way, that can be for many souls, and that doesn’t hamper anyone. A clear way, where there is no discrimination; but, yes, certainly, with its own characteristics, such as sanctifying work. How can we be aggressive doing proselytism? The affection we have for souls is not aggressive. From the beginning of the Work, we have known how to understand and forgive everyone; to drown evil in an abundance of good. It is only God who judges… Our ‘aggression’ is charity, to want what is best for others, but without forcing them to go against their will. Even those who are determined to remain in error deserve our affection. As in previous get-togethers, the questions were very varied. Someone expressed his desire to go to Japan, or Russia, as soon as possible. Another wished to know more details about the history of the Work, asking when this or that intercessor had been invoked. Or in a more direct way: Father, how did the preparatory prayer for the meditation originate? It is a very common text that is prayed in many places. I modified it a little, especially adding: that you see me that you hear me; it was as if my soul needed it in those moments when I needed to affirm my faith. What do you do when you say that prayer? My Lord and my God: you put yourself in God’s presence. I firmly believe that you are here, that you see me that you hear me; you declare your faith with a statement that was born from my weakness. I adore you with profound reverence: is the appropriate greeting of a creature, adoration; it is all logical…, with a human and divine logic! I ask you for pardon of my sins: that is what I would tell you if I had offended you: it is noble and reasonable to ask for pardon; it is even more so with God! And grace to make this time of prayer fruitful: because we cannot fulfil anything that is worthy of eternal life, if we are lacking the grace from Heaven; and we want our prayer to be supernatural, agreeable to God. Afterwards come the recommendations, which make up for our lack of titles: My Mother Immaculate, Saint Joseph my Father and Lord – I have a great devotion to him – my Guardian Angel intercede for me. How logical it all is! There were a number of questions also on the aspirations: ite ad Joseph; Mater Pulchrae Dilectionis; Sancta Maria, Spes Nostra, Sedes Sapientiae… The Father answered them repeating what he had commented on many times before. He stopped a bit more on one aspiration. Omnia in Bonum! What a joy to realise how true it is that everything that happens is for good! I left England, where I had hoped to work for many days, and got the benefit of being able to spend these moments with you. God sometimes takes away things, some things, that seem good, but which are not so in fact. And at times we perhaps can have the same reaction as the child whose mother does not allow him to play with a lit candle, because of the danger of setting the house on fire. Sometimes we don’t understand something, not even a word… And those who consider themselves wise, even less, because they are blinded by pride. I don’t like cry-babies, but don’t be afraid to shed tears sometimes, like a child who has been left without a toy. I have cried a lot in my life, but I have never lost my joy. Father, is sincerity important? Very, because if you are sincere and speak in time and clearly, it is certain you will reach the end of the way and will help those around you to complete the journey of their life joyfully. And if some day we think we are no use? That’s ridiculous! Am I of use for anything? The only thing I do is get in the way! The Pauline doctrine is fulfilled here too: what is sick, what is despicable, what is weak, that is what the Lord places as the keystone of the arch. When we despise ourselves because we think that we are no use for the vocation Christ has given us, such humility comes from the devil… and sometimes is simply pure laziness. Is it good to think about Heaven? Very often it is a need, and always an act of faith and hope, a good recourse when faced by something 48 that costs us an effort, because we know that Love awaits us in Heaven. And at the moment of death? I hope, my son that the Lord will give us the strength to go through it with dignity. In the Work we have always lived without fear of life or of death. Time flew. In spite of his evident tiredness, the Father did not leave a single question unanswered, adding at times brief comments and gestures, which amused us, enabling his words to leave his children at peace. In the end don Florencio mentioned that it was time to finish, and the Father, who moments earlier had shown no signs of being in a hurry, got up immediately. Very early on the 26th of July the Father celebrated Mass in the oratory of El Pedroso, the conference centre next door, and don Alvaro did so in La Estila. Only a few people were present. By the time the rest of us were getting up the Father had already left by car. The morning was dark and overcast, while the continuous drizzle – the oldest inhabitant of Santiago – was causing the cobblestones to gleam.

3 December 1961

That Sunday, 3rd December 1961, was the first of Advent. In the get-together we had lit the first candle of the Adventskranze, a sign that there was less than a month to go before Christmas. During the week, there had been some events related to family life. On Wednesday, for instance, we had the Brief Circle and celebrated Emilio’s birthday; while on Thursday 30th we celebrated the saint’s day of Andrés, and we had the vigil with the Blessed Sacrament exposed. I remember that around that time an issue of Obras had come out; we read a little from it in one of the get-togethers. We learned, among other things, a few details of the then Estudio General de Navarra, and the student Residence in Amsterdam. The morning of 3rd December had gone by like many other Sundays of the year: study, work, sport… The get-together of those who ate at the second sitting had only just begun, when our Father came to the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo accompanied by don Alvaro. He wanted to have a get-together with us. Very quickly, as soon as the rest of the people found out, they arrived. Before beginning, our Founder addressed Fernando, a jet pilot: My son, here you can go faster than by plane: it is all a matter of wanting to. And while the last of the students of the Roman College were still coming in, he commented: Now only a few of us are left. Before, there were two hundred here, and now you are eighty. But in three or four years we will get back to the full number: we need to. It was a rather long get-together. The Father talked about a number of things. He started with a reference to the expansion of the apostolic activity of the Work all over the world. At the end of December, a group of brothers of yours will go to Paraguay, he told us. Now, in South America the only place left to start is only Bolivia, although we already have a group of Bolivians, who are receiving formation in Pamplona. And next year – for a number of reasons the initial attempt has been delayed – we will go to Australia. And when his eyes alighted on two brothers of ours, he changed the topic and said affectionately: You don’t know how much I have prayed for you because, for reasons we don’t need to mention, both of you are especially in my heart. As neither of the two said anything in return, he amusingly referred to the reputation Basques have for not showing their feelings. If someone were to say this to me it would make me emotional, because I have a heart; but these Basques… Blessed be God, who keeps my heart alert, praying always, every time more, for all of you. He turned to someone next to him. Where are you from? From Lerida, Father. It was there that I took my examination of the first year of Bachillerato, in a building that was threatening to collapse; it was all propped up at the time. I was eleven years old then; I am now sixty. I expect they have fixed it by now. Throughout the get-together, our Founder talked about fidelity to our vocation, and told us to pray for it for everyone in the Work. Do you ask the Lord for my perseverance, so that I may serve him joyfully and happily, so that I may think only about him and not about myself? And without waiting for an answer, he continued: Our service is in laetitia, forgetting about ourselves. We invent ninety-nine percent of our problems: they are a lie, fibs which we let grow, reasons we invent that are not reasonable but are used to hide our concupiscence. If you want to be happy, forget about yourselves, and dedicate yourselves to the service of others, for God’s sake. If you live like that, when the moment comes to make the examination of conscience – which you must never omit – you can say: Lord, I have not thought about myself, I have not remembered myself all day! You will then go straight to your goal. I’m now in the thirty-fourth year of my vocation to Opus Dei. The greatest joy of my life, a great, great joy – and you are a part of it – is faithfulness to my vocation: in cold and in heat, feeling like it and not feeling like it, in bad weather and calm. Faithful to my vocation, with God’s grace and the help of my heavenly Mother. You too can rely on God’s help. Don’t let me down! Help one another to persevere and be saints. On our own we’re weak; all united we’re strong. I need you: each of you is my strength, to the point where sometimes, quite sincerely, I go to the prayer and say to our Lord, “Lord, I ought to be lying face down on the ground like a worm in your presence, with my mouth stuck to the ground; but look at my sons, how faithful they are! How much they love you! How they surrender their youth to you!” Each one of you should do the same, realising you are never alone, but can depend on the strength of everyone else: which is one more reason to be faithful. Faithfulness is, at the same time, easy and difficult. But we have to struggle every moment. God is content with that. Listening to our Founder’s words, how easy it was to formulate resolutions of fidelity! During the get-together there were hardly any questions. Our Father, little by little, was sharing with us the ideas he had in his heart. And a good part of the time was dedicated to commenting on the Gospel account of the resurrection of Lazarus. First, he had us consider that our dealings with Christ had to be ones of friendship, such as the disciples had. My sons, the life of Christ, but specially those scenes of Bethany, are a call to friendship. Have you seen how affectionately and trustingly Christ’s friends addressed him? Lazarus’ sisters very naturally reproached him for his absence. “We sent you word! If only you’d been here!” Don’t you feel like saying, Lord Jesus, come and teach me how to treat you in the same friendly, loving way as Martha, Mary and Lazarus did, and as those first twelve did, who may have followed you, at first, for reasons that were not very supernatural. But with the coming of the Holy Spirit, they gave themselves even unto death. Our Father explained that our dealings with Jesus Christ have to be confident and persevering, as were those of the family of Bethany. It can happen sometimes: Lord, see, I am sick. Lord, you died on the Cross out of love for me: come and heal me. And it seems as though Jesus does not hear our prayer. Trust, my child, and persevere in calling upon that loving Heart. See what he tells his disciples: Lázarus, amícus noster, dormit (John 11:11): he sleeps, our friend is sleeping. Our Lord understands that we can fall asleep. He wants us to trust him. No matter what happens, he can bring us back to life. Our faithfulness, he told us, is forged in the struggle of every moment. Defeats do not count if we go to Christ, who is Life and the source of victory. But Christ needs our cooperation, our will to allow Him to act in us. Do you see? It seems as if there is no remedy. How many times I must have told you that our fidelity is forged in the battle of each moment, with our wretchedness, and our mistakes. Woe to the one who does not struggle! Because he is losing his interior life, and it is very difficult to bring a dead person back to life. But you have seen how the dead too come back to life, when they hear the voice of God: Lazare, veni foras! (John 11: 43). Sons of my soul, if earthly things do not move you, then let the voice of Christ move you. And consider that my words can be God’s voice at this moment: Lazare, veni foras! And at once, the man who had died came out. Do you see? Lazarus came back to life because he heard God’s voice, and he wanted to come out of that state. If he had not wanted to move, he would not have returned to life. And you? Do you want to? And now let’s make a resolution. Always have faith in God. Always hope in God. Always love God. He will never abandon us, even if we seem rotten. A man who is dead to grace can rise again with a little bit of good will, because God’s mercy is infinite. 51

Do you remember the son of the widow of Naim? Adulescens, tibi dico, surge! (Luke 17:14). Stand up! And he stood up. Christ continues working these miracles now, through you. How many times, in the name of the Lord, you have said to a friend of yours: tibi dico, surge! And he has got up from his lukewarmness, from his lethargy, from his sins! We can all get out; we can all get up… And if we don’t have enough faith, we can say with the Apostles: Adauge nobis fidem (Luke 17:5). Let us ask our Lord, now, that no one who comes to the Work may lack faith. We lose faith every time that we have a period of laxity, of laziness; when we abuse grace or are even in a hurry with the sacraments, and ill-treat holy things. Never doubt that you can bring back to the faith someone you know, or someone you don’t know, who is far away, even in the antipodes. I am praying for him this very moment, and asking for him the gift of faith. Our Father reminded us that we were at the beginning of Advent. Soon, he said, the Lord will be coming to earth. He asked us to pray continually, so that Christ could come truly to the hearts of all mankind. My sons, during these days when we are beginning the time of Advent, let us tell our Lord: Come, Jesus my Lord; come into my heart and into the hearts of all Christians; and come especially into the hearts of those who have at some time thought of giving themselves to you and then abandoned the idea, not out of bad will, but through weakness. Let us pray for the whole world, for all people of all races and tongues and religions; for all those who have a vague idea of religion, and for those who do not know the faith. And this zeal for souls, which is a genuine and clear proof that we love Jesus, will make Jesus come. Before finishing the Father wanted us to consider the infinite Love the Lord has for us, and the happiness which awaits us with a life given to his service. Don’t be surprised to see that you are weak, my sons. If I have my arms and my heart open for each one of you, not as a father but as a mother, think how the Heart and the arms of Christ will be. We have the right to consider this, because we follow him. We deny ourselves the attractions of the world, of the flesh, of concupiscence – which are what can rob us of our faith – because we are eager for Love with a capital L, for hope and faith. Virtues are not something theoretical; with the help of God, we try to practise them. At this very moment, while I talk and you listen without the noise of words, we are activating the three theological virtues. The Lord has wanted us for himself, completely for Himself. Love with a capital L awaits us. Think about it, if you want to, with your human heart. Consider that the whole of Wisdom, the whole of Love, all the Beauty, all the Goodness of God will be poured into each one of us, satisfying us without ever satiating us! That is what love will be: without any treachery, forever! My children don’t play the fool. What are you thinking about? Are we stupid? Pray so that all your brothers may understand this, and may know how to live in this way: out of love, through love, with love, in love and for love. We are lovers! Is that clear, my sons? When he had said this, our Father got up. He had left us abundant material to take to our prayer leading up to Christmas.

1 April 1962

On Saturday 31st March the Father’s retreat, made with the members of the General Council and the Regional Delegates who were in Rome during those days, came to an end. In the Roman College we were eagerly waiting to greet him for the recent anniversary of his priestly ordination. The following day, the Fourth Sunday of Lent, dawned with a clear blue sky. The good weather was inviting for a walk through the city. We were making plans in the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo, when I heard our Father’s voice, who at that moment was entering the room. Joy and surprise were reflected on our faces. We went to kiss his hand. But with a very affectionate gesture he did not let us. After listening to all our greetings, he sat down and we surrounded him immediately. The group grew as others came in. It was noticeable that the Father wished to refer to something special. I would like you already to keep very much in mind those who are to be ordained this year. They will be few; but the intensity of your prayer and your sacrifice, and the joy of your self-surrender, will help to make up for the fact that they are few in number. I would soon like to have around a hundred ordained each time. We have a great need for Numerary priests. Without priests we cannot progress. We need priests who possess our spirit, well prepared, cheerful, with a sporting spirit in life, who are ready to make sacrifices for their brothers, who know that all of us love them with our whole heart, as happens with the other members of Opus Dei. Pray that they be very cheerful and very holy: that they do not think about themselves (along our way there is no time for having personal worries) so that they only think of the glory of God and the good of souls. He then referred to the apostolic zeal we all have in the Work, by specific divine will, as a necessary and inseparable expression of our vocation. He had us consider how God has chosen us to do great things, if only we allow him to work through us. Our Lord, you must notice, makes use of something earthly to produce divine effects. We have read it in today’s Gospel. Jesus Christ, who could draw food out of nothing, wanted to use those loaves of bread that boy had so as to give something to eat to the crowd that followed him. Now he wants us to add something of our own: he wants us to cooperate and trust him. If we were to consider our own poor personal conditions, or were conditioned by a thousand external difficulties, it would be natural for us to become despondent. But as we do the best we can, and trust in God’s almighty power and only think about the good of souls, we go ahead joyfully. The Lord counts on us, although he does not need us for anything. That is why I tell you again that nobody is necessary in Opus Dei; but at the same time we are all needed. God, who in his providence has wished that men share in his Redemption, has made us co-redeemers. Demanding a total loving surrender from us, he helps us overcome the fact that we are very little, and makes us instruments of divine works. Each one of us is clay; but this earth, mixed with his grace, becomes an ointment capable of cleaning, and giving light to blind eyes. My children, with the light of your dedicated life, of your sanctified work, of your faithful perseverance, you will bring many souls to God, to the warmth of the Work. It was noticeable that our Father was not only speaking for that small group of us who were listening to him, but to all his children including those who would come later. Those of us who were by his side at that moment had the clear awareness of being links in a long chain, united by fraternal charity, entrusted with passing on to others the same pulsing enthusiasm we had received from him. We cannot be happy if we don’t make others partake in our joy. But never forget that the first manifestation of proselytism is to love each other a lot (without being silly), that you help each other to be faithful, so that we can say like those martyrs of Sebaste: forty of us have entered into battle, forty crowns we ask of you. If you really love each other, you will help one another to persevere. 53

He then asked: Will you really help each other?... Wherever you are? These few moments were deeply intense. Listening to that question, which was a fatherly one, affectionate yet demanding, made our petition for the holiness of all our brothers take on a new urgency. When you are old, tell the others what I have been telling you. These words will have some authority, being the words of the Founder. The Father was saying that we should help one another. He urged us to go together to seek our heavenly crown, and that we should obtain it together. He impressed on our souls the urgency of being involved in ensuring that no one get lost along the way. It depends a lot on each one of us. I have told you that, when one of my sons loses his vocation, those who lived with him are partly to blame – and could even have sinned grievously – because they hadn’t managed to help him. Is it possible for a poor creature to get lost, and the others don’t notice until it’s too late? Bodily illnesses of the are noticed by the pallor in one’s face, by the feverish look, by loss of appetite, by tiredness… Well, with those of the soul, the same thing happens. If you are concerned about your brothers, you will spot that perhaps someone abandons the Norms, that he has little enthusiasm, that he is not very mortified… You must notice! Is that clear? Because, if he is helped, if he wants to struggle, he gets over that bad moment. Everyone can get over it, everyone, if he fights! I have thirty-two years’ experience. For a moment the room became silent. We felt intensely that our Father was leaning on each one of us. He entrusted us with the care of the pusillus grex. Afterwards, he added jokingly: My sons, don’t become so serious! The atmosphere became relaxed once more, thanks to a couple of amusing remarks. When the laughter came to an end, the Father returned to the first topic of the get-together. He recalled the episode of the second miraculous catch of fish, which he had commented on so many times in meditations and talks, to stress the point that apostolic activity in the Work is the fruit of a joint action, the organic collaboration of priests and lay persons. The dominant passion of the priests of Opus Dei is to give doctrine and direct souls, to preach and hear confessions. The priests have to give their lives completely to this work. And in order to “kill” the priests with work, the lay people have to bring them souls, since it is you who cross the ocean from shore to shore. You cast the nets where the directors tell you and bring the fish to Christ’s feet, like the Apostles after that marvellous catch. Your mission is to kill your priest brothers, who are on the shore, with work, by bringing them souls. And they are happy to let themselves be “killed.” Fishing for souls requires lay people to be truly dedicated, well-prepared in every field, including their profession. A member of Opus Dei, if he mends shoes, has to mend them well; if he is an engineer, he has to build his bridges carefully. I’m not saying he has to be a genius, but competent yes, well versed in his profession. With those words the get-together ended. When our Father said it was a fine day, and that we should go out and make good use of the sun, we had to restrain a ‘No!’ We started to get up and accompanied him to the door. A quarter of a century has gone by since that get-together of the 1st of April. Once again our dreams have fallen short, because since then the Work has extended to new countries; it has experienced a healthy growth with men and women of every race and background. However, there is still a long way to go: there are nations and cities where that most beautiful war of love and peace has not yet begun. That is why, after so many years, our Father’s words still ring in our ears, urging us to ask for many vocations for his Work.

2 July 1962

It would be difficult to forget that date. It was the last get-together I had in Rome with our Father. The following morning a group of students from the Roman College left from Stazione Termini, on our way to Barcelona and Madrid; only Gerry and Jorge went to Pamplona. When the Father came down to the soggiorno after lunch, his first words were for those who were leaving Rome. Perhaps he noticed we were rather pensive. What was clear is that he immediately dispelled the pain we were feeling. It gives me great joy when you leave… I am certain that wherever you go, there will be a faithful soul, a reliable man, a person who wants to sanctify himself and others; because I see you have a great sense of responsibility, which is the best sense one can have. As he spoke, the Father fixed his gaze on one, then on another, and another…, as if emphasising that his words were ones of fatherly confidence, and meant for the ears of each one. His eyes also met mine briefly. And what he said to me then, with that look of his, has helped me so much afterwards! A few days ago – the Father continued – I commented to your brothers on the General Council that to have a sense of responsibility is a great gift of God. The spirit of the Work tends to make us responsible, and, at the same time, serene; it helps us remain firm and at ease, come what may! Do you know what it is to be serene? The Father paused for a moment. A serene man is one who knows how to master his passions, who may commit a fault but does not collapse, who does not lose his peace, or his interior balance, because he realises that other souls rely on him. That is why serenity is a consequence of a sense of responsibility. We will always act serenely and responsibly, if we move with the faith of children of God, with the hope of one who rests in the Lord, with the charity of one who does everything for love and only for love. We also need to make an effort to practise all the cardinal virtues: one cannot have a sense of responsibility when there is a lack of prudence or justice, or the other moral virtues. The pause was even shorter than the one before. Do you want us to draw another consequence from having a sense of responsibility? Yes, Father. The Work continually gives you formation: a treasure of doctrine and life which it places in your hands. However, it is not just for you to hold on to, but so that it overflows and trickles down all around you. I rejoice to see you like a recipient, retaining all that the Directors pour out when they form you; but you also have to be a fountain of clean water, fresh, noble and healthy, which provides others with the opportunity to be filled with the supernatural and human spirit of Opus Dei. My children, what do you want me to say? Teachers! May you be teachers. Frequently I have been able to say to all those who in one way or another specialise in education: you have to be teachers, but you will not be if you do not have a great spirit of sacrifice. And I say the same thing to you now, because to become a teacher, things have to be done in such a way that your brothers begin where you leave off, pouring out the waters of experience in such a way that other brothers may be filled with them. In order to teach others one has to have a sense of responsibility; one has to be humble, and place others above one’s own head: don’t make yourselves indispensable. You must teach in such a way that the others will be better than you, because they can count on what you know. Our Father used to tell many stories in the get-togethers: they were like flashes of good humour, very vivid accounts that helped us to retain better what he taught us. On this occasion as well he referred to one, which had to do with what he had been saying to us. Once, many years ago, I was in a seminary. The rector, a friend of mine, told me that they had a cook 55 who, when he prepared a special dish, would shut himself up in the kitchen and did not even allow the kitchen boys in… Isn’t that ridiculous? Yes, Father, we answered with a smile. Well then, don’t be silly, and teach your brothers, so that your Father’s desire may be fulfilled, so that those who come after us will always be better than us… And he continued: Lately I have been going to the artists’ studio quite frequently. One day I said to Manolo: Think about what the old masters used to do. In their studio they did not have the modern ways of reproducing paintings or sketches. But each of them would gather around himself several disciples and would teach them to copy one of his paintings, passing on his technique: sometimes he gave them a rough sketch, or only an idea… The disciple did the actual painting, but really the painting was the master’s work, because he provided the subject, the composition, the choice of colours…; as well as adding at the end the firm, clear strokes, the tonality of light and shadow, which crowned the painting. However, when someone asked, those medieval masters were honest and humble enough to clarify: It’s not mine; it has been painted by so and so. Thanks be to God, this is what happens in the Work, especially in the case of the Directors, because they know that if they thought they had to do everything themselves, many things would not get done, or would be done badly. In the Work everyone has a precise job to carry out, and in this way everything progresses. The one who knows how to govern, knows how to teach his brothers, trusts them, and is humble enough to transmit his experiences without keeping anything back. The Director has to work like this, even though there might be some who think he does nothing at all. He gave us another example, to illustrate this last idea. Remember the story about the village idiot. There was a celebration, and band had arrived: clarinets, trombone, and other wind instruments… as well as the conductor with his baton. The idiot was so excited: he thought it was all very difficult…except for the job of the conductor of the band, who only had to wave his stick. And he commented: To play the trombone, not for me, but to wave the little stick, that’s something I could do just as well as him. For there are people who think that what is important and difficult is to play the trombone. You, on the other hand, take the little stick, especially the Directors who have to be humble and fulfil that duty, even though sometimes there are silly people who think that to wave the baton needs no effort at all. My sons: put at the service of your brothers the faculties God has given you; help them with all your talents: with the knowhow, with your scientific, literary, artistic, sporting qualities… With all your qualities! This time the pause was a little longer, but nobody interrupted. Our Father concluded: I think I have told you, more or less, something that I particularly wanted to tell you. Take these things to the prayer, meditate on them in the presence of God, and you will decide to be more humble. Perhaps you will see that what seemed a defect in one of your brothers is a wonderful sacrifice. In that way you will be good sons of your Father, and elder brothers to those who will come later, because you will have the spirit of a good teacher who knows how to give himself humbly to others, so that they be well formed and carry out the Work better than you. Don Alvaro’s health was not very good these days, and, as a rare exception, he wasn’t present at the get-together. Our Father asked us again, as he did it frequently those days, to pray for him so he would recover soon. He had made the same request to those who had gone to Salto di Fondi for their summer semester. That is why we were few in the common room. The get-together lasted an hour and a half. There was also a chance to sing a while. At some point, the Father told us a story about his time in Saragossa. When I was very young, and was living in the Seminary of Saragossa, I once got very annoyed over something. Then one day, the professor of Canon Law told us this story, about a business-man who used to buy cinnamon and grind it in a very good mill of ball-bearings to convert it into the finest powder. There was one problem: whenever a ball wore down, he had to order a replacement from Germany. One day he was left without any; and having got tired of waiting for them to come from Germany, he went down to the river bank and took three roundish stones, as hard as flint, more or less similar in size to the original ones. He put them in the mill and kept turning them round and round… And after a fortnight they were nice and 56 round, just like the German ones, and they ground the cinnamon perfectly. The professor finished his story with the comment: God Our Lord does the same thing with the souls he loves. Do you understand, Escrivá? Those words did me a lot of good. It is clear that no one is going to become a saint with the help of Prester John of the Indies, but rather by the people around us. I would give my life for each one rather than offend anyone, but it is also good that we receive a few blows. We have to be grateful to our Lord for bringing us to these Centres of formation, so we lose our awkwardness, smooth the corners, and become rounded and pleasing to our Lord. If you sometimes suffer because of some misunderstanding, don’t allow yourself to be resentful, or think that it is a lack of charity; rather think that it is you who are at fault. But keep in mind that the Lord permits these things in order to form us, to polish us. Our Father God makes use of those small daily things

– more than big troubles – to get us to improve and become saints. Don’t forget it. I remember very well that professor’s illustration. He was called don Elias Ger.

8 September 1962

During the first years of the apostolic work in France it was practically a tradition for our Father to spend some days in Paris towards the end of summer. Those attending the International Summer Course that we used to organise in Bercy in those days, making use of the free places left by the residents, eagerly shared with us the hope of meeting our Father. Thanks be to God, during those early first years many students got to know the Work through that activity; they came closer to God, and a good number asked for the Admission. However, in 1962 the course ended and the students left without our Father having come. After that there was going to be an annual course in Bercy, and we also began to get the house ready for the new academic year. While we were painting, ironing curtains and coverlets, our ears and our hearts were attentive to the phone. And what we were so much looking forward to, happened: we heard those days that the Father would be in Paris on the 7th and 8th of September, and would come to Rouvray. We were overjoyed; the minutes flew by and yet they seemed very long… On the afternoon of the very day he arrived, we had a wonderful get-together with him in the sitting room of Rouvray. As well as those who were already there, several who worked in an Administration also arrived. To make sure she could be with the Father, the red lamp came early in the morning, and stayed the rest of the day. They were treasured moments, in which our Father told us many things, especially about apostolate and proselytism. We also contributed, asking him to pray for those friends of ours who gave hope of a vocation, and telling him in detail about the work we did with the residents. In the article of Noticias which describes those moments, there is a sentence which reflects the optimistic, demanding, lively tone of that get-together: We have a weapon more powerful than the atomic bomb: prayer. Residences are a means, but you cannot be content with thirty residents; the radius of your action has to reach the three thousand that are outside. Is that clear? In the diary of Rouvray for that day we read: We spent more than an hour and a half with the Father: How our sisters in other countries will envy us! But most likely they will be happy with our good fortune. Before he left, the Father agreed to have some photos taken of him with us. Our joy is unending, for he says that tomorrow he will celebrate Mass here, as well as Don Alvaro and Don Javier. When they left we went back to our normal work. We all thought the same: we were grateful for what we had lived through, and we were preparing ourselves well for the next day.

A colossal work Saturday, 8th of September 1962. Feast of the Nativity of the Virgin. We will never in this life be able to forget that day! It is with this joyful exclamation that the diary of Rouvray continues. The Father celebrated Mass in our oratory. As there were several employees and a small group of residents, we organised things so that nearly everybody of the Work could attend, without leaving the Residence unattended. After the thanksgiving I will see those of the Regional Advisory in their offices, he had said as soon as he arrived. And that is how it was. After that we asked the Father if he wanted to see how the house was looking. He agreed, and we accompanied him to the floors downstairs. He liked the decoration which had been achieved with the furnishings and the old, traditional furniture we had bought in a flea market and later restored. Later on, the Father again wanted to have a get-together with those of us in the Work who lived in Rouvray. As we settled down he made comments to this one and that one; for example, I remember the 58 comment he made to one who was Spanish, regarding her half-French: When a foreigner speaks incorrectly, it can be amusing; it is sufficient for her to make herself understood… But try to keep learning to improve your French. This time too the main topic was apostolic zeal. We had tried very hard to get the Residence going, and we were very keen, looking forward to the apostolic fruits. But the Father looked much further ahead: Rouvray was a part of the immense apostolic work that we had before our eyes: the residents, other students, and domestic workers, professionals, and housewives..., in Paris to start with, but we had to dream about extending the Work all over the country! Referring to the meeting he had had with our sisters of the Advisory, he commented: I was telling your sisters this morning that the corporate works are always a means, never an end (…). A Residence, a Home Management College, a University are means, means to an end, which is to find many people who are ready to serve the Lord in the middle of the world. So that we could all see very clearly the essential difference between the means and the end, I remember that he compared these activities with a pen or a typewriter, which are instruments equally useful and necessary in their way. Our Founder’s looks, expressive, alive, were enough to show the joy he had in his soul when he dreamt about the apostolic work that his daughters could carry out. It is colossal! he said to us. In the morning he had referred to our work as a sowing of peace, of heaven. He urged us to give a strong push to the traditional means of St Raphael and St Gabriel and the activities of spiritual formation. So you must pray. The works of St Raphael and S Gabriel have no limits. Then in a few strokes, he outlined the projects in which the Assistant Numeraries had a very important role to play. And when these works are already well organised, with everybody’s help, with your cheerfulness, with your work; if these younger daughters of mine are cheerful, if they are every day more pious, and they really put their shoulder to the wheel, then, we shall have to start a St Raphael Circle for girls in Paris in the hospitality sector: helping them, teaching them, lending them a hand. My daughters – speaking directly now to the Assistant Numeraries – the Lord has loved you as much as me; He has given us the means to come close to Him. But these girls do not know him yet. One has to pray for them especially. Do you agree? He asked one of them, who was so attentive she didn’t even blink: Yes, Father, she exclaimed. Of course! This is what you have come to Paris for…, our Founder concluded smiling. Then he continued: The Administrations of our Centres are not there just for doing material work but also for spiritual tasks; they are a splendid opportunity to help us keep close to God our Lord, by means of small mortifications… asking for permission, if at some moment you feel that the Lord is asking you for a mortification of greater significance! My daughters, eat well, and sleep enough. Treat each other affectionately, without doing anything silly: with the love of Christ. You must struggle so as not to displease your sisters. We are obliged to make the life of holiness of those around us lovable, and for that we have to be able to deny ourselves in many details that do not hurt us, and which make the life of self-surrender easier for those we live with. He then referred to such moments – which, naturally come when we work hard – when we notice the burden of the day and the heat. Using a typical Spanish expression, he continued: And when you feel tired, say so, and don’t “pull a long face”. Go to the director or the person who hears your chat and tell her! We have to speak up, because sometimes, when we get too engrossed in our own things, our head doesn’t function properly: be sincere, simple; everything can be put right. A little later he would again insist on the importance of caring for this aspect of our self-surrender well: My daughters, tell it all. What can happen? Nothing! I get tired too. Look at our Lord, how He felt sometimes: fatigatus ex itinere, tired with so much walking, to do good for souls.

A short-term goal and a dream of our Founder At one point in the get-together, the Father summarised what he had said already: This year we are going to increase the work of St Raphael and of St Gabriel in France. What do you think? Like when there is a battle…. Let there be no wars! They are a terrible scourge for countries! Ask God our Lord for peace in the world, my daughters. I was saying before, that in wars there were artillery batteries which, at a distance of some kilometres, concentrated on firing at the enemy; so that by the time the infantry arrived at the battlefield, the enemy had been practically eliminated. Well, we are going to do something similar for the work of St Raphael: preparing the way with aspirations, with mortifications, finishing our work well and offering it to our Lord…: so that when the time comes to speak with people, souls will be well disposed to hear God’s call. Take courage, my daughters: if the works of St Raphael and St Gabriel go well, there will be many vocations. In the morning, our Father had reminded us clearly that the apostolate proper to the Work is built on friendship and confidence: to know the girls’ names, what they study, how they spend their time; to form them in such a way that they live their faith. Afterwards, the girls who have been coming to the Centre for a month or a month and a half begin to do the chat – without realising it – and launch out into doing apostolate: that is the moment to organise catechesis, visits to the poor… My daughters, it is not necessary for me to descend into further details. You have a lot of written material on the work of St Raphael. Do your prayer on all you have there. Only a small number should come to the circles, to preserve a sense of intimacy. Begin with one, two, or three… and when there are six, divide them into two groups. Some days you may give the St Raphael class to one person only…! When that happens, in God’s eyes, that day has been very effective. And he also added, a little later: You have to move the people you deal with; you have to go out and get them, you have to be totally committed to each one of them. You will then have many friends who with time will be a great help, and many will whistle, with God’s grace. We listened eagerly to our Father’s words. He then went on to talk to us about something that had a special place in his heart: My daughters, I dream about the marvellous apostolate that can be done here, in Paris: to start a residence for girls who work in the hospitality sector, to look after them affectionately and with a great sense of freedom. In Opus Dei we do not take anybody’s freedom away; we teach them how to use their freedom well. A few generic regulations – very few – are established, just enough for order in the house and to help people’s formation. If some youngster doesn’t accept these indications, you explain to her that it is better to find somewhere else, and you continue being her friend. Anyone who wants to be well-behaved will always find the means to be so in our Centres. Speaking again directly to the Assistant Numeraries, because of the important role they play in this, he concluded: Pray for this especially. Without you, daughters of my soul, nothing gets done in Opus Dei. You have to help in all the corporate works. If you are souls of prayer, which is to do your prayer as well as you can, if you seek presence of God with aspirations, with acts of love and atonement, then, my daughters, you will be effective and understanding towards others. And this is the secret of your happiness and your strength: to be more concerned every day about others, to make God’s way easier for those who live with us.

To explain and defend vocation The Father wanted us to speak, to ask him something. Immediately someone took the initiative. Why do we kiss the rosary before saying Psalm II? It is to show our devotion. In the Church’s liturgy, do you see how the priest kisses the vestments? In social life also, it is an expression of affection, of courtesy. 60

Don Alvaro intervened to give another very clear example: “It is like when you kiss the Father’s hand.” The following question was about the day on guard. Our Father, referring to what had been the central theme of the get-together, commented: This year, whichever of you who is on guard each day also has to pray a lot for the consolidation of the works of St Raphael and St Gabriel, don’t you agree? Then go to the fraternal chat and, even if they don’t ask you, say: I have done this and this… The family conversation continued; our Father was interested in specific details of each one: for instance, he wanted to know not only the city, but also the district and the street in Madrid where one of us had lived. In the end, as if speaking to recent vocations, he talked about how we deal with our own family. Treat your parents affectionately, very much so. It is natural that sometimes, if they do not know the Work, they may feel a bit uncomfortable with your vocation, but they do not offend God. You must have the holy know-how not to speak about things that might upset them, which, for the moment, they might not be able to understand. Don’t talk to them about the Work immediately. I have had my vocation now for thirty four years. At first, when I had to be in charge of everything, if there was a father or a mother who did not understand the vocation of their daughter or their son, I would encourage them: Speak, speak… They sometimes said such nonsense…! When they had got it off their chest, I would say: I agree with you totally. And immediately I would explain why: Having the knowledge you have of the Work, it’s only natural for you to say what you’ve said. Then the Father explained, with great affection, the way each person of the Work is free and responsible for what she does, and should learn how to explain – and, if necessary, also to defend – Opus Dei and her vocation. It is a natural manifestation of our lay mentality. Our Father concluded, specifying how to resolve issues that our parents may sometimes bring up: by being good, pious – holy! – good daughters, good students… and also have the know-how to win over our parents! Our Father’s last words, before giving us the blessing, were: I expect a lot of you. We were happy, and with a more than renewed enthusiasm to carry out a wider and deeper work of apostolate in the academic year just starting. The following day, early in the morning, the doorbell rang. It was Don Javier: he had brought a box of sweets from our Father. It was embellished with three ears of wheat: as if to remind us of the abundant harvest that our Lord and all the Work were expecting, after the generous sowing our Father had carried out in our souls Miraflores, 15 September 1962

In the summer of 1962, our Father’s visit to Saragossa on the occasion of being made doctor honoris causa by the University of Saragossa was still very fresh in our minds. Every time news came of his gettogethers in Rome or his apostolic journeys around Europe, we recalled those days that he was with us: the conferring ceremony in the auditorium of the University, the Mass in the church of San Carlos, the get-togethers in Miraflores… On Thursday 13th September, Jaime Tur, who was in Saragossa for an exam, arrived at Miraflores. He said that our Father was in Pamplona, and he told some details of a get-together that they had had in Aralar. 14th September, the Feast of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross, was marvellous. They called from Pamplona to tell us that our Father would come to Miraflores in the evening, around eight o’clock. ‘A few moments after learning this’, the diary records, ‘we began getting things ready.’ On arrival, after greeting our Lord in the oratory, our Father and don Alvaro went to the Director’s room. On the first floor, don Pedro, who had been ordained a priest in July, went to meet him. As it was the first time our Founder had seen him as a priest, he kissed his hands and asked for his blessing. Although it was late, and in spite of his being tired from the journey, our Father spent some time with his older sons, vivid moments, full of affection. He made use of the opportunity to remind us of the care we should take in the formation of new vocations. They have to be treated one by one, like jewels, the same way as a goldsmith mounts each one of the precious stones in a vessel of great value. One could see that our Father was very happy, paying great attention to each one of us. Someone told several stories of the apostolic work with priests. Our Founder made a few comments in which he emphasised the need to look after them with great affection, and help them to feel very much accompanied by our Lord and our Blessed Lady.

At the first hour The following day, 15th September, feast of Our Lady of Sorrows, our Father celebrated Mass at nine o’clock in the oratory of the Residence. The people of the Work attended and some residents who had already come to stay in the Colegio Mayor, although the academic year had not yet started. After breakfast, our Father met briefly with a number of us in the director’s room. When don Pedro arrived our Father wanted a few photographs taken: he explained that this son of his had been working in the photographic laboratory during his stay in the Roman College. A few topics came up during those few minutes. I remember one very clear comment he made, referring to the promotion of university residences not being an aim of Opus Dei. The Work seeks the sanctification of all souls. The residences for students are only one means – one out of a hundred – to obtain that end. At ten o’clock in the morning our Founder received some Supernumeraries. Towards midday, accompanied by don Alvaro and don Javier, our Father went to visit the Archbishop of Saragossa. When he came back, some residents stood waiting at the entrance of Miraflores. Our Father was pleased with this “chance” encounter and stayed with them for a while. It was a short but very lively conversation. As nearly all of them were taking technical courses, our Father joked about it. Straightaway, a philosophy student spoke up. At last I’ve met a ‘poet’, our Father said, amused. Tomorrow in the Mass, I will keep you in mind so that you all pass your exams. I will not ask you to be brilliant: and I have my reasons for that, he added with good humour. 62

He did not go on to explain, but he made them understand that they should study with an upright intention, to serve the Lord, the Church and society, and not look for easy recognition in this life and with no higher purpose. He talked to them very clearly about the eminently apostolic purpose of the corporate works, in particular the residences for students, together with their unprofitable nature. He wanted them to understand that the members of the Work who lived there were university people like themselves, and; in some cases, members of faculty. He emphasised that they should have confidence in the people of the Work and to be sincere, especially with the Director and the priest. The mission of the Directors is to love you. They will tell no one what you say. We are all weak: if not, we would be overcome by pride. But when you fall, you have to try to be as little time as possible with your face on the floor.

Paying with one’s life It was around four in the afternoon when the get-together with the Numeraries and Associates in Saragossa began. Our Father spoke about prayer, self-surrender and proselytism. The Work, he said, is not an association of the faithful where one pays three pesetas for membership. Here you pay with your life. It is not we who look for God: it is He who looks for us first. We are souls who have given ourselves to the Lord, who seek holiness in the middle of the world. He explained to us that proselytism is like the beating of our heart, and then made this point: The son of mine who has no concern for proselytism iam foetet! He is dead, as Lazarus was when they took away the stone that closed the entrance to the sepulchre. If we are contemplatives, we shall have proselytistic zeal. We must be like burning coal: if you put it on a table, it burns the table; if it is put near an iron bar, it increases its temperature. We are interested in all souls! he emphasised strongly. Also in those of non-Catholics. They are won over with affection. He recalled how in his youth, while he lived in Saragossa, he had known many anarchists. They were anarchists out of ignorance, lack of doctrine. They were not bad people. I have never known bad people. Continuing with the subject of proselytism, he reminded us that to be in the Work, there are no upper age limits, or limits of profession or social condition… The only requirement is to have a vocation. He stressed a lot that Associates and Supernumeraries, according to their specific circumstances, should help the apostolic works just as the Numeraries do. And speaking to the Associates who were in the gettogether, he told them they should live their self-surrender with complete naturalness, without showing off when with their parents or brothers and sisters. He asked them to be clever, and to learn how to win them over without preaching at them. I envy you, he said. Your self-giving to God is total and complete, the same as mine. But you can go further. With the permission of the Directors you can go to the very doors of hell, to deal with your friends. All of us in the Work can and should give spiritual direction to other souls!, he continued. Some, with the mission received from the Directors, when they look after their brothers; and others, dealing with their friends, giving them advice, and directing them towards holiness. Even though that work of apostolate and friendship is not called spiritual direction, that is what it is. He then asked us to tell him some stories. Sebastian mentioned two new Centres and the Father pointed out that our works always begin small. Only monsters are born big, he concluded. Someone talked about the conference centres. Our Founder reminded us that to serve God a minimum of well-being is required: and he repeated an Italian saying, cuando il corpo sta bene, l’anima balla, which had helped him to understand that doctrine which was already in the teaching of St Thomas.

A sense of militia Our Father had spent a long time with his sons, but no-one felt it was too much. The opportunity for another meeting came with some cadets, who had not managed to come to the get-together, since they couldn’t leave the Military Academy earlier. Our Father met them in the Director’s little office and he encouraged them to perfect their professional formation constantly, so that they become good instruments in God’s hands. One of them said a few things about life in his Centre. Our Father advised him to speak with the local Director. In the Work, he said, we have a military sense: the Father confirms the authority of the local Directors. To do otherwise would be anarchy. Understand me well: a general does not contradict the authority of the colonel, nor the colonel that of the commandant. Governance in the Work is always collegial: no Director is a proprietor of anything or anyone. Using comparisons from military life, he also referred to the root of effectiveness in all work with souls. Prayer, my son, is our atomic bomb. Our being effective is all summed up in that phrase of Holy Scripture which I placed in the oratory of the General Council: omnes perseverantes unanimiter in oratione. That is why, when I read what a brother of yours once wrote to me in a letter – Things can’t go badly, Father, because we pray a lot – I felt very serene. Also using military terms, he commented on some aspects of the spirit of the Work, as for instance, love for the unity of our family. In military parades, for example, if some were to march more slowly they would upset the harmony of the whole. None of us must fall behind: we have to parade at the pace of our brothers, at God’s pace. That gathering was the end of the time spent with our Father in Saragossa: an unforgettable day, especially because of the affection and good humour he showed towards his sons. Early in the morning of the 16th, after celebrating Mass, he left Saragossa. As he was leaving Miraflores he glanced at a tapestry on the stairs, with the words: Cada caminante siga su camino (let every traveller follow his own way). He stopped and said that he had found that inscription in an old house in Valencia when he was giving a retreat to university students, soon after the war. He gave us his blessing and departed on his way to Barcelona.

23 August 1963 (I)

In 1963 I did my annual course in Pamplona, in Colegio Mayor Belagua. The previous years our Father had been in England, helping and guiding the apostolic work there. That summer, however, after visiting his sons in Zurich and Paris in the first week of August, he went to Reparacea, a house in Navarre in the Baztán Valley. He was accompanied by don Alvaro, don Javier and Javier. The fact that our Father was close by marked the whole of the course. His intentions were very present in our study, in the means of formation, and the periods of relaxation. From the day he arrived we were hoping to spend some time with him – a hope that increased when we learned that he had had a meeting with the academic staff of the University of Navarre. At last, on Thursday 22nd it was announced that he would come the following morning, around ten o’clock. That would not be the only time we would see him, for there would be a number of other get-togethers, both in Belagua and Aralar.

Make good use of the formation The sitting room of Belagua was full. The residents of the Centres of Pamplona had also come. Our Father arrived at the time announced and, after having greeted the Blessed Sacrament in the oratory, he wanted to receive the blessing of don Doroteo and don José Luis, whom he had not seen since their ordination. His first words during the get-together dealt with making good use of the means of formation, in particular the annual course: we had to acquire doctrine and put it into practice. Not like the man who said: “Do what I say, but not what I do.” It is necessary to give witness to doctrine with our own behaviour. Lead the way, first with the witness of your life, and afterwards with your behaviour, as the old Spanish saying puts it: Friar Example is the best preacher. You must lead, first with the witness of your life, and afterwards with your behaviour – as students, as professionals – and in that way you will show that you bear Jesus Christ within you, without doing anything strange. And he added: You cannot play with the grace of God when the Work, our Beautiful Mother, has provided us with these means to reach all souls; because we are interested in them all, without any exception. We have always opened our arms, wide open, to everyone. In that everyone he included non-Christians, the enemies of the Church, the separated brethren, who have lost the fullness of the faith… This time too, he repeated that list, and reminded us how he had insisted with the Holy See to be able to appoint non-Catholic Cooperators. The permission was obtained after insisting a lot and, above all, with the grace of the Lord. Theology teaches us that charity if it is to be Christ’s charity, has to have order. That is why we have great affection for all souls, without exception, but in the first place we have to love those in the Work. I love those who at this moment are passing by in the street, but not in the same way as I love you, although I am ready – with God’s grace – for any sacrifice for the good of souls. But if I were to love those people who are passing by in the street more than you, humanly speaking I would be an idiot, and supernaturally, my charity would be greatly disordered. Returning to the thread from which he had departed – the importance of being well formed – he continued: Our knowledge of doctrine provides us with material to light the fire in our prayer: Do not neglect your formation! Furthermore, it is not only for us. We have to be filled with doctrine so that our soul will overflow and reach others. But first we have to make good use of it ourselves, because of order and because nobody can give what he doesn’t have.

Our Father never counted the number of people in front of him. He spoke to and looked at each one, one by one. And even more so, if they were sons of his. At a given moment in that get-together he noticed don Julián and interrupted what he was saying. Pointing him out with a smile of fatherly pride, he told the rest of us: The first photograph of me that Pius XII saw was shown to him by Monsignor Montini. In it I appear with this son of mine next to me. He then continued: There are so many activities in the Work, so many external forms of corporate apostolate! But the most important apostolate is the personal directed apostolate: the one that does not appear in statistics. It is carried out by your brothers in every place and in every honest human environment, sacrificing oneself for others, being carriers of Christ, and placing Him in the hearts of others. Do you remember, Alvaro, in Molinoviejo, under the pine trees, when we prepared the first edition of the Catechism of the Work? Pepe Bueno was there – he was referring to his friend don José María Bueno Monreal, who later became Cardinal Archbishop of Seville – and he enjoyed reading some of the apostolates listed there. I say some, because our work is a sea without shores: broader and more diverse as time goes on, because the external forms will be different, depending on time and place, although the essence is always the same. What does the apostolate of Opus Dei really consist in? It can be summed up in taking the doctrine of Christ to people who are far from Him, who do not know Him. Our mission is to give doctrine through personal witness and by carrying out our profession honestly and uprightly. If we keep that marvellous companionship, if we stay calm when others lose their composure, your friends and colleagues will be astonished. Later they will come to you, certain that you can give them life and health. But you are young. Which apostolate can you never forsake? Let me tell you: that of St Raphael. The work of St Raphael precedes all the corporate works and always accompanies them. Where there is no work of St Raphael there is no apostolic work of Opus Dei. You who are young and so full of life, well, on the spiritual plane, you would not be alive if you did not feel determined to multiply the number of your brothers, if you did not look after the work of St Raphael. Our Beautiful Mother needs many young children, because a family without children is a family on the way to extinction. You must feel the great obligation to see that there are always many young vocations. Vocations come to the Work in very different ways, but the natural way by which God brings them is the work of St Raphael. Put everything you’ve got, your heart and soul, all your effort into it! Take good care of the personal apostolate of friendship and confidence. The duty of proselytism is the same for all, the same for you as for me. A son of mine, whether he is young or is already seventy years old – which is not only me, there are already a good number in Opus Dei – has no other choice but to do proselytism. You must all feel the Love of Christ and pass it on to others.
