True freedom With reference to proselytism, our Father reminded us that respect for the freedom of others is not a reason for lessening our apostolic desire: that would be a false excuse. When in doubt, let us put ourselves always on the side of freedom. I am not talking about politics. We must always defend freedom, which is necessary to be able to breathe out fully, and also to love Jesus Christ. You can’t serve God if you’re not free. Freedom, personal freedom! I do not find it in any part of the world, not even in the United States! he said jokingly, looking at an American who was present. With the excuse of a false respect for freedom, there are people who do not enter the lives of others or do apostolate. We have to try to take Christ to those around us, always with freedom. Why am I going to enter the lives of others? Because Jesus Christ has entered mine! It is not a matter of coercion. Only the mentally weak feel coerced, and there is no room for them in Opus Dei. No properly constituted individual feels coerced when presented with a divine path, a new light, which teaches him how to dominate his passions and serve God in laetitia. Does one have to ask them for permission? Jesus Christ did not ask me for permission to enter my soul. And you? You have not only the right but the duty to bring up the question of vocation with people who have the conditions to receive the call. There are many ways of following the Lord in Opus Dei. 66

He gave the example of what happens in healthy families. Children come when God wants. Some are tall, others short, some stout, and others thin; this one looks like granny, the other like great-grandmother… In Opus Dei, which is a family like that, both very divine and very human, there are Numeraries, Associates, Supernumeraries and marvellous Co-operators, even non-Catholics. I know some… What a wonderful family we are! Here there is a place for all types of people. We can, therefore, stir consciences and bring up the question of vocation with many people. St Augustine says that he who saves a soul has his own predestined. And I add that we cannot rest easy if we do not try to bring up the question of vocation with many. When bringing vocations to the Work, you affirm and strengthen your own vocation with a nut and bolt. After spurring us on to renew our spirit of proselytism, the Father reminded us of the condition required for the apostolate to be really effective: to forget oneself. This concern for others will prevent you having personal worries. Sometimes there may be scruples, silly things that God permits, but they disappear if those children of mine who are undergoing that situation pray, are obedient and look after the others. Don’t you see? Proselytism is not only to love those outside, but to love those inside first; not only to love those who have separated, but first to love those who do not wish to separate, and then those who have separated, with one’s whole soul. If you observe the order of charity, you will make and receive fraternal correction well, you will help one another, and will learn that the best mortification for each one is to put oneself out for the others – even if it is noticed, but even better if it is not

– so as to make the ways of God on earth more agreeable for others. Referring to the vocation of Numeraries, he developed the theme, talking about how splendid family life is in the Centres of Opus Dei. Some years ago, when a certain Numerary brother of yours found family life hard, I would take him by the ear, and tell him: “Go and stay with your parents for a while…” A week later I would receive a letter, and then another: “Father! I’m coming back, even if you don’t let me. I can’t stand it!” And this is because, in the midst of these trifles or little manifestations of pettiness and mistakes that can sometimes occur (they are always little things), our family life is wonderful. Our life – he said this stressing each syllable – is a di-vine mar-vel.

It is worth while We listened in silence, thanking our Lord for all the graces that come with our call to the Work. When our Father invited us to speak, the first topic that came up was the publications that were being prepared. He described in particular the volumes of Meditaciones which would begin reaching the Centres a few months later. The first edition was already about to appear. But always, he noted, what interests me is that each one spend one of his times of prayer each day on the topics which are of particular relevance to him, the ones that are more necessary for him; that fit in with his own concerns, which are never personal concerns. Afterwards he spoke about sincerity, which is at odds with shame: like a sensible patient who goes to the doctor and has no problem with undressing for an examination. To be sincere is not bad manners; it is to be well-mannered, Christian, refined… One has to say everything and let oneself be seen completely, the heart and the soul, without any corners. This marvellous simplicity is absolutely necessary, if we want to be faithful and happy. It happens to me as well. I am getting old and have more defects than you: the same, but intensified… and corrected, or not corrected. But when I have a silly idea, I go running to don Alvaro or don Javier, who are my custodes, and I speak with them. Everything that worries you is not the way to God. ‘So, Father, what is the formula to get rid of things that bother us and take away our peace?’ To love Jesus Christ in such a way that – out of love for him – we give ourselves to others. And that way we will come to our examination – that short pause at midday, and that longer one at night, not very long – able to say: I have not thought about myself! Then it can be seen that I’m not exaggerating when I say that we are contemplative souls at all the crossroads of life. We seek God in the middle of the world. We do not have grilles or bolts. When I go into a monastery, even though I understand and admire the religious vocation, I always leave a bit sad, because the Lord has 67 directed me to a different calling. But in the same way that those good religious seek God in their hiding, we look for him along all the pathways on earth! If we are faithful to this spirit, we can say with the Apostle: vivo autem iam non ego, vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20). We will be truly contemplatives, because we will have truly identified ourselves with our Saviour: with errors, but fighting to avoid them! He who does not fight does not have interior life, and will not be able to be contemplative. When you have been in the Work many years, perhaps you will discover many details that passed unnoticed before. And the devil, who is old and was once an angel – although he is silly, because he always repeats himself – will ask you: Do you see how better you were in the first years? And it is not so. In the first years there is fervour, and enthusiasm… Have you seen children? They go up and down… Then a person reaches serenity, maturity. The older ones discover things that children do not notice. For example, a crooked picture on the wall; children think it is beautiful like that, but their mother puts it straight. God grants us more light and we see the creases of the soul. Love makes little things bigger, shows the defects clearly; and because we love, it grieves us not to improve in those details. We sang a song of the Work – Fieles vale la pena – but only after our Father told us that if we saw him getting emotional, we should stop. While we sang, our Father showed affection to those closest to him. We knew we could go on until the last verse. When we finished, he told the story of the Italian ecclesiastic who asked what the inscription Vale la pena in the Cortile Vecchio of Villa Tevere referred to. Then he added: For a plate of lentils, we are capable of selling our birthright… No! Do not allow yourselves to be deceived by the lentils. In that get-together, our Father mentioned basic points of the spirit of Opus Dei, as if stringing them together one after another. And all that while we just listened, giving thanks to God for that di-vine marvel. It was, truly, worthwhile...

23 August 1963 (II)

There were good musicians on that annual course in Belagua. On 22nd August, when it was announced that our Founder would come the following morning, they started practising some songs of the Work, in case they had a chance. The get-together was long, and our Father touched upon those topics of always, which we must always consider in our prayer: personal apostolate, proselytism, love for the Church, fidelity to our divine calling…! After singing Fieles vale la pena we continued with Pito pa’ que pites tu, the last verses of which are: caminad con valor que se han abierto / los caminos divinos de la tierra. Los caminos divinos de la tierra – the divine paths of the earth. It is true. It is an old phrase in the Work. But if we do not struggle, we can go astray; it is easy to get lost; and, so as not to lose our way, we need to have good spiritual direction. In the Work, the spiritual directors are lay. But, without priests, what would we do? Nothing! The moment comes when souls come up against the sacramental wall, and then the presence of a priest with our spirit is absolutely necessary. In the first years there was only one priest in the Work, and that was me. Your brothers did the Confidence with me, because I did not want to hear their confessions. Sometimes – only a few times – I actually did. That sacramental wall is opened up by the priests. The lay people – canonically they are called “laity” – can kill themselves, the Directors can give spiritual direction as best they can, the others can do a lot of good with their example and with fraternal correction…, but if there were no priests in the Work, all that work would be lost. Our Father continued talking about the good shepherd, who knows his sheep: for us, the place where we are known and where we can be given what we need, is in the Work. That is why my sons always try to go to confession to priests of Opus Dei. And if some day someone has a serious fall, he goes to the doctor who knows him, to one who can cure the wound completely; not to a casual doctor, to a ‘quack’… I insist, if a son of mine, in a moment of great weakness, were to go to another priest, while able to go to a brother of his, perhaps that other confessor, who is not the good shepherd, might think: So that’s what those Opus Dei people are like! It would be a lack of justice towards their brothers, who fight to be saints. And although that priest would make no comment out of respect for the sacramental seal, he would never advise souls who go to him to get in touch with the men of Opus Dei! He asked us to pray for the priests of the Work, who were – then as now – few to look after the needs of the apostolic work: to pray, so that they would always be learned, holy, cheerful, and good servants of their brothers and all souls.

Feet of clay Referring to the need to recognise personal weaknesses with simplicity in confession and in spiritual direction, our Father put before our eyes the statue mentioned in the Book of Daniel, even adding to the description: The head of this image was of fine gold, its breast and arms of silver, its belly and thighs of bronze… but its feet were of clay (cf. Dan 2:31-35). As long as we remember that we have feet of clay, we are safe. On the other hand, when we are filled with pride, our feet crack open and we fall flat on our face. There are people who react in a silly way: Oh! I can’t! I don’t have a vocation! … On the contrary, even if you were to have committed a hundred thousand crimes, precisely because you have received that Christian vocation, you go to your brother the priest for Confession, and to the Confidence. There are friends around who share with each other the vilest things they have done, exaggerating and, unfortunately, having a lot of fun, like animals! And are we going to be overcome by shame when opening our soul in spiritual direction? Sincerity, simplicity, clarity. Go to the good shepherd. Always, go for it! Because we count on the grace 69 of our vocation, we will overcome all difficulties. And if there comes a time – I hope not – when you experience the misfortune of having a serious fall, if you are humble you will come out of that situation with greater resolve, filled with sorrow. I know some cases when I have had to cry out: o felix culpa! Because from the humble foundation of a person’s own self-knowledge, the tree of life started to grow again. When he talked about that demanding struggle against our passions, our Founder used to recommend that we should put seven bolts on the heart; seventy if necessary, he added on this occasion. He told us that he puts them on too, because passions are always the same. All that I tell you is pure gold, because it is sure doctrine. But I also am reminding you about it, as your Father, even though I am a poor man: pauper servus et humilis. How many times I have to repeat it! Cor contritum et humiliatum, Deus, non despicies. Contritum et humiliatum valde. I have known many Popes: I was born in the time of Leo XIII; I have lived through the pontificates of St Pius X, Benedict XV, Pius XII, John XXIII, and now Paul VI, the Monsignor Montini whom I met in 1946 when I arrived in Rome, and who treated me with so affectionately. You see: I have known many Popes; Cardinals, dozens; Bishops and Archbishops, hundreds… I insist that I am a sinner, a poor man; but God has chosen me to do Opus Dei, to be your Father. He will ask for an account of what I have said today. Take notes and meditate on them. And if some of you become priests, or continue as laymen – it is as important to be a well prepared layman as a well prepared priest – when you are old, you will be able to say: I heard the Father say this in Pamplona, on such and such a day – it is good to say the exact date – and it will have the interest and freshness of having heard it directly from the Founder. God will ask you to give an account of what you have just heard from the Founder, who is a poor sinner, but there is no other Founder after him. Is that clear, you rascals?

The concerns of a father and a mother Our Father had come to Pamplona just to be with his sons. But he had been talking for a long time and we needed a break. Everyone was happy when it was only a short one. When the get-together began again, those who had sat further away, tried to be closer this time. You have to eat and sleep, he said to us. If you don’t sleep well, say so. Eat with a good appetite, which is perfectly compatible with mortification. And make good use of time. Try to take the weekly walk and the short monthly excursion, and make use of the courses as an opportunity to study and also to rest, which is what they are for. It is a complete change of environment. Don’t think that these things I am reminding you about – that you should eat, that you should rest… – are mentioned by the Father because he is also a mother. Of course he is a mother! There is a Women’s Section, but there is no foundress. But it is not the mother who arranges these things; it is the Father. I point them out to you as father, because you must work like donkeys and you have to recover your strength. Opus Dei is operatio Dei, work of God. We sanctify ourselves with work. He who does not work cannot belong to Opus Dei. That is why we must take care to rest so as to work well later. Professional work is part of our divine vocation. During this second part, we asked more questions. Perhaps during the break some had agreed to ask a specific question for our Father to talk about. His answers were brief, direct: they were simple, clear ideas, to keep in our hearts and to remember. Father you have sometimes spoken about those two unnamed disciples who were in St Peter’s boat, during the second miraculous catch of fish...: The other two? You and I! Come on, my dear sons, let’s go and fish! Father, why don’t you tell us about obedience? Without obedience, the whole body of the Work would become undone. Don Alvaro has written an Acta for the altar of the oratory of Saint Michael, which says: in hoc pulcherrimo caritatis bello. Obedience is a weapon which keeps us united, together, in this war of Love. What has the Virgin Mary done for the Work? What must you do to please Our Lady? We have images of her all around, everywhere. There are so many details in our life of our affection for her: the Marian Norms, which show our gratitude because she is Co-redeemer. 70

Father, what about the special intention? It needs lots of prayers, many thousands of Masses, a lot of cheerfulness, a lot of mortification, and many little sacrifices. I passed a few words on to Carlos Cardona, so that he would put them in one of those internal publications that are like family letters: the purpose of that intention is to preserve the spirit of the Work and the effectiveness of the apostolate. From when I was sixteen until I was twenty-six, I was asking for Opus Dei without knowing what I was asking for. He recalled the aspiration he had engraved with a nail on the base of a statue of Our Lady of Pilar. It was found many decades later: Domina ut sit! Next to those words he had written the date, before the foundation of the Work. I know someone who used to sing: ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur?... Ecce ego quia vocasti me! The Work has been divine since its origin; and has continued with the ordinary Providence of our Lord. Opus Dei is based on Holy Scripture and on the doctrine of the Church, and not on any kind of miracles. Which is your favourite memory, Father? You. And then? You. And then? You again. It could not be otherwise. In a family, the best memories are the children. Ask any father! The Work is divine, and human too.

The ninety per cent Two months earlier, in June, Pope Paul VI had been elected. He had immediately confirmed that the Second Vatican Council, inaugurated by his predecessor John XXIII, would continue. The Holy Father had asked all the faithful to pray for its fruits. Someone in the get-together asked what was the main difficulty that the Church was facing at that time. All the problems of the Church are worth considering. We are interested in all of them, because we are Church. The main problem is the holiness of us who make up the Church. The great question is the salvation of all souls. The purpose of the Work is to give doctrine so that people become saints in their ordinary life, and have interior life. Father, about owing our parents eighty or ninety per cent of our vocation, don’t you think that is a bit too much? Sometimes I think it is little. Even in families which are not Christian, even when they are against the Church, for they have given us a marvellous foundation on which to receive the sowing. He talked about giving our heart. We had often heard him say that he blessed the human, holy love of our parents with his two priestly hands. But he recalled how the Church has always taught that the call to celibacy is, in itself, a higher gift of God, and this has been defined as a truth of faith. Already in The Way he had written: Marriage is for the rank and file, not for the officers of Christ’s army;3 even though very often a soldier in the ranks earns the highest decoration the army grants for heroic deeds in war, and perhaps a general can never wear one… What do you tell those of us who are going to form part of local Councils? I have shown you what to do, with many notes. When I speak about directors, I refer to the whole of the local Council: the Director, the Assistant Director, the Secretary and the priest, if there is one. In the Work, there never is an “owner director”: I “shot him in the back” as a traitor many years ago! In Opus Dei there are no dictators. Never forget it; there are no tyrants here. We do not tolerate them in Opus Dei. Governing is done by a group of people: it is collegial. First, matters are studied – by whoever has to study them – and then they are voted on. If they are not in agreement, the matter is passed on to the Regional Commission; and if there is no agreement there either, it goes to the General Council. It is voted on, and the minority bow their heads. They do so happily. We do not tolerate tyrants. Down with tyranny! I do not personally govern anything. I have kept for the Father – because this is what the Lord has wanted – four or five things which are more like tasks of a mother. It is a blessing to know that in

3 The Way, no. 28 the Work there is no such thing as the arbitrary decision of a master who does things on the hoof. Father, how can we learn to be poor? When you have noticed something clearly wrong, you write it down. And some time later, when giving a talk, you say: Now, let’s see… For instance, we have to go on a journey and we are in a restaurant. We ask for mineral water and it is warm. We can say: please bring some ice! Very well. But we do not say: bring another bottle which is cold! And pay for both. It is a small thing. It is not a sin, but it is a lack of poverty to ask for another fresh bottle, leaving one that we won’t drink. There are a hundred thousand more things like that. Poverty. I knew someone who used to say: ‘Clothes are for Juanito; not Juanito for the clothes.’ Afterwards he learned that although Juanito is not for the clothes, the clothes must be looked after. I like you to be well dressed. It is natural that clothes get worn out when they are used, but they must not be treated carelessly. When you give the Brief Circle, can’t you think of an example? That is why it is good to take note of your experiences: you can collect stories, and when you have to comment on something, you don’t become boring. If after ten or fifteen minutes into the talk you tell a story, your brothers will find it amusing and laugh – not obstreperously – and will learn from it. Before he finished, our Father asked for three sons of his who were soon going to other Regions: Australia, Canada and Central America. He gave them the blessing for the journey, and afterwards he gave us all the blessing, saying that he would have liked to have talked a bit more with each one.

19 March 1964

More than twenty years have passed since that day, but I still have the feast of 19 March 1964 in Rome clearly engraved on my mind. These memories have become more vivid still, thanks to the passage of time that strains out the unimportant while retaining and enhancing what is truly memorable. The afternoon before, the Rector of the Roman College had read out the list of St Joseph of the previous year. If I am not mistaken, he was interrupted by some eighty ovations; eighty acts of thanksgiving for the vocations that God had sent us during those months, through the intercession of St Joseph, our Father and Lord. Immediately, we composed the list for the next year: new names or a renewed effort to keep in mind the names repeated from the previous year. In the morning of the 19th we had a meditation and Mass in the oratory of Our Lady of Peace. Afterwards we were all eager to greet our Father. We didn’t have to wait too long, because he came down early to the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo. Some of us were able to wish him Happy Saint’s day directly. Auguri, Padre. As usual our Father had affectionate words for each and everyone. You must put on weight, he said to one Pepe. How strong you look, he said to another. In a few seconds, the sitting room was ready for a get-together. The Father talked to us at length about the feast of the day. Freedom and self-giving were the central themes. Today one of your brothers was telling us in the morning meditation that to renew is not simply to repeat something that has already been done. But I tell you more: to renew is to become young again, to become new. Os novos, the Portuguese call young people, os novos: how beautiful, to go back to being new! I am already sixty-two ... but when I say at the foot of the altar: ad Deum qui laetíficat iuventútem meam, I feel young, and I believe I will never grow old, because I will be constantly renewing myself. Be very young, all of you. Renew yourselves! Right now I renew my Fidelity, and I gain the indulgence. There are many young people who are old, because they are not able to give themselves. To renew is to become young again, to become new, to have a new ability to give oneself. When we renew our dedication out of devotion, on any day, at any hour, we are freer than ever. Each time we renew the Oblation or the Fidelity, out of devotion, in addition to gaining an indulgence, we tell our Lord: my freedom is for you. If my freedom and my honour are not for your service, why do I want them? And then we are freer than ever. I cannot understand freedom without self-surrender, nor self-surrender without freedom: one reality underlines and affirms the other. Our Father wanted to underline for us how freedom makes us young – because Love is always young

– and it is in this apparent servitude that we find our freedom. My sons, we are like a family, which grows and develops qua libertate Christus nos liberavit (Gal 4:31) – with the freedom and glory of the children of God, with the freedom that Christ has won for us. Because of our love for that freedom, we want to be well tied down. Moreover, that is the greatest expression of freedom; to say to our Lord: Put shackles of iron on my hands, tie me to yourself, because the only thing I want is to serve you and love you. Freedom, my children! But united. Think what would happen if our body did not have that apparent lack of freedom: the head would be to one side, and each one of the members on any one of the others. It is obvious that everything that has life and is capable of freedom is united. If complete freedom were to be given to the ear, to the brain or to the foot, what would happen? They would irrevocably become corrupt, they would die, because they would have lost that tie with the rest of the body, which gave them life. And if the finger 73 were to complain, for example, that it had no freedom, it would be stupid, because it would not be able to understand that if it were separated it would decay. And that is the freedom we do not want to have: the freedom to self-destruct, to die. Blessed be this dependence of ours, this yoke, which is the best sign of our freedom and of our life! We have said that we are young. To be young we need to have life, and to have life we need to be united; and that is why we cannot wish to separated from the body. At this point, our Father decided to interrupt the thread of the get-together a little, to explain why he gave us examples from ordinary life. You will say that I am very graphic… I have learned it from Jesus Christ. Have you seen how he taught in parables? And not to the illiterate, but to the Pharisees, members of the Sanhedrin, and the scribes… In the Work we do not need many geniuses. What would we do only with geniuses? But we must all be learned. And the wise among you – for there are some – only want to be learned; and as they are talented, they are unaware that they are wise. Immediately afterwards, without anybody asking any questions, he continued with the theme he had started with. Our life is all about love, about the love of God: per Christum, cum Christo et in Christo! True love, which is shown in our self-giving. That is why you have to combine freedom with love. You have to realise that licentiousness corrupts, it rots, it leads to death, and, by contrast, the freedom of Christ is love, selfsurrender, unity and Life. And so when the devil makes us feel the weight of that yoke we have taken on freely, we have to think that it is our pride which sometimes makes our self-surrender hard. And then, we have to ask for humility, in order to hear the words of the Lord: iugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve (Matt 11:30), which I like to translate freely in this way: my yoke is freedom, my yoke is love, my yoke is unity, my yoke is life, and my yoke is efficacy. Our Father’s words invited us to be responsible, to a fuller and more generous self-giving, to a renewal of our life as children of God. But he wanted us to consider some consequences of that combination of freedom and self-surrender. He began by talking to us about the common denominator. In the Work, we travel together only in what is indispensable, so that unity is not broken; but in everything else, total freedom. Love that holy freedom of the children of God! And when some do not understand it, don’t worry. When faced with calumnies, answer with the truth and a smile. There are truths which are essential, untouchable: those of the faith and morals – which are indicated by the Church – and, those of our spirit, which comes from God. In those things we all follow as one without giving in an inch. But in everything else, which is a lot, personal freedom! Having a ‘single party’ mentality in aspects of doctrine which the Church leaves to the freedom of theologians is bad. The one-party mentality in politics is very bad. In my country they say that all paths lead to Rome; at the same time, there can be a number of good parties, and several bad ones. The mentality of one pseudo-apostolic party is not Christian. Be great friends of personal freedom, which is not a theory but a reality. And afterwards, take that freedom and present it to God, with young bodies! The Father spoke forcefully. He wanted all that he was telling us to be engraved in our hearts as part of the treasure of the spirit of the Work which we should pass on to those who will come later. He was talking about the self-giving of Numeraries and Associates in the Work: Our dedication is unconditional. It is a manifestation of our freedom, against pride that seeks to defeat us. To take a graphic example, I like to explain that if a son of mine, on the brink of discovering the philosopher’s stone – repeating thereby the story of King Midas who converted everything he touched into gold – and only had to add one last drop to the potion, when he was told, “Look, you’re needed elsewhere...”, that son of mine would drop everything and, with complete freedom, go wherever he was asked to. At times I have had to do this, and in more important matters than the philosopher’s stone. Remember our parents’ pure, clean, dedicated love. We have said “No” to that human love for something better, without imposing conditions. A wife doesn’t tell her husband: “Yes, I’m yours, but on Fridays I go off with others.” Well, we can’t do this with Christ either. Our Lord wants our heart and our senses, our soul and our body, our mind and our will... Everything for God! Afterwards, he made us see how one of the first consequences of our self-surrender has to be 74 fraternity, affection and concern for our brothers. He gave us the example of the martyrs of Sebaste. I have a special devotion to the forty martyrs of Sebaste. I see in them something I like, and something else that I don’t like… so much. I like the fact they were united and asked the Lord, with that marvellous prayer: forty of us have entered into battle, we ask for forty crowns… And when that poor man weakened

– I do not think he deserted; it must have been a fit of madness, due to his sufferings – one of the torturers threw himself into the frozen water… What I don’t like is that, though they were armed, they did not defend themselves: they would have been martyrs all the same. It happens rarely, but when we lose someone along the way, I feel tremendously sorry, because in these thirty-six years I have not seen a single one who has been happy afterwards. They go after some small trinket… Afterwards, when they overcome the first frenzy, they usually react with great affection for the Work, but then it’s too late to remedy their situation. My sons, it is worthwhile helping one other to be faithful. That is why you must once again resolve to put more order into doing proselytism. In the order of charity, it is you, the people of the Work, who come first. Always remember this principle, in case it should occur to anyone to hunt lions with his imagination, while allowing those who are already “in the bag”,4 wounded by Christ’s love, to escape… And with good humour he clarified for those whose mother tongue was not Spanish: And if someone doesn’t know the meaning of that expression let him look it up in the dictionary… Love each other a lot. And when I say this, I am saying what is at the very heart of Christianity. Deus caritas est, God is love. Do you remember John, our patron, who is also a good model for learning how to love Jesus Christ? When he was very old, though he must have felt very young, when perhaps he was hardly able to speak because his body no longer responded, he would repeat to his disciples, filioli, diligite alterutrum (St Jerome, In ep, ad Galat. comm. 3, 6). My dearest children, love one another. Everything else is nonsense. Forget likes and dislikes; we act supernaturally. Love one another! Truly love one another! Already, we had been a good while in the get-together, but our Father wanted to go on and show us lavishly the fundamental aspects of the spirit of the Work for souls: for example, the sanctification of work. And, also, work, a lot of work. Professional work, whatever it may be, is, as it were, the hinge on which all the asceticism of the Work hangs. Any noble human task is sanctifiable. There is no work which we cannot convert into an instrument of sanctity, because human work is the fulfilment of a commandment of God: everything can be raised to a higher level. I do not dare to say which is more pleasing to God, the work of a professor at the Sorbonne or that of a village barber. I don’t know which is more pleasing to God. It depends on the purity of intention with which it is done, the self-giving – that is: freely saying yes to God – the spirit of sacrifice, a spirit of sacrifice which prompts one to place the last stone in each task, finishing it down to the last detail out of love. He also gave us some ideas to protect ourselves against doctrinal confusion and error. Charity always, with everyone. We cannot place error on the same level as truth, but – within the order of charity – we cannot ill-treat those who are in error. I do not understand violence. It does not seem to me to be an appropriate way either to convince or to win. Victory belongs to the faith. A soul who receives the faith is always victorious. The people who come to the Work and receive the grace of faith, how joyful, how victorious they feel, after having done away with darkness, with prejudices, even at times within the family! Ignorance is the enemy, and it is overcome with God’s grace, with prayer, with reasoning, never with argument: studying and making others study. And always with charity. Long live freedom, and down with fanaticism! We are not fanatics even of Opus Dei. The get-together was about to finish, and our Father wanted us to consider once more on the ideas that he had highlighted at the beginning: freedom and self-giving. He gave us two examples: that of a ship full of treasures in the middle of a storm, and that of a donkey’s life of work. We have all heard of those ships filled with precious cargoes which, faced with the danger of sinking,

4 Spanish “en el morral”

were saved because the crew threw overboard all the treasures, all the jewels they carried. If some day – it does not need to happen and it does not happen if you are smart and humble – but if one day, I say, you were to see opposition between freedom and vocation, it is time to follow the example of those sailors and throw overboard everything that is in the way, and tell the Lord: ecce ego, quia vocasti me (1 Sam 3:6) – here I am, because you have called me; ut iumentum! (Ps 72:23), like God’s little donkey. Nowadays, with all the motorcars around, a donkey is a sign of poverty… But I have always been attracted by the work of those animals. To me they seem to have the faces of professors! The donkey is patient, hard-working, tough and sober… Everything of his is made use of: when he dies, they can make drums from his skin, for a war of peace, or to play to the Child Jesus at Christmas. What a joy it is to be God’s little donkey! If we work like those sailors in the past, then we realise that we have greater freedom than ever, and are happier. Furthermore, we do not lose the cargo; it is recovered when the restless sea of our own passions calms down, when we have a little more of formation and when we speaks up sincerely. Ut iumentum! We will do what needs to be done, with humility and the wisdom of the donkey. Before leaving – I don’t remember if it was because of a question – our Father spoke about the work of those going to other countries. When we go to another country, instead of forming a cyst there, what is produced is the pastoral phenomenon of the pearl: a foreign body is introduced and immediately, marvellous cultivated pearls are formed. We carry with us the leaven of Christ, which makes the dough which will become bread ferment. We also put aside a portion which acts as leaven for the next batch. And so, at the end of three or four generations, the only thing that is left of that first leaven is the good taste, the bonus odor Christi (2 Cor 2:15)

* * * * * In the afternoon our Father came down again to the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo. During the gettogether, some of us told amusing stories and we sang. Our Father liked one of the songs especially. All of us sang the chorus: Aprite le finestre al nuovo sole; è primavera, è primavera. The words continued, as a gloss on what our Founder had told us in the morning, referring to our love for freedom: lasciate intrare un poco d’aria pura… The light of a clear spring sun filtered through the window panes that looked out to the street, like the words of the song, and a breath of fresh air wafted through the partly open window behind the piano. Our Father had already commented a number of times that this song seemed to describe the moment in which the soul finds God definitively, for all eternity. I want you to sing this song for me when I am dying, he repeated. Eleven years later, on 26 June 1975, when we learned that our Founder had gone to Heaven, the memory of that get-together of 19 March came alive in my soul with new strength, with the notes of a song that talks about spring and about eternal youth, which knows how to renew itself in constant selfgiving.

24 October 1966

When revising the notes taken during the get-together in the Roman College of the Holy Cross on 24th October 1966, I recall the great expectation of those dates. Besides our natural desire of wanting to spend some time with our Father, it would also be the first get-together of the academic year in which all the students of the Roman College were present. On the 12th October, our Founder returned from a long journey. He wanted to greet us immediately, stressing that we really are his ‘proximate occasion’. He came down to see us after dinner. He asked the names of those who had arrived recently and the Region they had come from. He asked the priests who had been ordained recently for their blessing as soon as they came into the sitting room. That day, our Founder told us about his trip: his medical examination at the University of Navarre Hospital; his stay in Barcelona, where some time ago he had become an honorary citizen… The gettogether passed very quickly, and we wanted more. And so it was: on Thursday, 20th October, our Father came down with don Alvaro after breakfast. Those of us who could not attend were consoled with what the others told us either in the dining room or in the get-togethers of the different groups. At the very beginning, our Founder asked them if they wanted to see the new Eucharistic columba in the Father’s oratory, and they all went to see it. He was very happy to be able to offer our Lord that gift, and he recalled – as he did, indeed, several times those days – the first monstrance they had in the residence of Ferraz: it was very poor, because they had no money to buy a better one for Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament but it was offered with as much affection as the columba. Returning to the soggiorno, he told us about his recent trip. The following day there was another short get-together, at the same time, next to the window that looks out onto the garden of the Villa. It often happened that while the Administration cleaned the area where our Father worked in Villa Vecchia, he would come down to be with us for a while. Knowing this, naturally, on the 23rd October, the day before what was then the feast of St Raphael, we went to bed convinced that the next day we would have a good get-together. Everyone was very excited, especially those who had come to the Roman College the week before. Our desires were fulfilled: on Monday 24th our Father came down immediately after breakfast, to greet the Rafas. The word went round immediately and in a few minutes the soggiorno of the Casa del Vicolo was full.

Thinking of the Roman College When we were all there, our Father gave us the good news that we had acquired a property near L’Aquila, in the Abruzzi, which would be used as the summer residence for the Roman College. Towards the end of this month we will take possession of the house of L’Aquila. It means that, instead of living like gypsies in Salto di Fondi, you will live like gypsies in this other place. We will have to fence it in and install a water supply. There used to be water, but not any more. We will have to be living there as best we can for three or four years. We will quickly repair the existing buildings to use it next year. In the meantime your architect brothers will be in charge of drawing up a plan for the project, which will be well done, and then we will begin to build. Our Father’s words made us daydream. We listened in amazement, imagining what that apostolic instrument would be in a not-too-distant future. For some time we had been looking for a summer residence for the Roman College, because the house at Salto di Fondi – which had been very useful since 1953 when the area was uninhabited – would not do now, given the rapid development of tourism. It was now necessary to find another place as soon as possible. It took a lot of searching to find the property of Tor d’Aveia. A little later, our Father again told us about another project he had been thinking about for many 77 years: the final centre of the Roman College. Around that time there had been hopes of finding something suitable, and our Founder wanted to tell us so that we would help with our prayers. One of the possibilities, which later came to nothing, was specially tempting, for it was near Villa Tevere, and had the remains of an underground catacomb dedicated to Saint Valentine, whose feast was precisely on the 14th February. If we were to acquire a house which is not very far from here– our Father said – I would hurry things up so that you could go there. It wouldn’t be comfortable, but we already have a good tradition of lack of comfort in Opus Dei, always accompanied by cheerfulness. And he added: As you are my proximate occasion, I would spend the day in the street, coming and going, to spend time in get-togethers with you. As he said these last words our Founder looked around the visitors’ room, with fatherly and motherly affection.

A discovery Together with the noble human means within our reach, our Father taught us always to put the supernatural means in the first place. That is why, after he had commented on the progress of these plans, he added: And now keep on praying, especially today, the feast of St Raphael. Our weapon is prayer, mortification, and work, which we convert into prayer. And immediately, with great simplicity, he had us share in a great discovery: that is how he chose to describe a special grace from God he had received the day before while celebrating Mass. Though sixty five years old, I have made a marvellous discovery. I love to celebrate Mass, but yesterday it cost me tremendous effort. What an effort I had to make! I saw that the Mass is truly Opus Dei, work, just like His first Mass: the Cross was work for Jesus Christ. I saw that the task of the priest, the celebration of Mass, is work, to confect the Eucharist; that one experiences pain, and joy, and tiredness. I felt in my flesh the tiredness of a divine work. I didn’t realise then what this divine caress had meant for our Father. And it is because our Founder knew how to tell us the highest experiences of his interior life with total naturalness, with a refinement and gift of tongues which were truly admirable. It was only years later, while meditating on his words time and again, that I have managed to realise better the richness of such a supernatural event. Although it was in another get-together – the one of 9th November 1966, a few weeks after this one – I feel I must record the words of our Founder on that occasion. Someone, referring precisely to what he had told us on 24th October, gave him the chance to speak about it again. Well, of course the Mass is Opus Dei! His first Mass, the Cross, also cost Christ an effort. His Most Holy Humanity resisted opening his arms on the Cross, with the gesture of an eternal Priest. I have never found it so hard to celebrate the Holy Sacrifice as that day when I felt that the Mass also is Opus Dei. It gave me great joy, but it left me exhausted. I am a doctor in Theology, and a member of the Pontifical Roman Academy of Theology… but this can only be seen when God wishes to grant it. And then, when I describe it, I do so with the shame of not having understood it before then. But it doesn’t matter: you remember Saint Paul? I delight to boast of the weaknesses that humiliate me, so that the strength of Christ may enshrine itself in me (2 Cor 12:9).

On the work of St Raphael We had kept silent after listening to our Father’s confidence. Internally, the resolutions to put more love into the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass arose easily. But the silence did not last long, because someone asked the first question of the get-together. It was an obvious question, considering the feast we were celebrating. Father, could you tell us about the first Circle of St Raphael? Our Father looked to where the voice had come from. He then told us about those beginnings: I cannot remember the exact dates; we would have to look at the documents in the archives. You may study them. What I can tell you is that the work of St Raphael started in my mother’s house, in Martinez Campos Street, Madrid. I received the boys in a room with a small picture of the Virgin Mary, which is now to be found in the Oratory of Santa Maria Stella Orientis; then, it was placed on top of a chest. Afterwards, when that became complicated, I looked for another solution. As I also carried out my priestly work with some boys from an orphanage of ragamuffins, I asked the nuns in charge of them to let me use a room, one day of the week. There, in that orphanage de Porta Coeli, is where the Circles of Saint Raphael began. Only three persons attended the first time. When we finished, we went to the nuns’ chapel to have Exposition of the Blessed Sacrament. And, as I have told you many times, when I turned to bless those boys, I did not see three, but three thousand, three hundred thousand, three million…, of all races, languages and colours. The look on our Father’s face reflected the emotion and gratitude to our Lord that those memories brought back. Memory of this inspired the Father to recommend some practical points. He said: we never missed a class of St Raphael because only a few turned up. If only one person of those invited comes, the class is given. If no one comes, we pray for them to come, and they will. But it is not enough to pray. We have to use human means, to keep active, do the work of St Raphael that is required, and work joyfully. Work is our way of serving God; it sanctifies us and allows us to sanctify others. And to make it clear that all of us have to contribute, and that no one is excused from doing apostolate, he added: Each one of us is part of a body. If you try to be in good health, if you behave well and give good example, as well as being a sheep, you are a shepherd, a good shepherd. Why does one have to be a priest to show concern for the souls of others? The Work is eminently lay. And when he looked around and saw a good number of priests, he commented with good humour: In the Work, we priests do not reach even two per cent of the total number of members. That is a fact, even though you see many cassocks here. That’s natural because this is an International Centre. But elsewhere it doesn’t happen. If in some place there are more priests, it is because they are just beginning. In places where the work is just beginning, there are normally more priests proportionately. I do not like only one to go, or two. If possible we send three.

Like the water in a canal Our Father stopped talking. The following question still followed the historical direction the gettogether had taken. Father, how did you explain the Work to the first people? Hilary’s question brought to our Father’s mind memories of the beginnings of Opus Dei. My mind turns to so many brothers of ours who have worked to establish Opus Dei in new countries and regions: How often, almost without realising, they have behaved in the way our Father was explaining now! I started by not speaking to them about the Work: I got them to work. I saw that this was the way the Lord behaved. Jesus Christ, when he called the Apostles, did not tell them at the beginning what he wanted to do. That is what I did with the first ones. And some will not even be able to tell you when they did the Admission. First comes life; then, the norm. I did not shut myself in a corner to think a priori how I should dress Opus Dei. When the child was born, then we dressed it; like Jesus Christ who coepit facere et docere (Acts 1:1), first He acted, and then he taught. First we had the water, and then we mapped out the canal. Not for one moment did I think of opening up a canal without counting on the water first. Life in Opus Dei has always come before the juridical form. That is why the juridical form has to be like a suit made to measure; and if it were not like that, it would be because it had been forced on us, changing the measurements or cutting it from someone else’s pattern. He then referred to the juridical solution of the Work, which by then could be seen clearly on the juridical horizon of the Church, thanks to the norms for the application of Vatican Council II, and specifically to the Motu proprio Ecclesiae Sanctae, promulgated by Pope Paul VI on 6th August 1966. I must tell you that, for the moment, the juridical way is already resolved. But for now we are not 79 interested in putting on the suit. It is better to wait a little, and keep on praying as if nothing had happened. When the right time comes, we will put on the suit, the trousers and the jacket. He again stressed the need to continue praying for that intention and not to stop. But, I insist: we have to continue praying as if the solution had not been found. You have opened up the way yourselves, with your prayers and with your life; and the Council has confirmed it through the selfless, unflagging work of some of your brothers. There is always someone praying for the Work, night and day; because we are in Europe, Asia, America, Oceania and Africa; and this has great strength before God. During those moments we experienced in a special way the Communion of Saints we live in the Work. Those of us who were there, close to our Father, represented our brothers on all continents. In spite of the physical distance, we could feel them especially near. Later, our Founder referred to the work of so many persons – and don Alvaro in the first place – who had made possible that juridical solution which fits the nature of Opus Dei so well. But, as in the Work there is no room for idiots, besides praying and offering many sufferings, we have tried to work juridically as much as possible. I communicated the solution we desired to the Holy Father John XXIII and to the present Pope, Paul VI. Then, the principles were taken up by the Vatican Council, and the Pope confirmed them and applied them with a Motu Proprio. As soon as the document appeared, the Secretary of the Council sent it to Don Alvaro, with congratulations. Anyone who has eyes can see that this is a suit made to measure for Opus Dei.

Other memories Our Father took up again the thread of what he had been saying and took us back once again remembering those first years of life in the Work. I did not say anything to my mother or my sister. I was always surrounded by eighteen or twenty yearold boys. Sometimes they asked me: Why don’t you prepare for an examination to become a professor? One day a bishop, a relative of ours, came to our home, and said to my mother: Lola, why doesn’t your son come to see me? I’ve got a position for him as a canon… In the end I had no choice but to explain to my mother and my sister what I was doing: the Work. I needed their help to set up the first residence. I hadn’t realised that my brother Santiago was listening. When I had finished, he said: ‘Ah! So that’s why you were ‘cilicing’ yourself!’ And it was because he had found a cilice stuffed into a pair of shoes. And he had run to my mother: Mama!, Mama! Look at what I have fished out from Josemaría! My mother told him to leave it where he had found it, and explained what it was. And, so as soon as he could, he came out with that peculiar word he had invented. We roared with laughter at the word Uncle Santiago had made up. Later our Father told us that a little while back he had had the great pleasure of giving First Holy Communion to Santiago’s two eldest children. I don’t remember how something related to Bishop Eijo y Garay came up: he was the Bishop of Madrid who loved our Father and the Work very much. He had already died by then. What our Father told us was a clear sign of the deep affection and veneration that Don Leopoldo had for the Work and our Father. In a newspaper I saw a photograph of the dead body of Don Leopoldo, the Bishop of Madrid, in which he appeared wearing a ring I had given him. They told me: It is because the Lord Bishop had said that he wanted to die with it. I learnt that the chaplain had kept it, and I asked for it. They have now returned it because somebody dealing with the will had heard that the ring had to be returned to Opus Dei when he died. Don Pedro Casciaro had designed it. I gave it to the bishop in 1940. I don’t know what the occasion was, because he did not have an Episcopal ring: he had been left with nothing during the war. Someone’s question allowed our Founder to explain the origin of the Custom of invoking the Holy Archangels Michael, Gabriel and Raphael as Patrons of the Work. During those first years, as I was always surrounded by young men, I did not have much freedom. That is why, when I wanted to make a spiritual retreat, I would go somewhere where I could be alone. I asked that no one disturb me, and I spent those days in prayer, apart from everybody. I went to different places. On several occasions I was in the friary that the Discalced Carmelites have in Segovia, built by St John of the Cross, who was a great architect. It rises on top of a rock, next to the river, and there is no damp, because the Saint knew how to get it right. On the other hand, in the chapel that was built later to venerate his remains, there is a lot of damp. It was there, in that chapel, that I began to invoke the three Archangels as Patrons of the Work. I spent hours praying, and I got a heavy cold. It was in 1932. There are notes written about all this. I do not like writing a diary, but I took notes. I remember an old monk of that friary who I went to confession to during the retreat. He had an unusual name, but was an extraordinary, holy man. He was called Narciso de San José. And that holy friar, filled with filial pride, told me, that he had kissed the head of his Holy Mother, Teresa of Avila. Our Father referred with great affection to St Teresa, for whom he had a great devotion. He recalled a well known episode of her life, which shows the feminine character of the saint from Avila. When Fray Juan de la Miseria, one of the first reformed Carmelites, wanted to make a portrait of her, the Saint did not like it: ‘May God forgive you, Fray Juan, for you have painted me as old and bleary eyed’, she protested. Well, on the occasion of one anniversary of the Order, they opened the casket with the remains of the Saint. And my good friar, already elderly, told me: I have kissed the forehead of my Mother St Teresa. And he was moved. Well, that’s beautiful. Our Father still spoke about a few other things, but the get-together soon ended. A wonderful gettogether, just like all the others I was lucky enough to attend, close to our Father! We continued celebrating the feast of St Raphael for the rest of the day: Solemn Exposition of the Blessed Sacrament in the oratory of Our Lady of Peace, get-togethers to celebrate the Rafas… Now, when going through the notes I took down that day, my heart is seized by the same emotions I felt then. Because our Father, by connecting the divine and the human, always knew how to enkindle our love for God and for our Beautiful Mother the Work.

9 July 1967

I will always remember with special affection and a little nostalgia the get-togethers with our Father in the Arco dei Venti, in which I often had the pleasure of being present during my stay in the Roman College. When the good weather arrived and the days were longer, from the soggiorno of the fifth floor of Uffici – which for the greater part of the day acted as a lecture room – we could see our Founder walking back and forth in the cortile of the Villa, together with don Alvaro and don Javier, while saying a part of the Rosary. When they had finished, we were often invited to have a get-together. The bell of Uffici would ring – a bell many will recall with joy, because it told us that the Father was waiting for us – and in a jiffy we were all surrounding him. Although they lasted a very short time – at least for those who had dinner at half past seven – these get-togethers were special, like a sweet we didn’t expect, although, to be more precise, they were very frequent during the sixties. The usual thing was that our Father would sit on the stone bench that runs along the wall of Villa Vecchia, at the corner with the Arco dei Venti. We closed the circle with some wooden benches that we brought from other places in the garden. But it was not always like that. On other occasions, especially if we were few, the get-together was peripatetic: our Father would walk from one end of the façade of the Villa to the other, while his sons surrounded him completely, opening and closing passageways through which our Founder’s calm measured steps could pass. The time I want to write about is the evening of 9th July 1967, which fits perfectly in the above framework. Thanks to the diary of the Roman College and to an article in our internal publications, my memories of that get-together are quite precise. The academic year had finished. A group of students from the Roman College would be going back to their Regions next day, after their years of formation in Rome. It was already very warm, and the hours of daylight were long. Our Father, who considered the presence of his sons as a proximate occasion, allowed himself to be overcome by the temptation and came down to the Arco dei Venti after dinner to be a while with us. The atmosphere, as I have said, was warm; and not only from a meteorological point of view. The emotion of those who were about to leave Rome was noticeable. Our Father was also moved. And the same applied to those of us who would spend a few more months close to our Father. Therefore, it was not surprising that this get-together was especially emotional. At about eight o’clock two guitars appeared. They passed from hand to hand until they reached an expert, who, after plucking the strings to tune the instrument, started to sing:

They say that one does not feel sad saying goodbye… Tell the one who says that to you to bid goodbye!

One does feel it, was the comment our Father made in a low voice.

To love the spirit of the Work The first question in the get-together was about the influence the Work had on spirituality. When the time comes, it will be very interesting to study the influence of Opus Dei on spirituality. There are already some brothers of yours working in that field, apart from the things that you have already, written by me from as long ago as 1928. It is self-evident, to whoever has eyes in his head, that the life of the Work has influenced and still influences Christian spirituality and theology; it is as evident as the fact that you 82 and I wear glasses. But we do not like to give ourselves importance. All that work will be done, little by little, as time goes on. And could it not happen that as the years go by the spirit of Opus Dei could lose its strength? another asked. No, my son, not if you are faithful. We have all the grace from God and, also, we have taken all possible precautions, both spiritual and juridical, so it may never happen. I hope that God’s goodness will make all my sons very faithful. Whoever does not want to be faithful will go away and leave us in peace. Try to fulfil the norms well, trying to be constantly in the presence of God and to fulfil the duty of each moment. Don’t complicate your lives with foolish things: in this way you will serve the Work well. While you are here also, you have the special duty to study our spirit very well, and try to acquire all the theoretical knowledge possible and put it into practice. We do not seek merely theoretical knowledge: our formation necessarily has to give rise to deeds. Our Father encouraged us, especially, to take great care of our prayer, our relationship with God. Whether our prayer requires an effort or not does not depend on us. At times we can be very ardent, but on other occasions we will find things difficult. When you feel arid and cold, to the point of seeming incapable of even saying a Hail Mary, tell our Lord: My God, I want to love. And you are already with God and doing prayer. Which prayer is best: the one that comes without difficulty, like water from a fountain, or the one which is done by dint of a lot of personal effort? Either of the two. Where I come from they say – and though the comparison may sound irreverent, it isn’t – that there are many ways of killing fleas. There are many ways of reaching out to God, and in the Work we are taught how to do this. The important thing is to fight to be contemplatives in the midst of all the ordinary things we do: contemplative life, which is the spiritual foundation of the children of God in Opus Dei.

Opening up the way Songs alternated with questions and the answers our Father wished to engrave once again in the hearts of those sons of his who were leaving and of all the others. We all listened attentively, in a silence that was interrupted only by the screaming of the swifts in the sky overhead. He talked to us about the juridical path of the Work, which in those years was still being formulated, based on the prayers and the sacrifice of all, and by the intense, hidden work of our Founder, helped by don Alvaro. We are still constructing our way. The light is lit, but every day we have to give more strength and vigour to that light. The focus is the same as at the beginning, but its intensity increases with the strength of your virtues and with your numbers. Perhaps I will go without seeing it finished, but the Lord allows me to contemplate what perhaps he does not permit others to see. Rarely is a person who has begun a work – I did not want it: it never crossed my mind to found anything – permitted to see so much fruit in his lifetime. Later on, going back over the history of the juridical way, our Father told us – as he had done so many other times – that the Work was interested above all in the virtues of his sons. ‘And how can we define the Work in a non-juridical way?’ someone else asked. It is to forget oneself and, for the love of God, to be concerned for others. To dedicate to the Lord our whole life, personally and freely: all the activities, the spiritual ones as well as the material ones. You understand it well, although you might not know how to say it. In the next edition of the Catechism you will have it written down: how to explain, juridically and non-juridically, what the Work is. In the meantime, my son, have a little patience. In the Catechism of the Work all this will be dealt with very well and you will be able to teach the others. You will have to learn it by heart. He explained to us the reasons that had made a new edition of the Catechism of the Work advisable, and the precautions he had taken so that the genuine meaning of the norms in which the spirit of Opus Dei is expressed will never be changed. All living languages are constantly changing. The spiritual doctrine is expressed in oral or written language. And since at times words change their meaning as time goes by, we have to be alert so that the content will not change. That is why from time to time we renew our way of saying things. We change nothing of what we have received from God and we do not want those who will come later to understand it wrongly because of our mistakes. I would like to retain as far as possible the expressions we have always used. That is why the meaning of what has not changed has been left as it was. So, there will be no new things in the next edition of the Catechism; it will be the same as always. It is only the form that is new, so as to adjust to the variations of living languages. This difficulty does not exist in Latin, which is there, like a mummy, without changing. It can also be changed, but it is more difficult. They will change it, especially, if ignorant jurists come along, who not only give a different meaning, but also one contrary to the expressions in the Latin language. That is why many of the documents of the Work are written in Latin, so that it will be more difficult to be understood in a way different to what was originally intended.

Divine Graces From time to time the Father made some remarks which helped to create a more relaxed atmosphere, while encouraging us all. When I was young, I used to tell your first brothers: When I die, bury me standing up with an inscription saying: ut eatis. You must go throughout the world. But now I say that you should bury me like everyone else, which is the normal thing to do. And you will put an inscription saying: Here lie the remains of a sinner who loved Jesus Christ. Pray for him. I do not know if it was because of that remark that someone asked: Father, you told us that one day you thought you were going to die... It was not I who thought it; it was the doctors who told me. I do not wish to die, but to live and work for God. It is too comfortable to die. The one who wishes to die does not have the good spirit of Opus Dei. I was told by the doctors that I could die at any moment when I was ill with diabetes, and in fact one day it nearly happened. Our Father, who considered himself a sinner who loves Jesus Christ, also knew he had been given countless graces. He talked to us about one of those divine caresses; specifically what had happened in the Mass of the 14 February 1943. You know that I am a poor man and you will not be so careless as to tell these things that I am saying, at least while I am alive. But I would be lying if I did not recognise the marvels that God has carried out in the life of the Work. The Lord, who wished to make use of Balaam’s donkey, has also made use of this Father of yours as an instrument. On 14 February 1943, I began the Mass seeking the juridical solution to be able to incardinate priests in the Work. For a long time I had been trying to find it, without any success. And that day, intra missam, after the Communion, the Lord wished to give it to me: the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross. He even gave me the seal: the orb of the world with a cross inscribed. It was in Madrid, in a house your sisters had in Jorge Manrique Street. When I finished the Mass, I took a little piece of paper and made a sketch which I gave the following day to Don Alvaro. I asked Don Pedro Casciaro to draw it properly, but I did not like what he did; it was not how I wanted it. Then it was drawn by Ricardo Fernandez Vallespin, and we agreed that the long arm of the cross was one fifth in the upper part and four fifths in the lower part. That was an external sign that the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross, of which as yet I had no notion, had come into being.

A marvellous family We had been a long time in the get-together. Antonio, who was one of those leaving the following day, sang again a song that had been popular that year:

… sei tanto giovane … you are so young e tutto il mondo ormai and the entire world sta sorridendoti… is now smiling at you…

Our Father followed the lyrics of the song. What time is it? He asked. Quarter to nine, Father. How nice it is to be here! How good the Lord is, allowing these sons of mine who are going away, to be with us for a while. And looking at don Alvaro: I am going to spend a little while longer with these lads. I have seen very little of you this year. What with the journey and other things, I have come down very seldom. That is why I have escaped to be with you now. Our Father had been away from 19th April to 24th May, travelling through France, Spain and Portugal, spending time with quite a number of his sons. In Rome we had been following him with a lot of prayer, and through news that reached us from place to place. With regard to the new site of the Roman College – what today is Cavabianca – which was being earnestly sought during those years, our Father told us: I would spend the day in the street, coming and going, if they let me, to be a while with you. You are my proximate occasion, but I am not necessary. There are lots of brothers of yours around, who are very holy, and can give you wonderful formation. No one is indispensable in the Work, and yet, God wants to need us all. I always say the same thing because it is true. He made us consider that the Work is a true family. The person who governs the Work will always be called Father, because we are a family, Love him a lot, whoever he is. Those who come later – he continued – have to be loved more than me; be united to them, love them humanly and supernaturally, obey them, consummati in unum. Normally, in many institutions when the Founder disappears a sort of earthquake happens. I am not worried on that score: in Opus Dei it will not happen. Kiss the feet of the one who will come later, love him and pray for him to be very cheerful and very saintly, because he will certainly be learned. And he insisted: Not to call him Father, which might seem a small detail, would be a grave error: a sign of bad spirit. On the other hand, when speaking with people from outside, one says don this or don that, whatever his name is.

A beautiful love It was not hot: there was a pleasant breeze. We had left our jackets on the stone benches or on the side of the lawn. Franco lit one of the lanterns on the wall of the Villa. This time it was Giancarlo’s turn to sing. He was just passing through Rome:

Uno: non tradirli mai, han fede in te. One: Don’t ever betray them, they have faith in you Due: non li deludere, credono in te. Two: Don’t deceive them, they believe in you Tre: non farli piangere, vivono in te Three: Don’t make them cry, they live in you Four: Don’t abandon them, they will miss you Quattro: non li abbandonare, ti mancheranno

It was a song that had just been sung for the first time in a song festival,5 which was something like

5 Quando avrai le mani stanche tutto lascerai, per le cose belle ti ringrazieranno, soffriranno per gli errori tuoi. E tu ragazzo non lo sai, ma nei tuoi occhi c’è già lei, ti chiederà l’amore, ma l’amore ha i suoi comandamenti. Uno: non tradirla mai, ha fede in te. Due: non la deludere, lei crede in te.

the Ten Commandments for people in love. Our Father made use of those phrases, applying them to our vocation. He told us in Italian: You have heard it. Never betray Love, do not abandon it. Afterwards one cries, and for one’s whole life. We have to be faithful! He recommended us to have a great love for the Most Blessed Virgin, Mater Pulchrae Dilectionis: It is pure love that does not feel ashamed, a clean love. All the noble loves of the earth can be found there: love for God, for one’s parents, for one’s spouse, for brothers and sisters… All the clean things of the human heart have a place in the Heart of the Virgin Mary. Can one think that one’s vocation to the Work is the best? I don’t remember who asked the question, but I do remember our Father’s immediate reply: One should, one should! If not, it would be as dangerous as thinking one’s wife was worse than others. The one who thinks that is already preparing to commit adultery. To believe that one’s vocation is the most beautiful pleases God very much. It is natural to behave like that. It is a sign of one’s love for one’s vocation. The owner of a horse thinks it is wonderful: the best breed, the strongest, the one that jumps best over the obstacles, the most beautiful… He interrupted himself for a moment, with a gesture of complicity: … and, furthermore, in our case we are right.

Last advice By now it was completely dark; there were no swifts in the mirror of the sky over the garden. There were no clouds and we could see the stars coming out. Our Father had talked about many things, nearly always in answer to some question of ours: about the apostolic expansion throughout the world, about the annual courses – we were soon to go for the first time to Tor d’Aveia – and about the need to rest, about praying Psalm II, about mutual trust, the mission of priests… The get-together was coming to an end. The questions asked by one person or another followed in quick succession, on various topics, as if we wanted to stop the passage of time, as if this were the last opportunity to treasure these golden coins the Father himself was giving us. For some that was certainly the case. The Father’s answers were concise but substantial. How can we live the Holy Mass better? The Sacrifice of the Altar is the same as that of Calvary. Do not forget, when you are older, that the Father is ashamed of having taken so many years to discover that the Mass is the work of God, painful, hard: all the work of carrying the Cross on His shoulders, of feeling the buffeting and the spitting, of giving Himself in an act of supreme love: take and eat! How can we have more a more intimate relationship with the Blessed Trinity? Love our Lady and meditate on the Passion of our Lord. You will hear Him invoke the Father at the moment of his great miracles and when the Passion is approaching. And He will say: Abba, Pater! as St Paul teaches. In this way, even though you may not think so, you are drawing close to the Most Blessed Trinity. As if the Most Holy Humanity of Christ were left to one side, but it is not true. Is Our Lady the best way to reach God, or she the only way? I would say that she is the only one and the best, both things; to go to God and to come back to Him when we have lost our way. Our Father was now about to leave, when Franco announced he had a serenade ready. Let’s have it. If you have prepared it I’m ready to listen.

Tre: non farla piangere, vive per te. Quattro: non l’abbandonare, ti mancherà. E la sera cercherà fra le braccia tue tutte le promesse, tutte le speranze, per un mondo d’amore.

Some started the song, and we all joined in. We knew the chorus:

Es la hora de la ronda It is time for the serenade

El amor pasa; Love is passing by;

Hay que romper a cantar We have to break into song

Porque con hablar no basta… Because to speak is not enough…

Between the façade of the Villa, the Arco dei Venti and the edge of the lawn, we formed four or five concentric circles, moving while following the rhythm of the song. There were a hundred voices, perhaps not very united, but all with the same emotions and desires. Many of us had a lump in our throat. Half jokingly, Rafa told Fernando, as if summarisng everything: You will remember this moment always, won’t you? Let it be well imprinted in your mind. With the last notes of the song, our Father finished the get-together. Tomorrow a few of you will be leaving, isn’t that so? At the threshold of the door of the Villa, before entering, he gave the blessing to those who were leaving. Some knelt down. Others were standing in a row. Afterwards he gave a kiss to each one, and said a few words: You know that we don’t make supermen here. You are like all the others, and you are going to serve the others: place your heart on the ground so that others may tread softly. Our Father entered the Villa and the shadows of the garden were filled, little by little with the murmur of voices. We slowly entered Uffici and the garden was left in silence.

22 & 23

7 April 1968

In the morning, a solemn liturgical function in the Oratory of Santa Maria, with Benediction and the procession of palms. In the afternoon, a get-together with the Father. One of the longest of the whole year. That is how the notes of 7th April 1968 begin in the diary of the Roman College, where there is a record of the get-together we had with our Father on Palm Sunday. It went on for a long time, with many questions and answers. Our Father entered the soggiorno greeting Ermanno and Fabio, who celebrated their Saint’s Day that day, and everyone. In a few words, he invited us to fight to be truly saints, even though we might see ourselves very far from that goal, and asked us to pray so that the Father would also be good and faithful. We sat down around our Founder and the get-together started. Continuing with the topic he had just started, he taught us that we should take other people to our Lord, above all, with the example of our life. We cannot, he said, be like signposts, which show the way to a city but do not go there themselves. We must show our friends the way that takes us to God, but lead the way with our own struggle and effort, properly based on the Lord. I have to go to our Lord, and be with our Lord. And so must you, you rascal! Referring to roads, he talked about his forthcoming journey to Pamplona, to preside at some meetings of the Patrons of the University of Navarre. I have to go to Pamplona because I have appointed three bishops as members of the Board of Patrons of the Ecclesiastical Faculties of the University of Navarre. We have done so in agreement with the Holy See, because the University of Navarre is, juridically, a university of the Church: there was no other way of obtaining civil recognition for its degrees. On the other hand, the one already established in Mexico and the one we are preparing in Peru will not be Catholic universities, but enterprises carried out by ordinary citizens, inspired by a Christian spirit. They will be Catholic because they are promoted by Catholics, but without bearing that adjective in their name.
